Torsion pairs and 3-fold flops
Parth Shimpi

Abstract
This paper classifies t-structures on the local derived category of a 3-fold flopping contraction, that are intermediate with respect to the heart of perverse coherent sheaves. Equivalently, this describes the complete lattice of torsion classes for the associated modification algebra. The intermediate hearts are (1) categories of coherent sheaves on birational models and tilts thereof in skyscrapers, (2) algebraic t-structures described in the homological minimal model programme, or (3) combinations of the above over appropriate open covers. An analogous classification is also proved for minimal (and partial) resolutions of Kleinian singularities, thus providing a description of all torsion pairs in the module categories of (contracted) affine preprojective algebras. The results have immediate applications to the classification of spherical modules and (semi)bricks, and are first steps towards describing all t-structures and spherical objects in derived categories of surfaces and 3-folds.

\keypart
\keypart
\keypart
\keypart

It is a truth universally acknowledged, that a mathematician in possession of a
triangulated category, must be in want of tools to understand its
autoequivalences. This truth guides the homological algebraist’s pursuit of
t-structures, the representation theorist’s pursuit of torsion pairs, and the
geometer’s of spherical objects.

Such is also the predicament we find ourselves in;
this paper studies the derived category of a Gorenstein terminal
3-fold
X
appearing in a flopping contraction
\pi\colon X\to Z=\Spec(R,\mathfrak{m})
over a complete local base. The assumptions on the
singularities of
X
are equivalent to requiring that
R
is an isolated
compound Du Val (cDV) singularity. The bounded derived category of coherent
sheaves
\Db X
, as well as the full subcategory supported on the exceptional fiber

\Dm X=\left\{x\in\Db X\;\middle|\;\mathop{\text{Supp}}x\subseteq\pi^{-1}[\mathfrak{m}]\right\}

has been of interest to birational and symplectic geometers alike
[
Asp03
,
Tod08
,
HW18
,
HW23
,
KS24
]
.
The same can be said of the analogous situation in dimension
2
, where
Z

is a canonical surface singularity and
X
a (partial) resolution

[
Cra00
,
IU05
,
Bri09
,
IUU10
,
BDL23
]
.
In either setting, an understanding of autoequivalences, t-structures, and
spherical objects is desirable.

When
X
is smooth, the autoequivalences of the derived category are sometimes
controlled by
spherical objects
. In general they are not,
so Hara–Wemyss
[
HW24
]
argue for the study of
broader collections of objects which behave like the simples of an Abelian
category, namely
(semi)bricks
. The rich interplay between semibricks
and t-structures has been a staple tool for representation theorists

[
MS17
,
Asa20
]
, and Hara–Wemyss use
this to study the null subcategory

\mathcal{C}=\ker(\mathbf{R}\pi_{\ast})\subseteq\Db X
which is known to be the
‘finite-type’ counterpart to
\Dm X
, the latter exhibiting ‘affine’ behaviour

[
Bri09
,
HW23
]
.

Hara–Wemyss show that a global classification of hearts (of t-structures) and bricks in

\mathcal{C}
is indeed possible. The classification of spherical objects
(when they exist) comes as a corollary. Up to well-understood mutation
equivalences, the only hearts are the finitely subcategories

H_{1},...,H_{n}\subset\mathcal{C}
given by the homological minimal model
programme
[
Wem18
]
, and each is described as
the module category of some finite dimensional algebra

\Lambda_{i,\text{con}}

(i=1,...,n)
. Further every brick in

\mathcal{C}
is the track of some simple
\Lambda_{i,\text{con}}
-module.

Hearts on the ‘affine’ category

The category
\Dm X
, too, has algebraic hearts. Van den Bergh

[
Van04
]
observed that there is
a module-finite
R
-algebra
\Lambda
and a derived equivalence

\textnormal{VdB}\colon\Db X\to\Db\Lambda
that identifies
\mathcal{f\kern-0.5ptl\kern-1.0ptm\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}\Lambda
with the
full subcategory of
perverse sheaves

\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}\subset\Dm X
. This
serves as our reference heart. The homological minimal model program extends
this to a family of
R
-algebras
\{{}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\nu}\;|\;\nu\text{ a
sequence of mutations}\}
called
modification algebras
that are derived
equivalent to
\Lambda
(see
3.2
). Tracking the natural
hearts
\mathcal{f\kern-0.5ptl\kern-1.0ptm\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}{}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\nu}
across these equivalences then produces
more algebraic t-structures on
\Dm X
which we say are
mutations of

\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
.

However it is evident that there can be no autoequivalence which identifies
the above algebraic t-structures with the geometric (and
non-Artinian) heart
\coh X
. Other geometric hearts can be constructed
by iteratively flopping exceptional curves in
X
to obtain new flopping
contractions
\pi\colon W\to Z
, we say the 3-fold
W
thus obtained is a

birational model
of
X
. Indeed flops give derived equivalences

[
Bri02
,
Che02
]
, so
tracking
\coh W
across the Bridgeland–Chen functor
\Psi\colon\Db W\to\Db X
gives a geometric t-structure on
\Dm X
.

To complicate matters further, there are hearts which are ‘algebro-geometric’ in quite
the literal sense. The construction of the perverse heart is local, so any
crepant morphism
\tau\colon X\to Y
contracting some (but not all) of the

\pi
-exceptional curves gives a sheaf

{V}

of coherent

{O}_{Y}
-algebras and a derived equivalence
\Db X\to\Db\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{${V}$}
. This
identifies a category of suitable

{V}

-modules with a heart

\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}\subset\Dm X
whose objects are precisely complexes that look
like finite length
\Lambda
-modules on the
\tau
-exceptional locus and
like
{O}_{X}
-modules elsewhere (
theorem

3.16
). The
category is again not equivalent to any heart examined previously, and it is
hence apparent that any classification must also account for these (semi-)geometric
hearts.

Classification of intermediate hearts

The main result of this paper shows that, at least for sufficiently small
cohomological spread, the above possibilities are in fact exhaustive.

This involves building semi-geometric
hearts locally using algebraic and geometric categories for ‘smaller’ flopping
contractions (
3.4
), thus obtaining an inductive
description of t-structures. Indeed any partial contraction
\tau:X\to Y
is
an isomorphism away from the open locus
X^{\circ}
of its non-exceptional
fibers, while in any sufficiently small neighbourhood of a
\tau
-exceptional
fiber
C_{I}
, the map
\tau
restricts to a flopping contraction
X_{I}\to Z_{I}
with complete local base
Z_{I}
in
Y
. This gives a (flat) cover of

X
. We show any intermediate t-structure (i.e. a t-structure whose heart is
concentrated in cohomological degrees
0
and
-1
) decomposes into purely
algebraic or purely geometric hearts with respect to some such cover.

X^{\circ}
\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}X_{1}
\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}X_{456}
X^{\circ}
\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}Z_{1}
\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}Z_{456}
\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}1
\color[rgb]{0,1,1}2
\color[rgb]{0,1,1}3
\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}4
\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}5
\color[rgb]{0.01,1,0.48}6
\tau

Theorem A

((=
C
,
4.17
))
.

Let
K\subset\Dm X
be the heart of a t-structure that is
contained in
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}[-1,0]
. Then there is a birational model
W
of

X
, and a partial contraction
\tau\colon W\to Y
, such that
K
satisfies
the following after being pulled back across the flop
equivalence
\Psi\colon\Dm W\to\Dm X
.

1.

On the locus
W^{\circ}\subset W
where
\tau
is an
isomorphism,
\Psi^{-1}K
restricts to the category of coherent
sheaves
\coh W^{\circ}
, possibly tilted in skyscraper sheaves

\langle{O}_{p}\;|\;p\in Q\rangle
for some subset of closed
points
Q\subset W^{\circ}
.

2.

For each
\tau
-exceptional fiber
C_{I}
in
W
,
for sufficiently small neighbourhoods
W_{I}\supset C_{I}
and

Z_{I}\ni\tau(C_{I})
, the restriction of
\Psi^{-1}K

to a
W_{I}
is an (algebraic) mutation of the
category
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W_{I}}{Z_{I}}\right)$}}\subset\Dm W_{I}
.

Moreover, the above data uniquely determines
K
.

In particular if
\tau
above contracts all exceptional curves, then
K
is an
algebraic mutation of
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Z}\right)$}}
(hence also of
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
) and thus

K
is the category of finite length modules over a modification algebra. On
the other extreme if
\tau
contracts no curves then
K
is a geometric
t-structure on the birational model
W
.

Classification of bricks

As a corollary we get a succinct description of bricks in
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
, i.e.
\pi
-perverse sheaves whose endomorphism algebra is one-dimensional. This
extends to modification algebras a result of Crawley-Boevey
[
Cra00
, lemma
1]
, who showed that the dimension
vector of any brick-module over an affine preprojective algebra is a root.

Theorem B

((=
5.25
))
.

Let
b\in\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
be a brick. Then
b
is either

1.

a simple module over some modification algebra

{}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\nu}
, or

2.

a skyscraper sheaf on some birational model
W
of
X
,

tracked under appropriate equivalences induced by mutations or flops. In
particular, the class of
b
in the Grothendieck group is a primitive restricted
root of the (affine) Dynkin data associated to
X
.

In sufficiently restricted settings (e.g. under the assumptions imposed in

[
Tod08
]
), every algebraic mutation of

\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
is equivalent to the category of perverse sheaves on some
birational model
W
. Then the above result says that, up to applying mutation
functors, every brick in
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
is either a point sheaf
{O}_{p}
, or
the twisted structure sheaf
{O}_{C_{i}}(-1)
on some integral curve, or the
suspended canonical sheaf
\omega_{\underline{C}}[1]
of the scheme-theoretic
exceptional fiber on some birational model
W
. In general however there are
modifying algebras not related to birational geometry in any obvious way
(these are the ‘hidden’ t-structures alluded to
in
[
HW23
]
), and expressing their simple
modules in geometric terms is difficult. For a single flopping curve this is
accomplished by Donovan–Wemyss
[
DW24
, §4]
who
show that each such simple is, up to mutation, determined by the structure sheaf
or the canonical sheaf of some thickening of the exceptional curve.

Canonical surfaces and preprojective algebras

Suppose
\overline{X}
is a Gorenstein canonical surface that admits a crepant birational
morphism
\pi\colon\overline{X}\to\overline{Z}=\Spec(\overline{R},\mathfrak{p})
with complete
local base. The results of this paper, though developed in the context of
3-folds, apply verbatim to the derived category of
\overline{X}
and its

\pi
-perverse heart provided one suitably reinterprets the notions of
modification algebras and birational models.

Such a map
\pi
is necessarily a crepant (partial) resolution of the Kleinian
singularity
\overline{Z}
, i.e.
\overline{X}
is obtained by contracting some
exceptional curves in the minimal resolution
\widetilde{X}\to\overline{Z}
. It is
well-known that
\widetilde{X}
is derived equivalent to an affine preprojective
algebra
\Pi
; the contraction
\widetilde{X}\to\overline{X}
determines an idempotent

e\in\Pi
such that
\overline{X}
is derived equivalent to the
contracted
preprojective algebra

e\Pi e

[
KIWY15
]
.
This equivalence
\Dfl(e\Pi e)\to\Dm(\overline{X})
is also recovered by
Van den Bergh’s construction

[
Van04
]
, and algebraic
mutations of the category of
\pi
-perverse sheaves can be read off from the
tilting theory of
e\Pi e

[
IW
, §7.4]
.

The discussion of birational models is only slightly more subtle, as curves in
surfaces do not flop. Birational transformations (including flops in dimension
3), on the other hand, are naturally induced via geometric invariant theory.
Indeed
\widetilde{X}
appears as a moduli space
\mathcal{M}(\theta,\delta)
of
stable
\Pi
-modules, for some generic stability parameter
\theta

relative to a
\KK
-theory class
\delta

[
CS98
]
. Variation of GIT parameter

\theta_{1}\rightsquigarrow\theta_{2}
produces a birational map

\mathcal{M}(\theta_{1},\delta)\dashrightarrow\mathcal{M}(\theta_{2},\delta)
of

\overline{Z}
-schemes. In particular if
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
lie in
adjacent chambers then this birational map is defined away from a single
exceptional curve, and simple wall–crossing of the GIT parameter thus gives an
analogue of flops in dimension 2. The role of Bridgeland–Chen equivalences is
played by
reflection functors

[
SY13
]
, and the results are extended to the
contracted setting by Iyama–Wemyss
[
IW
]
.

With this, all arguments in
4
and
5
regarding the
partial order of algebraic intermediate hearts and its interactions with convex
geometry and line bundles on birational models hold verbatim. Thus the reader
indifferent to dimensions can safely read the remainder of this paper as if it
were written for surfaces.

To the reader interested in multiple dimensions, we remark that the
correspondence between our results for 3-folds and surfaces is natural. Indeed,
the morphism
\overline{X}\to\overline{Z}
can be embedded as a generic hyperplane section
(
general elephant
) of some 3-fold flopping contraction
X\to\Spec(R,\mathfrak{m})
, and the corresponding non-commutative algebras are
related by the reduction
e\Pi e=\Lambda\otimes_{R}\overline{R}
. By reducing both
to the fiber
R/\mathfrak{m}=\overline{R}/\mathfrak{p}
, Kimura

[
Kim24
, theorem 5.4]
shows that the corresponding
functor
\mathcal{f\kern-0.5ptl\kern-1.0ptm\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}(e\Pi e)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{f\kern-0.5ptl\kern-1.0ptm\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}\Lambda
induces a bijection of
torsion classes. Likewise lemma 5.1
ibid.
gives the correspondence
between bricks.

Mutating v/s tilting

To motivate why a result such as
Theorem

A
is desirable towards a full
classification, we briefly sketch the key argument of

[
HW24
]
. Each algebra

\Lambda_{i,\text{con}}
produced by the homological minimal model programme
is silting discrete
[
Aug20
]
, thus can be
assigned a finite and complete hyperplane arrangement, the
silting fan
.
The chambers
\sigma_{1},...,\sigma_{n}
of this arrangement are in bijection with
the hearts
H_{i}\subset\mathcal{C}
, and each minimal sequence of wall–crossings

\sigma_{j}\rightsquigarrow\sigma_{i}
is assigned an atomic mutation functor

\Phi_{ij}
. Then, say, given an object
x\in H_{i}[-n,0]
with non-zero
cohomologies in degrees
0
and
n(>\!0)
, Hara–Wemyss consider the set of
paths
\sigma_{j}\rightsquigarrow\sigma_{i}
such that
\Phi_{ij}(x)
lies in

H_{j}[-n,0]
and show that for the longest such path

\sigma_{j_{0}}\rightsquigarrow\sigma_{i}
, the object
\Phi_{ij_{0}}(x)
in fact
lies in
H_{j_{0}}[-n+1,0]
. Iterating shows
x
lies in some
H_{j}
after a
finite sequence of mutations.

Finiteness of the silting fan underpins the argument,
guaranteeing that any poset considered has maximal elements. The
affine curse ensures we don’t have such privileges when studying

\Dm X
, and the silting fan of
\Lambda
is infinite and incomplete. So
while there still is an assignment of algebraic hearts and mutation functors to
chambers and wall-crossings (
theorem

3.5
), the fan has
infinite paths which culminate outside its support.

We thus replace mutation and the silting fan with
tilting in torsion pairs

and the
heart fan
respectively. Given any Abelian category
H
,
Happel–Reiten–Smalø
[
HRS96
]
show that the
set
\tilt(H)
of intermediate t-structures (i.e. t-structures with hearts
contained in
H[-1,0]
) can be recovered by tilting
H
in its torsion
subcategories, and the containment order of torsion classes enhances

\tilt(H)
to a
complete lattice
(i.e. a poset in which every subset
has an infimum and a supremum).
Broomhead–Pauksztello–Ploog–Woolf
[
BPPW24
]
assign
each heart
K\in\tilt(H)
to a cone
\CC K
and show that the ensemble

\HFan(H)
of heart cones is a fan. This heart fan is complete and simplicial
for categories such as
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
, and the silting fan then sits inside

\HFan(H)
as a sub-fan. Relating atomic mutations to tilting in

functorially finite
torsion classes, we thus have a ‘completion’ of
silting theory.

Giving a complete description of the lattice of torsion classes is hard, even
for finite dimensional
algebras
[
Tho21
,
DIRRT23
]
.
The heart fan gives some insight into tilts satisfying numerical criteria
(
theorem

4.3
), but there are
examples of tilts of algebraic hearts which have trivial (i.e.
\mathbf{0}
)
heart cone (see
[
BPPW24
, example 6.7]
). In such
situations the numerical criteria lead to tautologies, and we are left to our
own devices.

The main result of this paper, then, is that such situations do not arise when
studying tilts of
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
. Consequently a complete description of the
lattice of torsion classes is possible.

Theorem C

((=
4.6
))
.

The heart fan of
H=\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
is given by an intersection arrangement
associated to the restriction of an affine Dynkin root system, i.e. one of
the arrangements described in

[
IW
]
. The heart cones are
described as follows.

1.

(=
5.17
)

The trivial cone
\mathbf{0}
is
not
the heart cone of any
intermediate heart.

2.

(=
4.9
)

A cone outside the imaginary-root hyperplane

\{\delta=0\}
is a heart cone if and only if it is
full-dimensional, and in this case it is the heart cone of a unique
algebraic heart in
\tilt(H)
. The positive and negative orthants

\mathrm{C}^{+},\mathrm{C}^{-}
are the heart cones of
H
and

H[-1]
respectively, and the heart associated to any other full
dimensional cone can be expressed as a mutation of
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}

by choosing wall–crossing paths from
C^{\pm}
.

The hearts
H
and
H[-1]
are the maximal and minimal elements
in
\tilt(H)
respectively, and the partial order on remaining
algebraic hearts respects (atomic) wall–crossing distance from these.

3.

(=
4.10
,
4.11
,
4.12
)

The induced finite hyperplane arrangement on
\{\delta=0\}
is
naturally identified with the (real) Picard group of
X
, in a way
that every maximal cone
\sigma
in this subfan thus parametrises nef divisors on a
unique birational model
W
of
X
. Such
\sigma
is then the
heart cone of
\coh W
tracked under the composite
Bridgeland–Chen functor
\Dm W\to\Dm X
.

4.

(=
4.19
,
4.17
)

More generally, every non–trivial cone

\sigma\subset\{\delta=0\}
can be assigned a unique partial
contraction
W\to Y
such that
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}
(tracked under
flop equivalences) is the maximal heart in

\tilt(H)
with heart cone
\sigma
. Every other heart with
heart cone
\sigma
can
be obtained by arbitrarily mutating the algebraic components of

\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
and tilting in skyscrapers in the geometric
components.

An algebraic intermediate heart
K
then satisfies

K>\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}
if and only if there is an atomic path

\mathrm{C}^{+}\!\rightsquigarrow\CC K
which can be atomically extended
to an infinite sequence of wall–crossings

\mathrm{C}^{+}\!\rightsquigarrow\CC K\rightsquigarrow\CC K_{1}\rightsquigarrow\CC K_{2}\rightsquigarrow\cdot\cdot\cdot
with
generic limit in
\sigma
. In this case
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
is the
infimum of the decreasing chain
K>K_{1}>K_{2}>...
in
\tilt(H)
.

In
4
we establish the numerical story, enumerating for each cone in
the intersection arrangement all the intermediate hearts and King–semistable
objects associated to it. This involves establishing the fact that coherent
sheaves on any birational model
W
(which are a priori only intermediate with
respect to the perverse heart on
W
) are in fact intermediate with respect to
the perverse heart on
X
. For this a careful analysis of the partial order is
necessary, and convex geometric tools are needed not only to supply a
systematic enumeration scheme but also to establish crucial limiting results as
in
Theorem

C

4
.

Once the heart fan is filled in, the question of whether there are any hearts
hidden away in the
\mathbf{0}
–cone remains. Here the affine blessing
ensures that the non-algebraic locus in the heart fan is codimension 1, so
every heart in
\tilt(H)
has tight algebraic bounds.

The following example is illustrative.

Single-curve flops

Let
R=\mathbb{C}\llbracket u,v,x,y\rrbracket/(uv-xy)
be the

\text{cA}_{1}
singularity, i.e. the base of the Atiyah flop

X\dashrightarrow W
where
X
is the neighbourhood of a
(-1,-1)

rational curve
C
. The two flopping contractions
X,W\to Z=\Spec R
are
obtained by blowing up the ideals
(u,x)
and
(u,y)
respectively.

Accordingly the modification algebra associated to

X
is
\Lambda=\End(R\oplus(u,x))
. It can be shown that all
modification algebras are isomorphic and are related by derived equivalences as
below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot\cdot\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dfl\End\left(\!\!\begin{smallmatrix}L\\
\oplus\\
(u,y)\end{smallmatrix}\!\!\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dfl\End\left(\!\!\begin{smallmatrix}R\\
\oplus\\
(u,y)\end{smallmatrix}\!\!\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dfl\End\left(\!\!\begin{smallmatrix}R\\
\oplus\\
(u,x)\end{smallmatrix}\!\!\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dfl\End\left(\!\!\begin{smallmatrix}K\\
\oplus\\
(u,x)\end{smallmatrix}\!\!\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot\cdot\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dm W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dm X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{1}}

Here the functors
\Psi_{i}\colon\Dfl(\End N)\to\Dfl(\End M)
are given as

\RHom(\Hom(N,M),-)
, and the index records which summand changes between

N
and
M
. This changed summand is computed via
mutation
, thus for
instance
(u,x)
and
(u,y)
are (first) syzygies of each other while

L\subset(u,y)^{\oplus 2}
is the kernel of the natural map
((u,y)\hookrightarrow R)\oplus((u,y)\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\to$\cr\raise 3.00003pt\hbox{$\lx@scalerel@obj{\sim}\mkern 7.0mu$}\cr}}}(v,x)\hookrightarrow R)
.

Any category
\Dfl(\End M)
has a natural heart
H_{M}=\mathcal{f\kern-0.5ptl\kern-1.0ptm\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}(\End M)
, and
tracking this across equivalences appearing in the above diagram (and their
inverses) produces new t-structures in
\Dm X
. Such a t-structure is
intermediate with respect to the perverse heart when the chain of
functors
\Dfl(\End M)\rightsquigarrow\Dm X
is chosen as short as possible,
i.e. the tracks of
H_{M}
that lie in
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}[-1,0]
are of the form

\textnormal{VdB}^{-1}\circ(...\circ\Psi_{0}\circ\Psi_{1}\circ\Psi_{0}\circ...)H_{M}\quad\text{or}\qquad\textnormal{VdB}^{-1}\circ(...\circ\Psi_{0}\circ\Psi_{1}\circ\Psi_{0}\circ...)\!{}^{-1}\;H_{M}[-1].

Note the specified chain completely determines the domain of the functor, so we
may drop the subscript for brevity. We also drop
VdB
from the
notation, so for instance
\Psi_{1}H
is the image of
\mathcal{f\kern-0.5ptl\kern-1.0ptm\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}(R\oplus(u,y))
under the functor
\textnormal{VdB}^{-1}\circ\Psi_{1}
. The algebraic hearts
in
\Dm X
thus obtained are in natural bijection with full-dimensional cones
in the
\widetilde{\text{A}}_{1}
(
) intersection
arrangement, see
fig.

1
.

H[-1]
H

\Phi_{1}H[-1]
\Phi_{0}\Phi_{1}H[-1]
\Phi_{1}\Phi_{0}\Phi_{1}H[-1]

Figure 1:

The heart fan for a minimal resolution of the

\text{cA}_{1}
singularity.

Hyperplanes are induced by the
\widetilde{\text{A}}_{1}
root
system, where the simple real roots
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}
are
identified with the
\KK
-theory classes of
\omega_{C}[1]
and

{O}_{C}(-1)
respectively.

The ray
\{\delta=0,\alpha_{0}\geq 0\}
, geometrically the ‘limit’ of the path

\CC(H)\dashrightarrow\CC(\Psi_{0}H)\dashrightarrow\CC(\Psi_{1}\Psi_{0}H)\dashrightarrow\cdots
,
is the heart cone of
\coh X=\,\mathop{\textnormal{}}{inf}\,\{H,\,\Psi_{0}H,\,\Psi_{1}\Psi_{0}H,\,\ldots\}
. Likewise it is also the heart cone of the
reversed geometric heart

\anticoh X=\mathop{\textnormal{}}{sup}\,\{H[-1],\Phi_{1}H[-1],\Phi_{0}\Phi_{1}H[-1],\ldots\}
which can
be obtained by tilting
\coh X
in the torsion class generated by all
skyscrapers.

The ray
\{\delta=0,\alpha_{0}\leq 0\}
is similarly seen to be the heart cone of

\textnormal{flop}(\coh W)
and other geometric hearts that live on
W
, where

\textnormal{flop}\colon\Dm W\to\Dm X
is the Bridgeland–Chen flop functor (in this
case isomorphic to
\textnormal{VdB}^{-1}\circ\Psi_{1}\circ\textnormal{VdB}
).

Now the partial order on algebraic hearts (indicated in
fig.

1
) is
such that wall–crossings correspond to simple tilts
(
5.2
), thus
\Psi_{0}H
is obtained by tilting
H

in the torsion class
\langle\omega_{C}[1]\rangle
while
\Phi_{1}H[-1]
and

H[-1]
are related by a tilt in
\langle{O}_{C}(-1)\rangle
. Considering
how simples sit in relation to torsion pairs then allows us to ‘push’
non-algebraic intermediate hearts
K\in\tilt(H)
towards the geometric hyperplane.

Algorithm 1.1

(Simple–tilting)
.

Let
K
be a non-algebraic intermediate heart. Write
H=T\ast F
for the torsion pair corresponding to
K(=F\ast T[-1])
.

1.

Since
K\neq H
, one of the simples of
H
(say

S_{0}=\omega_{C}[1]
) lies in
T
and this forces the inequality

K<\Psi_{0}H
.

2.

The inequality
\Psi_{0}H>K
shows that
T
intersects

\Psi_{0}H
nontrivially, hence must contain some simple
\{\Psi_{0}S_{0},\Psi_{0}S_{1}\}
of
\Psi_{0}H
. Since
\Psi_{0}S_{0}=\omega_{C}
lies
outside
H
, we must have
\Psi_{0}S_{1}\in T
and thus

\Psi_{1}\Psi_{0}H>K
. Iterating, we have
K<...\Psi_{0}\Psi_{1}\Psi_{0}H
for arbitrarily long chains, and hence
K\leq\coh X
.

3.

On the other hand
K\neq H[-1]
so
F
contains some simple of

H
, which in this case must be
S_{1}={O}_{C}(-1)
. This shows

K>\Phi_{1}H[-1]
, and iterating as above gives
K\geq\anticoh X
.

4.

Since
\anticoh X
and
\coh X
both share the heart cone

\{\delta=0,\alpha_{0}\geq 0\}
,
K
must do so too, and in
particular
K
is numerical. In fact
K
can be expressed as a
tilt of
\coh X
in
\langle{O}_{p}\;|\;p\in Q\rangle
for some

Q\subset C
.

Thus every heart in
\tilt(\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}})
is either algebraic, or can be
shown to be geometric via the above recipe.

Multi-curve flops

After accounting for perverse hearts for partial contractions, the above
description of the heart fan and the partial order carries over to higher rank
cases. Thus for a crepant resolution
X\to Z
of the
\text{cA}_{2}
singularity

Z=\Spec\mathbb{C}\llbracket u,v,x,y\rrbracket/(uv-xy(x+y))
, the heart fan of

\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
is described by an
\widetilde{\text{A}}_{2}

(
) root system as in

fig.

2
.

The
3
-dimensional heart fan for a
\text{cA}_{2}
resolution, sliced
along affine hyperplanes (left) and the unit sphere (right). The
mutation functors are abbreviated, e.g. by writing
\Psi_{01}
for

\Psi_{1}\circ\Psi_{0}
. Cones in the hyperplane
\{\delta=0\}
have
been labelled by the
3
-fold whose geometric hearts
they are associated to, where e.g.
\nu_{212}X
is the flop of
the second curve in
\nu_{12}X
.

Given a non-algebraic heart
K\in\tilt(\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}})
, we can follow

algorithm

1.1
to produce bounding sequences

\ldots\Psi_{i_{2}}\Psi_{i_{1}}H\quad>\quad K\quad>\quad\ldots\Phi_{j_{2}}\Phi_{j_{1}}H[-1]

by iterated simple tilting. However this does not suffice for ‘pushing
K
to the geometric hyperplane’, and it is possible that the corresponding paths in the heart fan do not converge
to the same cone (illustrated in
fig.

2
). In this regard the
single-curve case is deceptively simple.

Establishing
\CC K\neq 0
thus demands more finesse, and our strategy is
to utilise line bundles on birational models for this purpose.
Under the identification of
\RealPic X
with the
hyperplane
\{\delta=0\}
, (the proper transform of) any line bundle
{L}\in\Pic W
corresponds to a vector
\theta
in the heart fan, so for any cone

\sigma
we can consider the submonoid
\Pic W\cap\sigma
(e.g. if

\sigma=\CC(\textnormal{flop}(\coh W))
then this is the monoid of nef bundles). On
the other hand,
\Pic W
has a natural action on
\Dm W
which induces an
action
\Pic W\circlearrowright\Dm X
across the composite flop equivalence.
Using the abbreviated notation
{L}\otimes H\coloneq\textnormal{flop}({L}\otimes_{W}(\textnormal{flop}^{-1}H))
, we establish the following in
5.3
.

Theorem D

((=
5.10
,
5.12
,
5.13
))
.

Writing
H=\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
for the standard heart in
\Dm X
, the
following statements hold.

1.

Given any birational model
W
of
X
and a line bundle

{L}\in\Pic W
, any heart in
\Dm X
of the form

{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H
or
{L}\otimes H[-1]
lies in
\tilt(H)

if and only if
{L}
is nef.

2.

If
W^{\prime}
is another birational model such that

{L}\in\Pic W
and its proper transform
{L}^{\prime}\in\Pic W^{\prime}
are
both nef, then there are equalities of t-structures

{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H={L}^{\prime}\,\check{}\,\otimes H
,
{L}\otimes H[-1]={L}^{\prime}\otimes H[-1]
.

3.

For any cone
\sigma\subset\CC(\textnormal{flop}\coh W)
in

\HFan(H)
, the induced actions of monoid
\Pic W\cap\sigma
on
the subsets

\sigma\text{-}\tilt^{+}(H)=\;\bigcup_{{L}\in\Pic W\cap\sigma}\,\left[{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H,H\right],\qquad\sigma\text{-}\tilt^{-}(H)=\;\bigcup_{{L}\in\Pic W\cap\sigma}\,\left[H[-1],{L}\otimes H[-1]\right]

respect the partial order inherited from
\tilt(H)
and the
monoid-order on
\Pic W\cap\sigma
.

4.

Each heart in the posets above is algebraic, and conversely every
algebraic heart in
\tilt(H)
lies in some poset of the above form
for suitable
W,\sigma
.

5.

The infimum of
\sigma
-
\tilt^{+}(H)
is the maximal element of

\tilt(H)
with heart cone
\sigma
, likewise the supremum of

\sigma
-
\tilt^{-}(H)
is the minimal element of
\tilt(H)

with heart cone
\sigma
.

\left[\!\begin{smallmatrix}1\\

\rightsquigarrow

\coh X
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
\text{flop}(\coh\nu_{1}X)

Continuing from
fig.

2
, the actions of
\Pic X
and

\nu_{1}X)
on
H
are shown. Here

\left[\!\begin{smallmatrix}i\\
j\end{smallmatrix}\!\right]

denotes the line bundle on
X
which has degrees
i
and

j
on the two exceptional curves respectively, and we use double
brackets (
\llbracket\!\begin{smallmatrix}i\\
j\end{smallmatrix}\!\rrbracket
) for bundles on the flop
\nu_{1}X
.

Note that since

\left[\!\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\!\right]
and
its proper transform
\llbracket\!\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\!\rrbracket
are both
trivial on the flopped curve, their actions coincide i.e.
\left\llbracket\!\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\!\right\rrbracket\check{}\,\!\!\otimes\!H=\left[\!\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\!\right]\check{}\,\!\!\otimes\!H
.

The shaded region represents
\sigma
-
\tilt^{+}(H)
for

\sigma
shown in
fig.

2
.

The above result can be seen as a ‘fixed-point theorem’ for
\tilt(H)
. Indeed if
{L}\in\Pic X
is an ample line bundle, then the orbit of

H
under iterative applications of
{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes(-)
limits to

\coh X
which is fixed as a heart by
\Pic X
.

We then have the following recipe, which shows every heart cone is non-zero.

Algorithm 1.2

(Skyscraper–hunting)
.

Suppose
K\subset\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}[-1,0]
is a non-algebraic t-structure
corresponding to a torsion pair
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}=T\ast F
.

1.

By
lemma

5.22
there is a
unique birational model
W\in\mathop{\textnormal{}}{Bir}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
such that

\textnormal{flop}^{-1}(K)
is intermediate with respect to

\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Z}\right)$}}
and also contains the full subcategory
\{w\in\coh W\;|\;\mathbf{R}\pi_{\ast}w=0\}
(we say
K

lives
on

W
). Thus replacing
K
by
\textnormal{flop}^{-1}(K)
if
necessary, we may assume
K
lives on
X
.

With this hypothesis satisfied, every skyscraper sheaf
{O}_{p}\in\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}\cap\coh X
is either torsion or torsion-free with
respect to the torsion pair
T\ast F

(
lemma

5.24
).

2.

Suppose
C_{i}\subset X
is an exceptional curve and
{L}_{i}\in\Pic X
is the line bundle with degree
1
on
C_{i}
and degree
0

elsewhere. In particular, the maximal and minimal elements of

\tilt(H)
whose heart cone is generated by
{L}_{i}
are the
categories
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{X_{i}}\right)$}}
and
\antizeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{X_{i}}\right)$}}

respectively, where
X\to X_{i}
is the partial contraction of
C_{i}
.

Now if some
p\in C_{i}
satisfies
{O}_{p}\in T
, then

lemma

5.18
shows
{L}_{i}^{\otimes(-n)}\otimes H>K

for arbitrarily large
n
, and thus
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{X_{i}}\right)$}}\geq K
. Likewise if some
p\in C_{i}
satisfies
{O}_{p}\in F
, then we have
the bound
K\geq\antizeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{X_{i}}\right)$}}
.

3.

Thus if there is some curve
C_{i}
with two points
p,q\in C_{i}

such that
{O}_{p}\in T
and
{O}_{q}\in F
, then
K
lies in the
interval
\left[\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{X_{i}}\right)$}},\antizeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{X_{i}}\right)$}}\right]
and in particular has non-zero
heart cone.

4.

Otherwise by connectivity of the exceptional curves in
X
,
either
T
or
F
contains
all
the skyscrapers. By a similar
argument, this forces one of the bounds
K\geq\coh X
or
K\leq\anticoh X
. The bound on the other side can then be found by chasing
simple tilts from
H
or
H[-1]
as in
algorithm

1.1
,
and this suffices to prove that
\CC(K)
is non-zero
(
lemma

5.20
).

Heart cones of semi-geometric hearts

Continuing to work with the
\text{cA}_{2}
crepant resolution above, consider a
flop
\rho:X\dashrightarrow\nu_{1}X
, and the partial contraction

\tau\colon X\to Y
of the flopped curve. The heart cone of the associated
semi-geometric heart
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
is given by the ray
\sigma=\CC(\coh X)\cap\CC(\textnormal{flop}\coh(\nu_{1}X))
.

To examine other hearts that lie on
\sigma
, note that
Y
has a unique
singular point (the image of the flopped curve), and
\rho
is simply the
Atiyah flop over a neighbourhood
Z_{1}
of this point. Computing the category of

\sigma
-semistable objects then recovers the category of perverse sheaves
associated to the
\text{cA}_{1}
flopping contraction
\tau^{-1}Z_{1}\to Z_{1}
,
and thus (up to tilting in skyscrapers in the smooth locus) every other intermediate
heart with heart cone
\sigma
is obtained from tilts of this smaller category

\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{\tau^{-1}Z_{1}}{Z_{1}}\right)$}}
.

Convex geometrically this manifests as the fact that the
\text{cA}_{2}

intersection arrangement ‘looks like’ the
\text{cA}_{1}
arrangement locally around

\sigma
(as is apparent from
figs.

1
and
2
). This phenomenon is
more general and a correspondence between the heart fan of
H
and that of its
semistable subcategories is sketched in
remark

4.5
.
Broomhead–Pauksztello–Ploog–Woolf’s
tangent multifan
construction

[
BPPW24
, ‘Further work’]
investigates this to a
greater depth.

Notation and Conventions

We work over the ground field
\mathbb{C}
, and fix once and for all a
complete local isolated compound du Val singularity
Z=\Spec R
with singular
point corresponding to the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}\subset R
.

All algebras we consider are finitely generated
R
-algebras. Given
such an algebra
\Lambda
, we write
\Db\Lambda
for the bounded derived
category of right
\Lambda
-modules and
\Dfl\Lambda
for the full
subcategory of complexes whose cohomology (with respect to
\mathcal{m\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}\Lambda
) has
finite length. This forms a triangulated subcategory of
\Db\Lambda
, and we
write
\KK\Lambda
for the Grothendieck group of
\Dfl\Lambda
.

Likewise, all
3
-folds we consider are
Z
-schemes. Given such a scheme

\pi\colon X\to Z
, we write
\Db X
for the bounded derived category of coherent
sheaves on
X
and
\Dm X
for the full subcategory of
\Db X
containing
complexes whose cohomology sheaves are supported on
\pi^{-1}[\mathfrak{m}]
.
Again, we write
\KK X
for the Grothendieck group of
\Dm X
.

Given full subcategories
U,V
of an Abelian (or triangulated) category, we
write
U\ast V
for the full subcategory of objects
x
that sit in an exact
sequence
0\to u\to x\to v\to 0
(resp. an exact triangle
u\to x\to v\to u[1]
) with
u\in U
,
v\in V
. Note the operation
(\ast)
is
associative
[
BBD82
, lemma 1.3.10]
so writing

U^{\ast n}=U\ast\cdots\ast U
(
n
factors) is unambiguous, and
we write
\langle U\rangle=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}U^{\ast n}
for the
extension-closure of
U
. If
U
sits in a triangulated category and

I\subset\mathbb{Z}
is an interval, we write
U[I]=\langle\{u[i]\;|\;u\in U,\;i\in I\}\rangle
and employ obvious shorthands such as

U[0,1]=U[[0,1]]
,
U[\leq 0]=U[(-\infty,0]]
when convenient.

§ 2 Dynkin combinatorics and intersection arrangements

A recurring motif in our treatment is that of
mutation combinatorics, which allow us to enumerate sets over Dynkin data. We
briefly explain the construction in an abstract setting, and recognise its
various manifestations as they come up throughout the article.

§ 2.1 Sets with mutation

Let
G
be a Dynkin graph with associated Weyl
group
\textnormal{W}(G)
, which is generated by simple reflections
\{s_{i}\;|\;i\in G\}
. When
\textnormal{W}(G)
is finite there is a unique longest element
w_{G}
in
the weak (Bruhat) order, and further
[
IW
, lemma
1.2]
there is an involution
\text{inv}_{G}:G\to G

such that
w_{G}s_{i}w_{G}=s_{\text{inv}_{G}(i)}
. If
\textnormal{W}(G)
is not finite, we
simply declare
\text{inv}_{G}
to be the identity.

For any subgraph
J\subset G
, this defines a map
\iota_{J}:G\setminus J\to G
given by
\iota_{J}(i)=\text{inv}_{J+i}(i)
where

\text{inv}_{J+i}
is the involution for the full subgraph
J\cup\{i\}
. When the choice of
J
is clear, we simply write
\iota
instead of

\iota_{J}
.

Definition 2.1
.

The simple mutation of
J\subset G
at
i\in G\setminus J
is given by the subgraph
\nu_{i}J=\{i\}\cup J\setminus\{\iota(i)\}
in
G
.

Clearly, simple mutation is involutive in the sense that

\nu_{\iota(i)}(\nu_{i}J)=J
.

When iterating we omit brackets, thus writing

\nu_{i_{n}}\ldots\nu_{i_{1}}J
to mean
\nu_{i_{n}}(\nu_{i_{n-1}}\ldots\nu_{i_{1}}J)
,
noting that the sequence makes sense only if
i_{1}\in G\setminus J
and

i_{j+1}\in G\setminus(\nu_{i_{j}}\ldots\nu_{i_{1}}J)
for each

j=1,\ldots,n-1
. We say such a sequence of symbols

\nu=\nu_{i_{n}}\ldots\nu_{i_{1}}
is a
J
-path of length
n
.

Definition 2.2
.

A
set with
G
-mutation
is a set
A
equipped with a map

\mathbb{J}\colon A\to 2^{G}
that assigns each element of
A
to some subgraph
of
G
, and a collection of functions
{(\nu_{i}\colon\{a\in A\;|\;i\notin\mathbb{J}(a)\}\to A)}_{i\in G}
(called
simple mutations
) such that
for each
a\in A
, we have
\mathbb{J}(\nu_{i}a)=\nu_{i}\mathbb{J}(a)
and

\nu_{\iota(i)}(\nu_{i}a)=a
.

If
A
is a set with
G
-mutation, the
exchange quiver

\ExQuiv(A)

is a quiver with vertices
A
and a labelled arrow
i:a\to\nu_{i}a
for
each
a\in A
and
i\in G\setminus\mathbb{J}(a)
. Paths in the
exchange quiver can be described by a sequence of valid mutations from a
specified starting vertex—it is easy to see that each
\mathbb{J}(a)
-path

\nu=\nu_{i_{n}}\ldots\nu_{i_{1}}
describes a unique path in the exchange quiver

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline qn:positivepath}a\xrightarrow{\;i_{1}\;}\nu_{i_{1}}a\xrightarrow{\;i_{2}\;}\cdots\xrightarrow{\;i_{n}\;}\nu_{i_{n}}\nu_{i_{n-1}}\ldots\nu_{i_{1}}a

(1)

which we call the
positive path

\nu
from
a
, and further every
path in the exchange quiver corresponds to a unique pair
(a,\nu)
in this
way. If the positive path (
1
) has minimal length among all
paths in
\ExQuiv(A)
from
a
to
\nu a
, we say it is
minimal
.

Remark 2.3
.

In what follows, we will work with both—an ADE Dynkin graph
\Delta
and
its affine counterpart
\underline{\Delta}
. Thus to avoid confusion when
considering subsets
J\subset\Delta\subset\underline{\Delta}
, we use the term

spherical
J
-paths
for the paths corresponding to the ambient
graph
G=\Delta
and reserve the unqualified term
J
-path to mean paths
for
G=\underline{\Delta}
. Writing
0\in\underline{\Delta}\setminus\Delta
for the
extended vertex, it can be seen that for such
J
there is a natural
bijective correspondence between spherical
J
-paths and
J
-paths in
which the symbol
\nu_{0}
does not occur.

Example 2.4
.

Given a Dynkin graph
G
, the set of all its subgraphs
2^{G}
is
naturally a set with
G
-mutation. This naturally breaks up into smaller
sets with
G
-mutation called
mutation classes
, where the mutation
class of
J\subseteq G
is the subset of
2^{G}
containing subgraphs that
can be obtained from
J
by iterated mutation. Thus
\{\emptyset\}
and

\{G\}
are mutation classes of the trivial subgraphs
\emptyset
and

G
respectively. Iyama–Wemyss
[
IW
, §4.2]

compute all mutation classes containing ‘large’ subgraphs of affine Dynkin
diagrams, i.e. subgraphs whose complements contain
3
vertices. We draw
the mutation class
\mathcal{E}_{7,4}
in
fig.

4
.

Figure 4
:
The mutation class of
J=\{2,3,5,6,7\}
inside the

\widetilde{\text{E}}_{7}
Dynkin graph

1
2
3
4
5
6
7
0
, where subgraphs are indicated by marking off
their vertices with a cross (
). Arrows
indicate simple mutation, the symbol
\nu
has been omitted for
brevity.

Example 2.5
.

If
A
is a set with an action of the Weyl group
\textnormal{W}(G)
, then there is
a natural
G
-mutation structure given by
\mathbb{J}(a)=\emptyset
,

\nu_{i}(a)=a\cdot s_{i}
for all
a\in A
,
i\in G
. Thus for instance
the set of chambers
\Chambers(G)
in the Tits cone of
\textnormal{W}(G)
is a set
with
G
-mutation, where the (right)-action is by simple wall crossings.

§ 2.2 Intersection arrangements

The combinatorial construction central to our exposition is a generalisation of
example
2.5
, where a subset
J\subset G

determines a hyperplane arrangement in Euclidean space
[
IW
, chapter
1]
and
G
-mutation is realised as an action of
the associated Deligne groupoid (also called the
J
-cone groupoid
, see
§ 2.3.1
ibid.
). The construction is most relevant to us when
G
is
an ADE Dynkin graph
\Delta
, or its affine counterpart

\underline{\Delta}
which has extended vertex
0\in\underline{\Delta}\setminus\Delta
, and
we recall necessary details below.

Affine root systems

We recap the unrestricted (Coxeter) setting first, following e.g.
[
Kac80
, §1]
.

Given the affine Dynkin diagram
\underline{\Delta}
of rank
n
, the
root lattice

\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{h}(\underline{\Delta})
is a free
\mathbb{Z}
-module with
basis given by the
simple roots

\{\alpha_{i}\;|\;i\in\underline{\Delta}\}
.
The Cartan matrix associated to
\underline{\Delta}
gives a degenerate symmetric bilinear
form
(-,-)
on
\mathfrak{h}
, and we use these to define the
simple
reflections

s_{i}\colon\mathfrak{h}\to\mathfrak{h}
(
i\in\underline{\Delta})
given by

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline qn:simplereflectiononroots}s_{i}(\alpha_{j})=\alpha_{j}-(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})\alpha_{i}.

(2)

These define the
Weyl group

\textnormal{W}(\underline{\Delta})=\langle s_{i}\;|\;i\in\underline{\Delta}\rangle\subset\text{GL}(\mathfrak{h})
, which is isomorphic to the
Coxeter group associated to
\underline{\Delta}
with standard generators

\{s_{i}\;|\;i\in\underline{\Delta}\}
. In fact each subset
J\subseteq\underline{\Delta}
determines a parabolic subgroup
\textnormal{W}(J)=\langle s_{i}\;|\;i\in J\rangle\subseteq\textnormal{W}(\underline{\Delta})
, which is the Coxeter group associated to the full subgraph
spanned by
J
. If
J\neq\underline{\Delta}
then this is a finite Coxeter group,
and hence has a unique Coxeter element
w_{J}
(defined to be the longest
element in the Bruhat order).

The
set of real roots

\textnormal{Root}(\underline{\Delta})
is the union of

\textnormal{W}(\underline{\Delta})
-orbits of the simple roots. We say a real root is

positive
if it can be expressed as a non-negative linear combination of
the simple roots, and write
\textnormal{Root}^{+}(\underline{\Delta})
for the set of positive real
roots. The set
\textnormal{Root}^{-}(\underline{\Delta})
of
negative real roots
is defined
likewise.

Vertices in the diagram
\underline{\Delta}
can be Lie-theoretically assigned
numerical labels
(\delta_{i})_{i\in\underline{\Delta}}
which are computed and given
for each Dynkin type in
[
Kac80
, table Z]
. This is
always a tuple of positive integers, and the ‘extended’ vertex

0\in\underline{\Delta}\setminus\Delta
is assigned the integer
\delta_{0}=1
. Then
the vector
\delta=\sum_{i\in\underline{\Delta}}\delta_{i}\alpha_{i}
is called the

primitive positive imaginary root
in
\mathfrak{h}
, and any non-zero
multiple of
\delta
is called an
imaginary root
. The
root
system
of
\underline{\Delta}
is then the set of all (real and imaginary) roots in

\mathfrak{h}
, and is preserved by the action of the Weyl group which acts
transitively on
\textnormal{Root}(\underline{\Delta})
and fixes
\delta
.

Restricted root systems

Suppose
\mathfrak{J}\subset\underline{\Delta}
is such that

|\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J}|\geq 2
. This defines a decomposition

\mathfrak{h}(\underline{\Delta})=\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{J})\oplus\mathfrak{h}(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J}),

where we write
\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{J})
for
the span of the simple roots
\{\alpha_{i}\;|\;i\in\mathfrak{J}\}
(and define

\mathfrak{h}(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
likewise). We say

\mathfrak{h}(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
is the
restricted root lattice

associated to
\mathfrak{J}
, and the
restricted root system
is the image of the
root system under the surjection
\mathfrak{h}(\underline{\Delta})\twoheadrightarrow\mathfrak{h}(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
.

The notions of restricted (simple,
positive, negative) real roots and restricted imaginary roots are defined
likewise as the non-zero images of the corresponding objects in

\mathfrak{h}
. In particular there are
|\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J}|
restricted
simple roots
\{\alpha_{i}\;|\;i\in\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J}\}
, and a

primitive positive restricted imaginary root

\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}=\sum_{i\in\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J}}\delta_{i}\alpha_{i}
. Writing

\textnormal{Root}(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})
for the set of restricted real roots (and defining the
subsets
\textnormal{Root}^{\pm}(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})
of positive and negative restricted real
roots accordingly), we thus see that the restricted root system is given as

\underbrace{\textnormal{Root}^{+}(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})\;\sqcup\;\textnormal{Root}^{-}(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})}_{\textnormal{Root}(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})}\;\sqcup\;\;\{n\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}\;|\;n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}\}.

To obtain the analog of a Weyl-group action for restricted roots, it is
necessary to consider simultaneously all restricted root lattices

\mathfrak{h}(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J})
ranging over
\mathfrak{J}
-paths
\nu
.
In the Coxeter setting, a simple reflection
s_{i}\in\textnormal{W}(\underline{\Delta})
was
characterised by the property of being an involution such that

s_{i}(\alpha_{j})-\alpha_{j}
is equal to
-2\alpha_{i}
if
j=i
, and is a
non-negative multiple of
\alpha_{i}
otherwise. Iyama–Wemyss suggest the
following generalisation.

Proposition 2.6

(
[
NW23
, lemmas 5.1 and 5.2]
)
.

For
\mathfrak{J}\subset\underline{\Delta}
and
i\in\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J}
, the linear
map
\mathfrak{h}\to\mathfrak{h}
given by the action of
w_{\mathfrak{J}}w_{\mathfrak{J}+i}\in\textnormal{W}(\underline{\Delta})
maps the subset
\mathfrak{h}(\nu_{i}\mathfrak{J})
isomorphically onto
\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{J})
, thus inducing an isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{h}(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\nu_{i}\mathfrak{J})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{h}(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{\mathfrak{J}}w_{\mathfrak{J}+i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

(3)

This map preserves the root systems, in particular acting on the simple
roots and the primitive positive imaginary root as

\varphi_{i}(\delta_{\nu_{i}\mathfrak{J}})=\delta_{\mathfrak{J}},\qquad\varphi_{i}(\alpha_{\iota(i)})=-\alpha_{i},\qquad\varphi_{i}(\alpha_{j})-\alpha_{j}\;\in\;\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\cdot\alpha_{i}\quad(j\neq i).

Further, defining

\varphi_{\iota(i)}:\mathfrak{h}(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})\to\mathfrak{h}(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\nu_{i}\mathfrak{J})
analogously, we have

\varphi_{\iota(i)}=(\varphi_{i})^{-1}
.

Hyperplane arrangements

Let
\mathfrak{h}
be the root lattice associated
to
\underline{\Delta}
as above, with the action of

\textnormal{W}(\underline{\Delta})
as described. The dual action on the Euclidean space

\Theta=\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathfrak{h},\mathbb{R})
preserves each of the
subsets
\{\delta>0\}
,
\{\delta<0\}
, and
\{\delta<0\}
, and the
fundamental domains for the respective actions have closures given by the cones

\displaystyle\mathrm{C}^{+}=\{\theta\in\Theta\;|\;\theta(\alpha_{i})\geq 0\text{ for all
}i\in\underline{\Delta}\},\qquad\mathrm{C}^{-}=-\mathrm{C}^{+},

\displaystyle\mathrm{C}^{0}=\{\theta\in\Theta\;|\;\theta(\alpha_{i})\geq 0\text{ for all
}i\in\Delta,\quad\theta(\delta)=0\}.

Further the faces of
w\mathrm{C}^{+}
are all given by
w\mathrm{C}_{J}^{+}
for some
subset
J\subset\underline{\Delta}
, where

\mathrm{C}_{J}^{+}=\mathrm{C}^{+}\cap\bigcap_{i\in J}\{\alpha_{i}=0\}.

The faces
w\mathrm{C}^{-}_{J}\subseteq w\mathrm{C}^{-}

(
J\subseteq\underline{\Delta}
) and
w\mathrm{C}^{0}_{J}\subseteq w\mathrm{C}^{0}

(
J\subseteq\Delta
) are given likewise, and as a consequence we have a
complete simplicial fan in
\Theta
called the
Weyl arrangement
given
as

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline qn:titsdecomposition}\Arr(\underline{\Delta})=\underbrace{\left\{wC_{J}^{+}\;\middle|\;w\in\textnormal{W}(\underline{\Delta}),\quad J\subseteq\underline{\Delta}\right\}}_{\Arr^{+}(\underline{\Delta})}\;\;\cup\;\underbrace{\left\{wC_{J}^{0}\;\middle|\;w\in\textnormal{W}(\Delta),\quad J\subseteq\Delta\right\}}_{\Arr(\Delta)}\;\;\cup\;\underbrace{\left\{wC_{J}^{-}\;\middle|\;w\in\textnormal{W}(\underline{\Delta}),\quad J\subseteq\underline{\Delta}\right\}}_{\Arr^{-}(\underline{\Delta})}.

(4)

The Weyl arrangement is induced by the root system for
\underline{\Delta}
, in the
sense that every face can be described as the intersection of half-spaces
defined by root hyperplanes
\{\alpha=0\}
. Further each such hyperplane
is corresponds to a unique positive root in
\textnormal{Root}^{+}(\underline{\Delta})\cup\{\delta\}
.

The subfan
\Arr^{+}(\underline{\Delta})
is supported on the rational subset

\{\delta>0\}\cup\{0\}
, and is called the
Tits cone
. The subfan

\Arr(\Delta)
is supported on
\{\delta=0\}
, and induces a complete
simplicial fan on this hyperplane. Further it has finitely many cones which are
indexed over the parabolic subgroup
\textnormal{W}(\Delta)
. This is possible because

\textnormal{W}(\underline{\Delta})
decomposes as a semi-direct product of
\textnormal{W}(\Delta)
and
the coroot lattice, and the action on
\{\delta=0\}
is such that

\textnormal{W}(\Delta)
acts faithfully while the coroot lattice fixes the hyperplane
pointwise.

Now a subset
\mathfrak{J}\subset\underline{\Delta}
with
|\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J}|\geq 2

defines the restricted root lattice
\mathfrak{h}(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
,
which we view as a split quotient of
\mathfrak{h}
. Accordingly, the dual
space
\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})=\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathfrak{h}(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J}),\mathbb{R})
can be
identified with the subspace
\bigcap_{i\in\mathfrak{J}}\{\alpha_{i}=0\}\subset\Theta
.
Since the subspace is defined by root hyperplanes, every cone
\sigma\in\Arr(\underline{\Delta})
intersects
\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
in a face of

\sigma
, whence the Weyl arrangement induces a complete simplicial fan

\displaystyle\Arr(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left\{\sigma\cap\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})\;\middle|\;\sigma\in\Arr^{+}(\underline{\Delta})\right\}}_{\Arr^{+}(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})}\;\cup\;\underbrace{\left\{\sigma\cap\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})\;\middle|\;\sigma\in\Arr(\Delta)\right\}}_{\Arr(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})}\;\cup\;\underbrace{\left\{\sigma\cap\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})\;\middle|\;\sigma\in\Arr^{-}(\underline{\Delta})\right\}}_{\Arr^{-}(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})}

called the (full)
\mathfrak{J}
-cone arrangement
. This is precisely the subfan of

\Arr(\underline{\Delta})
supported on
\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
. It can be
seen that the
\mathfrak{J}
-cone arrangement is induced by the restricted root system
associated to
\mathfrak{J}\subset\underline{\Delta}
.

\{\delta_{J}=0\}
\{\delta_{J}+1=0\}
\{\delta_{J}-1=0\}

Figure 5:
Representative affine slices of the
\mathcal{E}_{7,4}

arrangement, associated to the Dynkin data

(
J=\{2,3,5,6,7\}
) as in
fig.

4
. The principal
chambers
\mathrm{C}_{J}^{+},\mathrm{C}_{J}^{0},\mathrm{C}_{J}^{-}
are indicated by the symbols

+,0,–
respectively.

The subfan
\Arr^{+}(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})
, called the
Tits cone
, contains
infinitely many cones and is supported on the subset
\{\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}>0\}\cup\{0\}
. The subfan
\Arr(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})
on the other hand is a complete
simplicial fan on the hyperplane
\{\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}=0\}
, with finitely many cones
whose combinatorics are controlled by the ADE Dynkin diagram
\Delta
. We
write
\Chambers(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})
for the subset of maximal cones in

\Arr^{+}(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})
, and
\Chambers(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})
for the subset of
maximal cones in
\Arr(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})
.

Simple wall crossings

Any chamber
wC^{+}\in\Chambers(\underline{\Delta})

has
|\underline{\Delta}|
codimension-one faces (
walls
), where the
i
th
wall
w\mathrm{C}^{+}_{\{i\}}
spans the root hyperplane
\{w\alpha_{i}=0\}
. The
wall
w\mathrm{C}^{+}_{\{i\}}
is a face of precisely one other chamber, which
lies on the ‘other side’ of this hyperplane. Examining
(
2
) shows that this chamber is given by

ws_{i}\mathrm{C}^{+}
, and we say
ws_{i}\mathrm{C}^{+}
is obtained from
w\mathrm{C}^{+}
by
a
simple wall crossing
. Iyama–Wemyss
[
IW
, §1]
show that while there may not be an underlying
group action on the chambers of the
\mathfrak{J}
-cone arrangement, one can navigate
between them by analogous wall-crossing combinatorics.

To explain the construction, we first note that if
w\mathrm{C}_{J}^{+}=w^{\prime}\,\mathrm{C}_{J^{\prime}}^{+}
, then we must have
J=J^{\prime}
and
w\textnormal{W}(J)=w^{\prime}\,\textnormal{W}(J^{\prime})
as
cosets. Thus for each
\sigma\in\Chambers(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})
there is a unique
subset
\mathbb{J}(\sigma)\subset\underline{\Delta}
such that
\sigma
can be
expressed as
\sigma=w\mathrm{C}^{+}_{\mathbb{J}(\sigma)}
. Further the
codimension-one faces of
\sigma
are in bijection with

\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathbb{J}(\sigma)
, where
i\in\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathbb{J}(\sigma)
determines the face

\sigma_{i}=w\mathrm{C}_{\mathbb{J}(\sigma)+i}
. We then have the following.

Lemma 2.7

(
[
IW
, lemma 1.23]
)
.

Given
\sigma\in\Chambers(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})
and

i\in\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathbb{J}(\sigma)
, there is a unique cone

\nu_{i}\sigma\in\Chambers(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})
which satisfies

\nu_{i}\sigma\cap\sigma=\sigma_{i}
, which we call the
simple wall
crossing of
\sigma
at
i
. Explicitly, if

\sigma=w\mathrm{C}^{+}_{J}
then this cone is given by

\nu_{i}\cdot w\mathrm{C}_{J}^{+}=w\cdot w_{J}w_{J+i}\cdot\mathrm{C}^{+}_{\nu_{i}J}.

This gives
\Chambers(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})
the structure of a set with

\underline{\Delta}
-mutation, i.e. we have

\mathbb{J}(\nu_{i}\sigma)=\nu_{i}\mathbb{J}(\sigma)
and

\nu_{\iota(i)}\nu_{i}\sigma=\sigma
. Moreover, this set has a connected
exchange quiver (i.e. any two chambers in
\Chambers(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})
are
connected by a finite sequence of simple wall crossings.)

Analogously,
\Chambers(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})
equipped with the data of simple wall
crossings is a set with
\Delta
-mutation, and this has a finite and
connected exchange quiver.

\nu_{4}\nu_{1}\nu_{0}\mathrm{C}_{J}^{+}
\nu_{4}\nu_{1}\nu_{0}\mathrm{C}_{J}^{+}
\nu_{4}\nu_{0}\nu_{1}\mathrm{C}_{J}^{+}

Figure 6:
Continuing
fig.

5
, some wall crossings in the

\mathcal{E}_{7,4}
Tits cone are indicated. Within
each chamber
\sigma
, the walls are labelled by indices
i\in\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathbb{J}(\sigma)
such that crossing the

i^{\text{th}}
wall lands in the adjacent chamber
\nu_{i}\sigma
.

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}{}

If we write
\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}=\mathrm{C}^{0}\cap\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
(even when
\mathfrak{J}
does not lie in
\Delta
),
we see that every
\sigma\in\Chambers(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})
can be written as

\nu\mathrm{C}^{0}_{\mathfrak{J}}
for some spherical

\mathbb{J}\left(\mathrm{C}^{0}_{\mathfrak{J}}\right)
-path
\nu
.
It follows that if
\mathfrak{J}\subset\Delta
, then the
\mathfrak{J}
-cone arrangement

\Arr(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})
can be explicitly described as

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline qn:intersectiondecomposition}\underbrace{\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\text{ an $\mathfrak{J}$-path}\\
\phantom{\mathfrak{J}}\end{subarray}}\faces\left(\nu C_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}\right)}_{\Arr^{+}(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})}\quad\cup\;\underbrace{\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\text{ a
spherical}\\
\text{$\mathfrak{J}$-path}\end{subarray}}\faces\left(\nu C_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}\right)}_{\Arr(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})}\quad\cup\;\underbrace{\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\text{ an $\mathfrak{J}$-path}\\
\phantom{\mathfrak{J}}\end{subarray}}\faces\left(\nu C_{\mathfrak{J}}^{-}\right)}_{\Arr^{-}(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})}.

(5)

The weak order on chambers

Fixing the base chamber
\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}

in the
\mathfrak{J}
-cone arrangement endows the set of chambers

\Chambers(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})
with a partial order, called the
weak order
,
where two chambers
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}
satisfy
\sigma\geq\sigma^{\prime}
if and only
if every hyperplane separating
\sigma
from
\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
also separates

\sigma^{\prime}
from
\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
. Evidently the poset then has a unique
greatest element,
\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
.

The following lemma summarises key properties
of this partial order, following
[
IW
,
Sal87
]
.

Lemma 2.8
.

Fix a chamber
\sigma\in\Chambers(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})
and a positive

\mathbb{J}(\sigma)
-path
\nu=\nu_{i_{n}},...,\nu_{i_{1}}
.

1.

If
\nu
has length
1
, then one of
\sigma<\nu\sigma
or
\sigma>\nu\sigma
must hold. Moreover, the
relation is covering, i.e. there is no chamber
\sigma^{\prime}

satisfying
\sigma>\sigma^{\prime}>\nu\sigma
(or

\nu\sigma>\sigma^{\prime}>\sigma
).

2.

If
\sigma\geq\nu\sigma
, then the path
\nu
is

minimal
(i.e. has minimal length among all positive paths
from
\sigma
to
\nu\sigma
) if and only if it is

reduced
(i.e. crosses each hyperplane at most once), if and
only if it is
atomic
(i.e. successive truncations of
\nu

give a monotone sequence
\sigma>\nu_{i_{1}}\sigma>\nu_{i_{2}}\nu_{i_{1}}...>\nu\sigma
).

3.

If
\sigma\geq\nu\sigma
, then the interval

[\nu\sigma,\sigma]
contains finitely many elements.

Now any two chambers in a locally finite hyperplane arrangement can be connected
by a reduced path. It follows that every relation in the poset

\Chambers(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{I})
is realised by the Hasse quiver, i.e. we have

\sigma<\sigma^{\prime}
if and only if there is a chain of covering relations

\sigma=\sigma^{0}\lessdot...\lessdot\sigma^{n}=\sigma^{\prime}
. This also shows that
every covering relation in the poset must arise from simple wall crossing, so
that the Hasse quiver is a subquiver of the exchange quiver in the obvious way
and a positive path is atomic if and only if it lies in the Hasse quiver.

Analogous statements hold for
\Chambers(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})
, with maximal
chamber
\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}
.

§ 3 Algebra, geometry, and everything in between

As in the introduction, let
Z=\Spec R
be a complete local isolated
compound du Val (cDV) singularity, equivalently the spectrum of a
three-dimensional complete local Gorenstein
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
R
such
that the unique singularity at the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
is at worst
terminal. In particular,
R
is a normal domain.

A
flopping contraction
over
Z

[
KM98
, definition
6.10]
is a projective birational
morphism
\pi\colon X\to Z
such that
X
is normal, the exceptional locus is
codimension
2
, and the canonical divisor on
X
is numerically
\pi
-trivial (equivalently

\pi^{\ast}\omega_{Z}=\omega_{X}
, i.e.
\pi
is
crepant
).

Fixing such a flopping contraction
\pi\colon X\to Z
we look at the
triangulated category
\Dm X
containing complexes of coherent sheaves
supported within
\pi^{-1}[\mathfrak{m}]
, and seek to examine
hearts of (bounded)
t-structures
on
\Dm X
—an example being
\coh X=\Coh X\cap\Dm X
.
More generally any Abelian category
H
is naturally the heart of a
t-structure inside its bounded derived category
\Db H
.

Thus in this section
we enumerate derived equivalences of
X
with a multitude of algebras,
varieties, and more generally, non-commutative schemes, thereby enumerating
hearts in
\Dm X
.

§ 3.1 Van den Bergh’s equivalence

By Kawamata’s
vanishing theorem, the map
\pi
is acyclic i.e.
\mathbf{R}\pi_{\ast}{O}_{X}={O}_{Z}
and thus the exceptional fiber
\underline{C}=\pi^{-1}[\mathfrak{m}]

is such that the reduced subscheme
C=\underline{C}_{\text{red}}
is a finite
collection of rational curves
[
Van04
, lemma
3.4.1]
.
In this context, for each integral exceptional curve
C_{i}\subset C
Van den
Bergh
[
Van04
, §3.5]
gives
the construction of a distinguished indecomposable vector bundle
{N}_{i}
on
X
,
such that the bundle
{V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}={O}_{X}\oplus\bigoplus_{i}{N}_{i}
is tilting.
Consequently
X
is derived equivalent to the basic
R
-algebra
\Lambda=\End_{X}{V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
via the functor

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline qn:zeroperforpi}\textnormal{VdB}\colon\Db\Lambda\xrightarrow{\hskip 7.97224pt(-)\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}_{\Lambda}{V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}\hskip 7.97224pt}\Db X.

(6)

This equivalence maps the natural heart
\mathcal{m\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}\Lambda\subset\Db\Lambda
to
the
category of perverse sheaves

\zeroPer\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}=\text{VdB}(\mathcal{m\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}\Lambda)
, which
should be thought of as the ‘standard’ heart in
\Db X
owing to its amiable
cohomological properties. By
[
Van04
, corollary
3.2.8]
,

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline qn:zeroperdefn}\zeroPer\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}=\left\{x\in\Coh X[0,1]\;\middle|\;\begin{array}[]{l}\mathbf{R}^{1}\pi_{\ast}(\textnormal{H}^{0}x)=0,\quad\pi_{\ast}(\textnormal{H}^{-1}x)=0,\\
\Hom(c,\textnormal{H}^{-1}x)=0\text{ for all }c\in\Coh X\\
\text{satisfying }\mathbf{R}\pi_{\ast}c=0.\end{array}\right\}.

(7)

The equivalence (
6
), being
R
-linear, identifies the
subcategory
\Dm X\subset\Db X
of complexes with cohomology supported within

C
with the subcategory
\Dfl\Lambda\subset\Db\Lambda
of complexes
whose cohomology modules have finite length over
\Lambda
(equivalently, the
thick subcategory generated by simple
\Lambda
-module.)

Definition 3.1
.

Write
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
for the subcategory
\zeroPer\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}\cap\Dm X
of
\Db X
, which under the equivalence (
6
) corresponds to the
natural heart
\mathcal{f\kern-0.5ptl\kern-1.0ptm\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}\Lambda\subset\Dfl\Lambda
of finite length

\Lambda
-modules.

Dynkin data for flopping contractions

Returning to the contraction

\pi\colon X\to Z
, the integral components of the reduced exceptional fiber
C
can be
naturally indexed over a subgraph of a Dynkin diagram by considering a generic
hyperplane section (
general elephant
)
\overline{Z}=\Spec\overline{R}\hookrightarrow\Spec R
and the pullback

\overline{X}=X\times_{Z}\overline{Z}
. Indeed by assumptions on
R
, the
variety
\overline{Z}
is the germ of a canonical surface singularity and the
map
\overline{X}\to\overline{Z}
is a crepant partial resolution which is
independent of the choice of general elephant
[
Rei
, theorem
1.14]
. In particular
\overline{X}
is obtained by
contracting a subset of exceptional curves in the minimal resolution of

\overline{Z}
. By the McKay
correspondence
[
McK80
]
, the exceptional curves
in the minimal resolution are indexed over some ADE Dynkin graph
\Delta
.
Recording the indices of the contracted curves in a subset
\mathfrak{J}\subset\Delta

shows that the integral components of
C
are naturally indexed over the
Dynkin subgraph
\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J}
and we can write the reduced exceptional
fiber as
C=\bigcup_{i\in\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J}}C_{i}
where each component
C_{i}

is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

The indexing naturally carries over to indecomposable summands of Van den Bergh’s bundle

{V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
in the following fashion.

Lemma 3.2
.

The bundle
{V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
has precisely one indecomposable summand isomorphic to

{O}_{X}
. Further, for each irreducible exceptional curve
C_{i}\subset C
(
i\in\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J}
) in
X
, the bundle
{V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
has
an indecomposable summand
{N}_{i}
such that
the closed subscheme
\textnormal{c}_{1}({N}_{i}\check{}\,)\subset X

transversely intersects
C_{i}
once, and is disjoint from
C_{j}
for
j\neq i
.

This assignment
C_{i}\mapsto{N}_{i}
is a bijection between integral

\pi
-exceptional curves in
X
and non-free indecomposable summands of

{V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
.

Proof.

This is implicit in the construction of
{V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
, see

[
Van04
, §§ 3.4 and
3.5]
.
∎

It is convenient to view
\Delta
as sitting inside the associated extended
(affine) Dynkin diagram
\underline{\Delta}
with extended vertex
0\in\underline{\Delta}\setminus\Delta
.

We reserve the index
0
for the trivial summand of
{V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
, writing

{N}_{0}={O}_{X}
. Thus indecomposable summands
{N}_{i}\subset{V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
, and hence also finitely generated indecomposable projective

\Lambda
-modules
P_{i}=\Hom_{X}\left({V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}},{N}_{i}\right)
, are
naturally indexed over
\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J}
. The indexing carries
over to simple
\Lambda
modules via the standard simple-projective duality.
By
[
Van04
, proposition 3.5.7]
,
the simple
\Lambda
-module
S_{i}
dual to
P_{i}
is given by

\textnormal{VdB}(\;S_{i}\;)=\begin{cases}\omega_{\underline{C}}[1],&i=0\\
{O}_{C_{i}}(-1),&i\in\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J}.\end{cases}

§ 3.2 Tilting equivalences

The approach we take to describing algebraic t-structures on

\Dfl\Lambda\simeq\Dm X
is via Iyama–Wemyss’s
observation
[
IW
,
IW14
]

that the tilting theory of
\Lambda
is controlled by the combinatorics of
certain reflexive
R
-modules.

Definition 3.3
.

An
R
-module
N
is said to be modifying if it is basic,
reflexive, and its endomorphism algebra
\End_{R}N
is Cohen–Macaulay.
We say
N
is an
modifying generator
if in addition it contains

R
as a direct summand (equivalently if it is Cohen–Macaulay).

The set of (isomorphism classes of) modifying
R
-modules is naturally
partitioned into
mutation classes

[
IW
, corollary
9.29]
, where two modifying modules
M,N
lie in
the same mutation class if they have the same number of indecomposable summands
and if
M
admits a two-term approximation by
\text{add}(N)
. Each mutation
class then furnishes a family of derived-equivalent algebras via the following
Auslander–McKay type correspondence.

Theorem 3.4

(
[
IW
, theorem 9.25]
)
.

If
M,N
are modifying
R
-modules in the same mutation class then

\Hom_{R}(N,M)
is a (classical) reflexive tilting
\End_{R}N
-module, and
further every reflexive tilting
\End_{R}N
-module arises in this way from
a unique module in the mutation class of
N
.

There is a natural isomorphism
\End_{\End_{R}N}(\Hom_{R}(N,M))\cong\End_{R}M

given by reflexive equivalence
[
IW14
, lemma
2.5]
. Then as is standard, the tilting
module
\Hom_{R}(N,M)
in the above setup induces quasi-inverse derived
equivalences

\Db\left(\End_{R}N\right){\mathrel{\to}^{(-)\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}\Hom_{R}(N,M)}_{\hskip 7.97224pt\RHom(\Hom_{R}(N,M),-)\hskip 7.97224pt}}\Db\left(\End_{R}M\right).\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline qn:brennerbutler}

(8)

Being
R
-linear, the functors restrict to equivalences between the categories

\Dfl(\End_{R}N)
and
\Dfl(\End_{R}M)
containing complexes with finite
length cohomology.

Mutating modifying modules

The key example of a modifying
generator is the module
\pi_{\ast}{V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
, which Cohen–Macaulay with a
Cohen–Macaulay endomorphism algebra by
[
Van04
, proposition
3.2.10]
. Given the setup of
our problem, we fix once and for all this choice
N=\pi_{\ast}{V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
, which
has endomorphism algebra
\Lambda=\End_{R}N\cong\End_{X}{V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
. Note that the
indecomposable summands of
N
are naturally indexed over

\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J}
by
lemma

3.2
.

Write
\mathop{\textnormal{}}{MM}{}^{N}R
for the mutation class of
N
, and
\mathop{\textnormal{}}{MMG}{}^{N}R\subset\mathop{\textnormal{}}{MM}{}^{N}R
for the subset of modifying generators in the mutation class.
Iyama–Wemyss’s correspondence above is complemented by the following result
which enumerates the entire mutation class of
N
.

Theorem 3.5
.

There is a bijection
\CC\colon\mathop{\textnormal{}}{MM}{}^{N}R\to\Chambers(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})
such that the following statements hold.

1.

Given
M\in\mathop{\textnormal{}}{MM}{}^{N}R
and
i\in\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathbb{J}(\CC M)
, let
M^{\prime}\in\mathop{\textnormal{}}{MM}{}^{N}R
be
such that
\CC(M^{\prime})
is the adjacent chamber
\nu_{i}\CC(M)
. Then
there is a unique indecomposable summand
M_{i}\subset M
such that

M/M_{i}
is a summand of
M^{\prime}
; this defines a bijection between

\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathbb{J}(\CC M)
and the indecomposable
summands of
M
.

2.

If
M
above is a modifying generator, then
the extended vertex
0\in\underline{\Delta}
lies outside
\mathbb{J}(\CC M)
and corresponds to the summand
M_{0}=R
.

3.

For
N=\pi_{\ast}{V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
, we have
\CC(N)=\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
and the
bijection in
1
coincides with the

(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
-indexing of summands given by

lemma

3.2
.

4.

Given
M,M^{\prime}\in\mathop{\textnormal{}}{MM}{}^{N}R
corresponding to the tilting
\Lambda
-modules

T=\Hom(N,M)
and
T^{\prime}=\Hom(N,M^{\prime})
, we have
\CC(M)\geq\CC(M^{\prime})
in the weak order on
\Chambers(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})
if and
only if
\Ext^{1}(T,T^{\prime})=0
.

Proof.

If
X
is
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial (i.e. a minimal model) then this
is
[
IW
, corollary 9.8]
. We sketch the
general argument.

By considering a general elephant
\Spec\overline{R}\hookrightarrow\Spec R
and the
corresponding reduction functor
\mathbb{F}{(-)}=(-)\otimes_{R}\overline{R}
,
Iyama–Wemyss
[
IW
, corollary 9.29]

construct the required bijection as a composite

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline qn:conesfromMMR}\mathop{\textnormal{}}{MM}{}^{N}R\xrightarrow{\;\Hom_{R}(N,-)\;}\{\text{basic ref.\ tilting $\Lambda$-modules}\}\xrightarrow{\;\mathbb{F}(-)\;}\{\text{basic tilting $\mathbb{F}\Lambda$-modules}\}\xrightarrow{\;g\text{-cone}\;}\Chambers(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})

(9)

where the final map assigns each basic tilting module over
\mathbb{F}\Lambda

(a contracted preprojective algebra) to the cone spanned by its
associated
g
-vectors. Note this requires an identification of the
vector space
\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
with

\KK_{\text{split}}(\mathcal{p\kern-0.5ptr\kern-0.5pto\kern-1.0ptj}\mathbb{F}\Lambda)
, for this we map the class of
the indecomposable projective
\mathbb{F}\Lambda_{i}=\mathbb{F}{\Hom_{R}(N,N_{i})}
to the
vector
\theta_{i}\in\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
dual to

\alpha_{i}\in\mathfrak{h}(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
.

Now the assignment
g\text{-cone}(-)
has each of the required
properties since wall-crossing is compatible with tilting mutation and

g\text{-cone}(\mathbb{F}T)\geq g\text{-cone}(\mathbb{F}T^{\prime})
in the
weak order if and only if
\Ext^{1}(\mathbb{F}T,\mathbb{F}T^{\prime})=0

[
IW18
, theorem 5.2]
.

Since
\Hom_{R}(N,-)
and
\mathbb{F}(-)
both preserve
indecomposability, and since
\mathbb{F}
preserves the tilting order

[
Kim24
, proposition 4.2]
, it follows that
the composite
\CC(-)=g\text{-cone}(\mathbb{F}\Hom(N,-))
has the
required properties too.
∎

The upshot is that
\mathop{\textnormal{}}{MM}{}^{N}R
inherits both—a partial order (where

M\geq M^{\prime}
if and only if
\CC(M)\geq\CC(M^{\prime})
in the weak order), and the
structure of a set with
\underline{\Delta}
-mutation (with exchange quiver isomorphic to that of

\Chambers(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})
). In particular for
M,M^{\prime}\in\mathop{\textnormal{}}{MM}{}^{N}R
there
exists a positive
\mathbb{J}(M)
-path
\nu
such that

M^{\prime}=\nu M
, and further if
M
and
M^{\prime}
are modifying generators then this
path
\nu
can be chosen to be spherical. Thus we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline qn:mm-via-paths}\mathop{\textnormal{}}{MM}{}^{N}R=\{\nu N\;|\;\nu\text{ a $\mathfrak{J}$-path}\},\quad\mathop{\textnormal{}}{MMG}{}^{N}R=\{\nu N\;|\;\nu\text{ a spherical $\mathfrak{J}$-path}\}.

(10)

This extends to an enumeration of a large class of algebras and their tilting
modules. Given two positive
\mathfrak{J}
-paths
\nu,\mu
we write

{}_{\mu}\Lambda_{\nu}=\Hom_{R}(\nu N,\mu N)

and note that this is a reflexive tilting
\End_{R}(\nu N)
-module
by
theorem

3.4
. We omit the empty path
\emptyset
from the
notation, so e.g.
\Lambda_{\nu}=\Hom_{R}(\nu N,N)
and

{}_{\nu}\Lambda=\Hom_{R}(N,\nu N)
. Happily, this is consistent with

\Lambda=\Hom_{R}(N,N)
.

Then each module
\nu N\in\mathop{\textnormal{}}{MM}{}^{N}R
can be used to obtain two equivalences
to
\Dfl\Lambda
. Indeed the constructions in (
8
)
are symmetric in the input data so we obtain a tilting
\Lambda
-module

{}_{\nu}\Lambda
and a tilting
{}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\nu}
-module
\Lambda_{\nu}
,
which in turn give two
mutation functors

\Dfl{}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\nu}\rightrightarrows\Dfl\Lambda
defined as

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline qn:twomutationfunctors}\Phi_{\nu}(-)=(-)\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}{}_{\nu}\Lambda,\qquad\Psi_{\nu}(-)=\RHom(\Lambda_{\nu},-).

(11)

Composing mutation functors

Of course for the purpose of defining mutation functors there is nothing special
about the choice of
N
and
\Lambda=\End_{R}N
, and we can define mutation
functors starting at
\Dfl(\End_{R}M)
for any modifying module
M
. That is
to say if
M=\nu N
for some positive
\mathfrak{J}
-path
\nu
, and if
\mu
is
a positive
\nu\mathfrak{J}
-path, then the reflexive tilting modules

{}_{\mu\nu}\Lambda_{\nu}\in\mathcal{m\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}({}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\nu})
and

{}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\mu\nu}\in\mathcal{m\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}({}_{\mu\nu}\Lambda_{\mu\nu})

define two functors

(\;\text{-}\;)\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}{}_{\mu\nu}\Lambda_{\nu},\quad\RHom({}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\mu\nu},\;\text{-}\;)\colon\Dfl{}_{\mu\nu}\Lambda_{\mu\nu}\rightrightarrows\Dfl{}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\nu}

which we again denote by
\Phi_{\mu}(\;\text{-}\;)
and

\Psi_{\mu}(\;\text{-}\;)
respectively.

The following lemmas record composition relations between such functors. It will
suffice to consider functors corresponding to paths of length
1
, and for
simplicity of notation we write
\Phi_{i}
(resp.
\Psi_{i}
) for the functor

\Phi_{\nu_{i}}
(resp.
\Psi_{\nu_{i}}
).

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
M=\nu N
be a modifying module determined by
\mathfrak{J}
-path
\nu
, and
fix a vertex
i\in\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathbb{J}(M)
.

1.

We have natural isomorphisms
\Phi_{i}\circ\Psi_{\iota(i)}\simeq\Psi_{i}\circ\Phi_{\iota(i)}\simeq\textnormal{id}
of functors

\Dfl{}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\nu}\to\Dfl{}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\nu}
.

2.

If
M>\nu_{i}M
, we have natural isomorphisms

\Phi_{\nu_{i}\nu}\simeq\Phi_{\nu}\circ\Phi_{i}
and

\Psi_{\nu_{i}\nu}\simeq\Psi_{\nu}\circ\Psi_{i}
of functors

\Dfl{}_{\nu_{i}\nu}\Lambda_{\nu_{i}\nu}\to\Dfl\Lambda
.

Proof.

The first statement is immediate from definitions. For the second, if

\nu N>\nu_{i}\nu N
in
\mathop{\textnormal{}}{MM}{}^{N}R
, then by
[
HW18
, theorem
4.6]
there is an isomorphism of

{}_{\nu_{i}\nu}\Lambda_{\nu_{i}\nu}-\Lambda
bimodules

{}_{\nu_{i}\nu}\Lambda_{\nu}\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}{}_{\nu}\Lambda\simeq{}_{\nu_{i}\nu}\Lambda
(where the tensor product is over

{}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\nu}
). The isomorphism
\Psi_{\nu_{i}\nu}\simeq\Psi_{\nu}\circ\Psi_{i}
is then immediate, while the isomorphism

\Phi_{\nu_{i}\nu}\simeq\Phi_{\nu}\circ\Phi_{i}
follows from the

\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}
–
\RHom
adjunction
[
IR08
, lemma
2.10]
.
∎

Lemma 3.7
.

Given a positive
\mathfrak{J}
-path
\nu=\nu_{i_{n}}...\nu_{i_{1}}
, we
can write
\Phi_{\nu}=\Upsilon_{{i_{1}}}\circ\Upsilon_{{i_{2}}}\circ...\circ\Upsilon_{{i_{n}}}
for some sequence of simple mutations

\Upsilon_{i}\in\{\Phi_{i},\Psi_{i}\}
. Further if the path
\nu
is
atomic (i.e.
N>\nu_{i_{1}}N>\nu_{i_{2}}\nu_{i_{1}}N>...>\nu N
), then
each
\Upsilon_{i}
above is of the form
\Phi_{i}
and we have
\Phi_{\nu}=\Phi_{i_{1}}\circ\Phi_{i_{2}}\circ...\circ\Phi_{i_{n}}
.

The analogous statement holds for
\Psi_{\nu}
.

Proof.

For
n\geq 1
we may write
\nu=\nu_{i}\nu^{\prime}
where
i=i_{n}

and
\nu^{\prime}=\nu_{i_{n-1}}...\nu_{i_{1}}
is a shorter path. By

lemma

2.8
we have either
\nu^{\prime}N>\nu N
or

\nu N>\nu^{\prime}N
. In the first case

lemma

3.6
gives us
