\Phi_{\nu}=\Phi_{\nu^{\prime}}\circ\Phi_{i}
,
while in the latter case we write
\nu^{\prime}N=\nu_{\iota(i)}\nu N
to get

\Phi_{\nu}=\Phi_{\nu^{\prime}}\circ(\Phi_{\iota(i)})^{-1}=\Phi_{\nu^{\prime}}\circ\Psi_{i}
. The result follows by induction.
∎

§ 3.3 Birational models

Geometric t-structures naturally arise from birational
modifications of
X
, where surgery operations described below replace
exceptional curves in a way that preserves the derived category.

Theorem 3.8

(
[
Che02
, theorem 1.1]
)
.

Suppose
Y
is a normal 3-fold with at worst terminal Gorenstein
singularities, and the maps
\tau\colon X\to Y
,
\tau^{\prime}\colon W\to Y
are
flopping contractions over
Y
such that whenever
D
is a
\tau
-nef
divisor on
X
, the proper transform of
-D
across the birational map

X\dashrightarrow W
is
\mathbb{Q}
-cartier and
\tau^{\prime}
-nef. Then
there is a Fourier–Mukai type equivalence of derived categories
\Db W\to\Db X
.

A map
\tau^{\prime}
satisfying the given properties, if it exists, is unique and is
called the
flop
of
\tau
. We say the equivalence
\Db W\to\Db X
is
the
Bridgeland–Chen flop functor
.

Remark 3.9
.

There are various equivalent definitions of a flop in the literature, see
for example
[
Kol90
]
. Given
a flopping contraction
\tau:X\to Y
between normal 3-folds, one typically
needs to choose a
\mathbb{Q}
-cartier
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor
D
on

X
such that
-D
is
\tau
-ample in order to define the
D
-flop
(i.e. the
(K_{X}+D)
-flip) of
\tau
, which is unique and independent of
the choice of
D
by
[
KM98
, corollary
6.4]
. In the special case that
X

(equivalently
Y
) is terminal, the existence of the flop is proved
in theorem 6.14
ibid.

Now the flopping contraction
\pi:X\to Z
of interest to us is over a complete
local base, and therefore we can freely contract exceptional curves
[
Sch01
, §2]
. That is to say, given any collection of
exceptional curves
C_{I}=\bigcup_{i\in I}C_{i}\subset X
, the map
\pi
admits
a unique factorisation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varpi}

(12)

where
\tau
contracts
C_{i}
to a point if and only if
i\in I
. We say

\tau
is a
partial contraction over
Z
. Since the singularity on

Z
is assumed to be isolated, it follows that both
\tau
and
\varpi

are flopping contractions. In particular we can consider the flop
\tau^{\prime}\colon W\to Y
of
\tau
to obtain a derived equivalence
\Db W\to\Db X
; further the map

\varpi\circ\tau^{\prime}\colon W\to Z
is itself a flopping contraction over
Z
so
we can iterate.

Wemyss’s homological minimal model programme

[
Wem18
]
addresses the question of classifying

birational models
of
X
, i.e. varieties
W
obtained by iteratively
flopping subsets of
\pi
-exceptional curves. Abusing notation to write

\pi\colon W\to Z
for the corresponding flopping contraction, we can again consider
the associated vector bundle
{V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Z}\right)$}}
, the modifying
R
-module generator

M=\pi_{\ast}{V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Z}\right)$}}
, and the equivalence

\text{VdB}\colon\Db(\End M)\rightarrow\Db W

constructed analogously to (
6
). In
this context, Wemyss shows that flops obey mutation-combinatorics following the
modifying generators.

Theorem 3.10

(
[
Wem18
]
)
.

If
\pi\colon W\to Z
is a birational model of
\pi\colon X\to Z
, then the
associated modifying
R
-module generators
M=\pi_{\ast}{V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Z}\right)$}}
and

N=\pi_{\ast}{V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
lie in the same mutation class, and every
module in
\mathop{\textnormal{}}{MMG}{}^{N}R
arises in this way from a unique birational model
of
X
.

Thus the set
\mathop{\textnormal{}}{Bir}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
of birational models of
X
is in bijection with

\mathop{\textnormal{}}{MMG}{}^{N}(R)
via the map
W\mapsto\pi_{\ast}{V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Z}\right)$}}
.
Since
theorem

3.5
shows
\mathop{\textnormal{}}{MMG}{}^{N}R
is a set with

\Delta
-mutation,
\mathop{\textnormal{}}{Bir}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
inherits this structure. That is to say for any

W\in\mathop{\textnormal{}}{Bir}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
with associated modifying generator
\pi_{\ast}{V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Z}\right)$}}=M
we
have
\mathbb{J}(W)=\mathbb{J}(M)
, and for any
\mathbb{J}(W)
-path
\nu

the birational model
\nu W
is the unique element of
\mathop{\textnormal{}}{Bir}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
satisfying

\pi_{\ast}{V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{\nu W}{Z}\right)$}}=\nu M
.

This mutation structure on
\mathop{\textnormal{}}{Bir}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
can be realised intrinsically as arising
from flops—the indecomposable summands of

M
are indexed over
\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathbb{J}(M)
in a way that
applying
\nu_{i}
corresponds to mutating the
i
th summand, and the only
free summand is
M_{0}=R
. It follows that the non-free indecomposable
summands of
{V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Z}\right)$}}
are indexed over
\Delta\setminus\mathbb{J}(W)
. By

lemma

3.2
these summands are naturally in bijection
with the integral exceptional curves of
W
. Therefore we see that the reduced
exceptional fiber of
\pi\colon W\to Z
can be written as a union of integral
curves
\bigcup_{i\in\Delta\setminus\mathbb{J}(W)}C_{i}
. The following result
of Wemyss realises the operation
\nu_{i}
on
\mathop{\textnormal{}}{Bir}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
as a flop of the

i
th curve.

Theorem 3.11

(
[
Wem18
, theorem 4.2]
)
.

Fix
i\in\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J}
, and suppose
\nu_{i}X=W\to Z
is the
birational model of
X
corresponding to the modifying
R
-module
generator
M=\nu_{i}N
with reduced exceptional fiber

\bigcup_{i\in\Delta\setminus\nu_{i}\mathfrak{J}}C_{i}
indexed as above. Then
W

is precisely the flop of
X
in the curve
C_{i}
, and

C_{\iota(i)}\subset W
is the proper transform of
C_{i}\subset X
.
Further, in this case the Bridgeland–Chen flop functor
\Db W\to\Db X

coincides with the composite equivalence

\Db W\xrightarrow{\hskip 7.97224pt\textnormal{VdB}^{-1}\hskip 7.97224pt}\Db(\End M)\xrightarrow{\hskip 7.97224pt\Psi_{i}(-)\hskip 7.97224pt}\Db(\End N)\xrightarrow{\hskip 7.97224pt\textnormal{VdB}\hskip 7.97224pt}\Db X,

and restricts to an equivalence
\Dm W\to\Dm X
between the full
subcategories of complexes supported on exceptional curves.

In what follows, we omit
VdB
from notation whenever convenient,
thus for example writing
\Psi_{i}
for both the mutation equivalence and
the flop functor in the above theorem.

By examining the connectedness of
\ExQuiv(\mathop{\textnormal{}}{MMG}{}^{N}R)
we also conclude
that any two birational models of
X
are connected by a chain of
single-curve flops, i.e. given birational models
W,W^{\prime}\in\mathop{\textnormal{}}{Bir}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
there is a
\mathbb{J}(W)
-path

\nu=\nu_{i_{n}}\ldots\nu_{i_{1}}
and a
sequence of flops

W\;\dashrightarrow\;\nu_{i_{1}}W\;\dashrightarrow\;\nu_{i_{2}}\nu_{i_{1}}W\;\dashrightarrow\;\cdots\;\dashrightarrow\;\nu_{i_{n}}\ldots\nu_{i_{1}}W=W^{\prime}.

Extending the notion of Bridgeland–Chen functors to chains of flops, in this
case we say the
flop functor
associated to
\nu
is the equivalence

\Db\nu W\xrightarrow{\hskip 7.97224pt\textnormal{VdB}^{-1}\hskip 7.97224pt}\Db(\End\nu M)\xrightarrow{\hskip 7.97224pt\Psi_{\nu}(-)\hskip 7.97224pt}\Db(\End M)\xrightarrow{\hskip 7.97224pt\textnormal{VdB}\hskip 7.97224pt}\Db W

which is evidently independent of the choice of
\nu
.

§ 3.4 Semi-geometric categories

When the flopping contraction
\pi\colon X\to Z
is not irreducible (i.e. the
reduced exceptional fiber has multiple components), there are intermediate partial
contractions
X\to Y\to Z
to be considered. In this subsection we establish
basic structural results about perverse sheaves arising from such morphisms.

Thus fix a subset
I\subset\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J}
, equivalently a collection of
exceptional curves
C_{I}=\bigcup_{i\in I}C_{i}
in
X
which can be
contracted via the map
\tau\colon X\to Y
as in (
12
). As
noted previously,
\tau
is a flopping contraction and in particular

[
Van04
, theorem A]
applies.
Thus there is a vector bundle
{V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
on
X
, furnishing a sheaf of

{O}_{Y}
-algebras
\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{${V}$}=\tau_{\ast}\sheafEnd{V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
such that there is a
derived equivalence

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline qn:semigeomperverseequivalence}\textnormal{VdB}\colon\Db\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{${V}$}\xrightarrow{\hskip 7.97224pt\tau^{-1}(-)\;\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}\;{V}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}\hskip 7.97224pt}\Db X

(13)

where the tensor product is over
\tau^{-1}\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{${V}$}
. We write

\zeroPer\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
for the image of
\Coh\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{${V}$}
under
this equivalence. By
[
Van04
, proposition
3.3.1]
, this category admits
the description

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline qn:zeroperdefn1}\zeroPer\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}=\left\{x\in\Coh X[0,1]\;\middle|\;\begin{array}[]{l}\mathbf{R}^{1}\tau_{\ast}(\textnormal{H}^{0}x)=0,\quad\tau_{\ast}(\textnormal{H}^{-1}x)=0,\\
\Hom(c,\textnormal{H}^{-1}x)=0\text{ for all }c\in\Coh X\\
\text{satisfying }\mathbf{R}\tau_{\ast}c=0.\end{array}\right\}.

(14)

Since we are concerned with complexes supported on exceptional fibers,
we also define the full subcategories

\displaystyle\Dm\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{${V}$}=\left\{y\in\Db\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{${V}$}\;|\;\mathop{\text{Supp}}y\subseteq\varpi^{-1}[\mathfrak{m}]\right\},

\displaystyle\coh\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{${V}$}=\Coh\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{${V}$}\;\cap\;\Dm\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{${V}$},\qquad\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}=\zeroPer\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}\;\cap\;\Dm X.

The functor
VdB
clearly restricts to an equivalence

\Dm\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{${V}$}\to\Dm X
, thus identifying
\coh\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{${V}$}
with

\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
. The truncation functors associated to the heart
\Coh\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{${V}$}\subset\Db\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{${V}$}
restrict to
\Dm\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{${V}$}
,
showing that
\coh\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{${V}$}\subset\Dm\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{${V}$}
(and hence

\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}\subset\Dm X
) is the heart of a bounded t-structure.

Remark 3.12
.

The following calibration is helpful—when
I=\emptyset
the map
\tau

is an isomorphism and we have
{V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}=\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{${V}$}={O}_{X}
, so that

\zeroPer\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}=\Coh\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{${V}$}=\Coh X
. In this case we have
\Dm\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{${V}$}=\Dm X
by definition. On the other extreme, when

I=\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J}
the map
\varpi
is an isomorphism and the sheaf

{V}

on
Y=\Spec R
corresponds to the
R
-algebra
\Lambda
. Since

(R,\mathfrak{m})
is complete local, a complex of
R
-modules has
support in
[\mathfrak{m}]
if and only if its total cohomology
has finite length, i.e.
\Dm\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{${V}$}=\Dfl\Lambda
.

Characterising perversity

We now show that the ‘algebraic part’ of

\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}\subset\Dm X
is generated by finitely many simple perverse
sheaves which depend only on a neighbourhood of the contracted curves, and
further can be read off as a subcategory of
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
. For simplicity we
first consider the case when
C_{I}\subset X
is connected, i.e.
\tau
is an
isomorphism away from a point
p=\tau(C_{I})\in Y
. Write
\underline{C}_{I}=\tau^{-1}(p)
for the scheme theoretic exceptional fiber of
\tau
, this
has underlying reduced subscheme
C_{I}
. Then we have the following.

Theorem 3.13
.

Let
C_{I}\subset X
be a connected component of the reduced exceptional
fiber in
X
, and
\tau:X\to Y
the crepant contraction of
C_{I}
. For
any complex of coherent sheaves
x\in\Dm X
supported within
C_{I}
, the
following are equivalent.

1.

The complex
x
lies in
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
, i.e.
x
is perverse
with respect to the contraction
\pi\colon X\to Z
.

2.

The complex
x
lies in
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
, i.e.
x

is perverse with respect to the contraction
\tau\colon X\to Y
.

3.

The complex
x
is filtered by the sheaves
{O}_{C_{i}}(-1)
for

i\in I
and the complex
\omega_{\underline{C}_{I}}[1]
(the
suspended canonical sheaf of scheme-theoretic exceptional fiber of

\tau
).

To prove this, we first establish the following lemma which reduces
conditions involving the null-category
\{c\in\Coh X\;|\;\mathbf{R}\tau_{\ast}c=0\}
(such as those appearing in the descriptions (
7
),
(
14
)) to checks on a finite collection of objects.

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
\tau:X\to Y
be the crepant contraction of
C_{I}
and
c\in\Coh X
be such that
\mathbf{R}\tau_{\ast}c=0
. Then
c
is filtered by the
sheaves
{O}_{C_{i}}(-1)
for
i\in I
.

Proof.

Note if
c
is as given, then we have
\mathbf{R}\pi_{\ast}c=0
and
hence
c\in\zeroPer\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
from the description (
7
).
Further
c
is supported within
\pi^{-1}[\mathfrak{m}]
. Thus
across the equivalence (
6
), we see that
c

thus corresponds to some finite length
\Lambda
-module (which we
again denote by
c
). In particular
c
is filtered by the simples
of
H=\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
and the problem is reduced to showing that the only
simples which can occur in a composition series for
c
are the

S_{i}
for
i\in I
.

But by
[
Wem18
, theorem 2.15]
,

\Lambda
can be expressed as the quotient of the path algebra of a
quiver with vertex set
\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J}
, and the simple
representation supported on vertex
i
is precisely the module

S_{i}
. Further writing
e_{i}\in\Lambda
for the vertex idempotent at

i\in\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J}
,
[
Wem18
, proposition
2.14]
shows that a

\Lambda
-module
x
is annihilated by
\sum_{i\notin I}e_{i}
if
and only if the corresponding complex
x\in\zeroPer\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
satisfies

\mathbf{R}\tau_{\ast}x=0
. In particular, the
\Lambda
-module
c

is annihilated by
\sum_{i\notin I}e_{i}
, and hence is filtered only by
the vertex simples
S_{i}
for
i\in I
as required.
∎

We then have the following.

Proof of
theorem

3.13
,

1
\iff
2
.

Let
x
be a complex of coherent sheaves with support in
C_{I}
, and for
brevity write
x_{i}=\textnormal{H}^{-i}(x)
for its cohomology sheaves.
Since the objects of both
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
and
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
can be
described as two-term complexes of coherent sheaves on
X
, we can assume

x_{i}=0
unless
i=0,1
.

If
x\in\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
, then by the description
(
14
) we see that
\tau_{\ast}(x_{1})=0
and hence

\pi_{\ast}(x_{1})=0
.

Likewise we have
\Hom({O}_{C_{j}}(-1),x_{1})=0
whenever
C_{j}
is
contracted by
\tau
, because
\mathbf{R}\tau_{\ast}{O}_{C_{j}}(-1)=0
.
On the other hand if
C_{j}
is not contracted by
\tau
and we have a
non-zero morphism
f:{O}_{C_{j}}(-1)\to x_{1}
, then the image
\img f

is a non-zero subsheaf of
x_{1}
supported within the finite collection
of points
C_{j}\cap(\bigcup_{i\in I}C_{i})
. But in that case,

\pi_{\ast}(\img f)
is a non-zero subsheaf of
\pi_{\ast}(x_{1})=0
, a
contradiction. Thus in fact
\Hom({O}_{C_{j}}(-1),x_{1})=0
for all
exceptional curves
C_{j}
, and hence by
lemma

3.14
we
have
\Hom(c,x_{1})=0
whenever
c\in\Coh X
satisfies

\mathbf{R}\pi_{\ast}c=0
.

Lastly, examining the Leray spectral sequence

\mathbf{R}^{p}\varpi_{\ast}\circ\mathbf{R}^{q}\tau_{\ast}(x_{0})\Rightarrow\mathbf{R}^{p+q}\pi_{\ast}(x_{0})

(which degenerates since all maps have fiber dimension
\leq 1
) shows
that
\mathbf{R}^{1}\pi_{\ast}(x_{0})
is filtered by

\varpi_{\ast}\circ\mathbf{R}^{1}\tau_{\ast}(x_{0})
(
=0
since

\mathbf{R}^{1}\tau_{\ast}(x_{0})
vanishes) and

\mathbf{R}^{1}\varpi_{\ast}\circ\tau_{\ast}(x_{0})
(
=0
since

\tau_{\ast}(x_{0})
is supported on a zero-dimensional subset of
Y
).
Thus we have
\mathbf{R}^{1}\pi_{\ast}(x_{0})=0
as well, and hence
x\in\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
.

Conversely if
x\in\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
, we see that
\tau_{\ast}(x_{1})
is a
coherent sheaf on
Y
with zero-dimensional support such that
\varpi_{\ast}\circ\tau_{\ast}(x_{1})=\pi_{\ast}(x_{1})=0
. This is possible only if

\tau_{\ast}(x_{1})=0
. Likewise since

\mathbf{R}^{1}\pi_{\ast}(x_{0})=0
, the Leray sequence shows we have

\varpi_{\ast}\circ\mathbf{R}^{1}\tau_{\ast}(x_{0})=0
and hence

\mathbf{R}^{1}\tau_{\ast}(x_{0})=0
. Lastly if
c\in\Coh X
is such that

\mathbf{R}\tau_{\ast}c=0
then we have

\mathbf{R}\pi_{\ast}c=\mathbf{R}\varpi_{\ast}\circ\mathbf{R}\tau_{\ast}c=0
, and hence
\Hom(c,x_{1})=0
. This shows
x\in\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
.
∎

To prove
2
\iff
3
, we
reduce the problem to a formal neighbourhood
Z_{I}\hookrightarrow Y
of
p
, i.e. the spectrum of the complete local ring
\widehat{{O}}_{Y,p}
. The scheme

Z_{I}
has an isolated cDV singularity at its closed point, and the restriction
of
\tau
is a flopping contraction
X_{I}\to Z_{I}
with reduced exceptional
fiber
C_{I}\subset X_{I}
. We can therefore consider the full subcategory
\Dm X_{I}\subset\Db X_{I}
of complexes supported on
C_{I}
.

Lemma 3.15
.

The restriction (i.e. pullback) functor
\Dm X\to\Dm X_{I}
gives an
equivalence of
\Dm X_{I}
with the full subcategory of
complexes in
\Dm X
supported within
C_{I}
.

Proof.

The schemes
X
and
X_{I}
have isomorphic completions along
C_{I}
,
and we write
\mathfrak{X}
for the associated Noetherian formal
scheme. Now
[
Orl11
, lemma 2.1]
shows
that the category
\Dm X_{I}
is the bounded derived category of the
Abelian category

\coh(X_{I})=\{x\in\Coh X_{I}\;|\;\Supp(x)\subset C_{I}\},

whilst the subcategory of
\Dm X
containing complexes supported
within
C_{I}
is the bounded derived category of

\Coh_{C_{I}}(X)=\{x\in\Coh X\;|\;\Supp(x)\subset C_{I}\}.

But proposition 2.8
ibid
. shows
that both categories above are equivalent (via pullback along
the canonical map associated to completion) to the category of torsion coherent
sheaves on
\mathfrak{X}
. In particular, the restriction functor

\Coh_{C_{I}}(X)\to\coh(X_{I})
is an exact equivalence and passing to bounded
derived categories gives the result.
∎

Thus we can identify
\Dm X_{I}
and its subcategories
\coh X_{I}
,

\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X_{I}}{Z_{I}}\right)$}}
with their images in
\Dm X
. In particular,

\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X_{I}}{Z_{I}}\right)$}}
(as a subcategory of
\Dm X
) is an algebraic
Abelian category equal to the extension-closure of its simple objects

\omega_{\underline{C}_{I}}[1]
,
{O}_{C_{i}}(-1)
for
i\in I
. The following is then
immediate.

Proof of
theorem

3.13
,

2
\iff
3
.

By
[
Van04
, proposition
3.1.4]
, the membership
of any complex in
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
can be checked locally with respect to the
flat topology on
Y
, in particular with respect to the flat cover

Y=(Y\setminus\{p\})\cup Z_{I}
. Now if
x
is supported within contracted
curves, the restriction of
x
to
Y\setminus\{p\}
vanishes and thus

x
lies in
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
if and only if its restriction to
X_{I}

(i.e.
x
viewed as an object of
\Dm X_{I}
) lies in

\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X_{I}}{Z_{I}}\right)$}}
, equivalently if
x
is filtered by the
simples of
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X_{I}}{Z_{I}}\right)$}}
.
∎

It is straightforward to generalise this to partial
contractions
\tau\colon X\to Y
associated to possibly disconnected collections of
exceptional curves
C_{I}\subset X
, since any complex in
\Dm X
decomposes
into a direct sum of complexes with connected support. In particular we can
define the category

\mathcal{p\kern-0.5pte\kern-0.5ptr\kern-1.0pt}_{I}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}=\left\{x\in\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}\;\middle|\;\Supp x\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in I}C_{i}\right\}

and note that if
C_{I}
has connected components
C_{I_{1}},...,C_{I_{K}}
, then this decomposes as

\mathcal{p\kern-0.5pte\kern-0.5ptr\kern-1.0pt}_{I}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}=\bigoplus_{J\in\{I_{1},...,I_{k}\}}\;\;\mathcal{p\kern-0.5pte\kern-0.5ptr\kern-1.0pt}_{J}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}.

But following
theorem

3.13
, each
\mathcal{p\kern-0.5pte\kern-0.5ptr\kern-1.0pt}_{J}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
is
equivalent to the category
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X_{J}}{Z_{J}}\right)$}}
associated to a formal
neighbourhood
Z_{J}
of the point
p_{J}=\tau(C_{J})\in Y
, and in particular is
generated by finitely many complexes which depend only on the scheme theoretic
exceptional fiber
\underline{C}_{J}
overlying
C_{J}
. Further, considering the flat
cover
Y=Z_{I_{1}}\cup\cdots\cup Z_{I_{k}}\cup(Y\setminus\{p_{I_{1}},\ldots,p_{I_{k}}\})
shows that a complex
x
supported
within
C_{I}
lies in
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
if and only if its restriction to each

X_{J}
lies in
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X_{J}}{Z_{J}}\right)$}}
, and this gives us the description

\displaystyle\mathcal{p\kern-0.5pte\kern-0.5ptr\kern-1.0pt}_{I}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}=\bigg\langle\{{O}_{C_{i}}(-1)\;|\;i\in I\}\;\cup\;\{\omega_{\underline{C}_{J}}[1]\;|\;J=I_{1},\ldots,I_{k}\}\bigg\rangle.

A consequence of the equivalence is that the property of a two-term complex

x
of coherent sheaves being ‘perverse’ can be checked with respect to any
partial contraction which maps
\mathop{\text{}}{Supp}(x)
to a (finite collection
of) points. That is to say, if
\tau\colon X\to Y
and
\tau^{\prime}\colon X\to Y^{\prime}

are two crepant partial contractions which contract all the curves containing

\mathop{\text{}}{Supp}(x)
, then we have
x\in\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
if and only if

x\in\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y^{\prime}}\right)$}}
.

Since
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
away from the contracted curves should mimic

\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{X}\right)$}}=\coh X
, we then have the following result which reduces the
construction of
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
to a binary choice between
\coh X
and

\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
on each
C_{i}
, effectively eliminating the need to consider
the geometry of the non-commutative scheme
(Y,\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{${V}$})
.

Theorem 3.16
.

If
\tau\colon X\to Y
is the crepant contraction of the exceptional subset

C_{I}\subset X
, then the associated heart
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
is the smallest
extension-closed subcategory of
\Dm X
containing the full subcategory

\mathcal{p\kern-0.5pte\kern-0.5ptr\kern-1.0pt}_{I}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
and all coherent sheaves on
X
that are supported within the
uncontracted curves
\bigcup_{i\notin I}C_{i}
. In other words,

\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}=\bigg\langle\underbrace{\left\{x\in\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}\;\middle|\;\Supp x\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in I}C_{i}\right\}}_{\mathcal{p\kern-0.5pte\kern-0.5ptr\kern-1.0pt}_{I}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}}\;\cup\;\left\{x\in\coh X\;\middle|\;\Supp x\subseteq\bigcup_{i\notin I}C_{i}\right\}\bigg\rangle.

Proof.

The category
\mathcal{p\kern-0.5pte\kern-0.5ptr\kern-1.0pt}_{I}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
clearly lies in
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
,
while if
x\in\coh X
is supported within
\bigcup_{i\notin I}C_{i}

then
\mathbf{R}\tau_{\ast}x
is a sheaf on
Y
and hence
x\in\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
. To furnish the required description of
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
,
it thus suffices to show that every object in the category is an
extension of complexes of the two given forms.

Now an arbitrary complex
x\in\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
can be written as an
extension of its cohomology objects with respect to
\coh X
, namely

x_{0}=\textnormal{H}^{0}(x)
and
x_{1}[1]=\textnormal{H}^{-1}(x)[1]
. It is clear from
(
14
) that
x_{0}
,
x_{1}[1]
are themselves
contained in
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
. Further we have
\tau_{\ast}(x_{1})=0
and
hence
x_{1}
must be supported within the contracted curve

\bigcup_{i\in I}C_{i}
, from which it follows that
x_{1}[1]\in\mathcal{p\kern-0.5pte\kern-0.5ptr\kern-1.0pt}_{I}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
.

On the other hand, writing
{I}\subset{O}_{X}
for the ideal
sheaf of the closed subscheme
\bigcup_{i\in I}C_{i}
, note that

y={I}^{n}x_{0}
(i.e. the image of the natural map

{I}^{n}\otimes x_{0}\to x_{0}
) is a subsheaf of
x_{0}
(in
particular, an object of
\coh X
) supported within
\bigcup_{i\notin I}C_{i}
for
n\gg 0
. The quotient
x_{0}/y
is clearly supported
within
\bigcup_{i\in I}C_{i}
. Further, applying
\tau_{\ast}
to this
quotient and examining the long exact sequence shows

\mathbf{R}^{1}\tau_{\ast}(x_{0}/y)
vanishes since

\mathbf{R}^{1}\tau_{\ast}(x_{0})
does, i.e.
x_{0}/y
lies in

\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
(and hence in
\mathcal{p\kern-0.5pte\kern-0.5ptr\kern-1.0pt}_{I}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
).

This concludes our proof that
x
is an extension of objects of the
required form.
∎

It is immediate from
theorem

3.16
that an object of

\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
is simple if and only if it is simple in some summand

\mathcal{p\kern-0.5pte\kern-0.5ptr\kern-1.0pt}_{J}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}\subset\mathcal{p\kern-0.5pte\kern-0.5ptr\kern-1.0pt}_{I}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
or a simple coherent sheaf
supported on the uncontracted locus, i.e. we have the following.

Corollary 3.17
.

For
\tau\colon X\to Y
the crepant contraction of
C_{I}\subset X
, the simple
objects of
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
are the skyscrapers
{O}_{p}
at closed
points
p\notin\bigcup_{i\in I}C_{i}
, the sheaves
{O}_{C_{j}}(-1)
for

j\in I
, and suspended canonical sheaves
\omega_{\underline{C}_{J}}[1]
for each
scheme-theoretic exceptional fiber
\underline{C}_{J}
of
\tau
.

§ 4 Numerical intermediate hearts

We begin the systematic investigation of t-structures on
\Dm X
that are

intermediate
with respect to the perverse t-structure, i.e. whose heart is contained in
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}[-1,0]
. Noting hearts of t-structures
have a natural partial order
(\leq)
given by containment of co-aisles,
the set of intermediate hearts we are concerned with is precisely the closed interval

\tilt(\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}})=\left\{K\;\middle|\;\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}[-1]\leq K\leq\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}\right\}.

The partial order on
\tilt(\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}})
corresponds to containment of torsion-free classes across the
Happel–Reiten–Smalø bijections
[
HRS96
]
.
That is to say, if we write
\tors(\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}})
(resp.
\torf(\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}})
) for the set of torsion (resp. torsion-free) classes in

\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
, then we have order-preserving bijections

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\tors(\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}),\subseteq\right)^{\text{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\torf(\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}),\subseteq\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\tilt(\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}),\leq\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-){}^{\perp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-){}^{\perp}\;\ast\;(-)[-1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{\perp}(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\;\ast\;{}^{\perp}(-)[-1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)[1]\cap\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\cap\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\scriptstyle\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}}

(15)

where the notation
U{}^{\perp}
(resp.
{}^{\perp}U
), for
U\subset\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
, denotes the full subcategory of all objects in
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}

that have no non-zero morphisms from (resp. to) objects of
U
.

In this section we furnish a complete description of the sub-poset of hearts in

\tilt(\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}})
which are
numerical
, i.e. ‘detected by

\KK
-theory’ in the sense of
definition

4.1
below.

§ 4.1 The heart fan

Fix a triangulated category
{T}
,
and a surjection
\KK{T}\twoheadrightarrow\mathfrak{h}
of the Grothendieck group of

{T}
onto a free Abelian group of finite rank. Taking

\mathbb{R}
-linear duals, note that the finite dimensional Euclidean space

\Theta=\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathfrak{h},\mathbb{R})
thus injects into

\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(\KK{T},\mathbb{R})
so that any vector
\theta\in\Theta
can be regarded as an
\mathbb{R}
-linear functional on

\KK{T}
.

Definition 4.1
.

Given the heart of a t-structure
H\subset{T}
, the
heart
cone of
H
(with respect to
\mathfrak{h}
) is defined to be the
subset

\CC(H)=\{\theta\in\Theta\;|\;\theta[h]\geq 0\text{ for all }h\in H\}
.

We say
H
is
numerical
if
\CC(H)
contains a non-zero vector.

For instance, if
H
is
algebraic
(i.e. has Jordan–Hölder
filtrations and finitely many simple objects) then
\CC(H)
is an orthant in

\Theta
, spanned by the dual vectors to the basis of
\mathfrak{h}
given
by classes of simples in
H
.

More generally
\CC(H)\subset\Theta
is a

rational cone
, i.e. a closed convex subset defined by inequalities with
coefficients in
\mathfrak{h}
. If a rational cone
\sigma\subset\Theta

lies in the half space
\{\alpha\geq 0\}
defined by

\alpha\in\mathfrak{h}
, we say the cone
\sigma\cap\{\alpha=0\}
is a

face
of
\sigma
, and write
\textnormal{faces}(\sigma)
for the set
of all faces of
\sigma
. A collection of cones
\Sigma
is a
fan
in

\Theta
if it is closed under taking faces and the intersection of any two
cones in
\Sigma
is a face of both. We say the fan is
complete
if its
cones cover
\Theta
, i.e.
\bigcup_{\sigma\in\Sigma}\sigma=\Theta
.

Theorem 4.2

(
[
BPPW24
, theorem A]
)
.

For
H
,
{T}
,
\mathfrak{h}
, and
\Theta
as above, the
collection of cones

\HFan(H)=\bigcup_{K\in\tilt(H)}\mathop{\textnormal{}}{faces}\left(\CC K\right)

is a fan in
\Theta
, called the
heart fan
of
H
(with respect
to
\mathfrak{h}
). If
H
is algebraic, then this fan is complete. If
in addition the map
\KK{T}\twoheadrightarrow\mathfrak{h}
is an isomorphism,
then an intermediate heart
K
is algebraic if and only if
\CC K
is a
full-dimensional cone, and in this case
K
is the unique intermediate
heart with heart cone
\CC K
.

The existence of the heart fan, our primary bookkeeping tool, is complemented by
the following observation that computes the ‘fibers’ of the map
\CC\colon\tilt(H)\to\HFan(H)
. To introduce some notation, if
H
is algebraic, then
note that any
\theta\in\Theta
induces a tripartition

H=H_{\textnormal{tr}}(\theta)\ast H_{\textnormal{ss}}(\theta)\ast H_{\textnormal{tf}}(\theta)

where
H_{\textnormal{tr}}(\theta)
(resp.
H_{\textnormal{tf}}(\theta)
) are the
numerical torsion
(resp.
torsion-free
)
classes

\displaystyle H_{\textnormal{tr}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\{h\in H\;|\;\theta[f]<0\text{ for all non-zero
factors }h\twoheadrightarrow f\neq 0\},

\displaystyle H_{\textnormal{tf}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\{h\in H\;|\;\theta[s]>0\text{ for all non-zero
sub-objects }0\neq s\hookrightarrow h\}

introduced by Baumann–Kamnitzer–Tingley in

[
BKT14
]
, while
H_{\textnormal{ss}}(\theta)
is
the wide subcategory of
\theta
-semistable objects
á la King

[
Kin94
]
given as

\displaystyle H_{\textnormal{ss}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\{h\in H\;|\;\theta[h]=0,\;\theta[s]\geq 0\text{ for
all sub-objects }s\hookrightarrow h\}.

Hearts
K\in\tilt(H)
with
\theta\in\CC(K)
arise from torsion
pairs that coarsen this tripartition.

Theorem 4.3

(
[
BPPW24
, corollary 3.3]
)
.

Suppose
H
is the heart of an algebraic t-structure on the triangulated
category
{T}
, and
\theta
is a vector in
\Theta
as above. For any torsion pair

H_{\textnormal{ss}}(\theta)=U\ast V
on the wide (Abelian) subcategory of

\theta
-semistable objects in
H
, the expression

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline qn:numtiltsbyUV}K=V\ast H_{\textnormal{tf}}(\theta)\ast H_{\textnormal{tr}}(\theta)[-1]\ast U[-1]

(16)

defines a heart in
\tilt(H)
that satisfies
\theta\in\CC K
. Each

K\in\tilt(H)
whose heart cone contains
\theta
arises in this
fashion, in particular there is a poset isomorphism

\tilt(H_{\textnormal{ss}}\theta)\longrightarrow\{K\in\tilt(H)\;|\;\theta\in\CC K\}

where the inverse map sends
K\in\tilt(H)
to
K_{\textnormal{ss}}(\theta)=\{k\in K\;|\;\theta[k]=0\}\subset\tilt(H_{\textnormal{ss}}\theta)
.

Setting
U=0,V=H_{\textnormal{ss}}(\theta)
in (
16
) yields the maximal
element
H^{\textnormal{tt}}(\theta)
in the subposet
\{K\;|\;\theta\in\CC K\}\subset\tilt(H)
, and it can be characterised as follows.

Corollary 4.4
.

Given an intermediate heart
K\in\tilt(H)
and a vector

\theta\in\CC(K)
, the following statements are equivalent.

1.

The heart
K
is maximal among tilts of
H
with
\theta
in
their heart cone, i.e.
K=H^{\textnormal{tt}}(\theta)
.

2.

We have the equality
H_{\textnormal{ss}}(\theta)=K_{\textnormal{ss}}(\theta)
.

3.

The category
H_{\textnormal{ss}}(\theta)
lies in
K
.

4.

The category
K_{\textnormal{ss}}(\theta)
lies in
H
.

Likewise setting
U=H_{\textnormal{ss}}(\theta),V=0
in (
16
) yields the
minimal heart
H_{\textnormal{tt}}(\theta)\in\tilt(H)
containing
\theta
in its heart
cone. Evidently
\{K\in\tilt(H)\;|\;\theta\in\CC(K)\}
coincides with the interval
[H_{\textnormal{tt}}\theta,H^{\textnormal{tt}}\theta]\subseteq\tilt(H)
.

We say a vector
\theta
is
generic
in a cone
\sigma
if it
lies in
\sigma
but not in any proper face of
\sigma
. If

\theta,\theta^{\prime}
are both generic in some cone
\sigma\in\HFan(H)
, the
above results imply that all their associated numerical categories coincide
i.e.
H_{\textnormal{tr}}(\theta)=H_{\textnormal{tr}}(\theta^{\prime})
,
H_{\textnormal{tf}}(\theta)=H_{\textnormal{tf}}(\theta^{\prime})
, and so
forth. Thus we use the notations
H_{\textnormal{tr}}(\sigma)
,
H_{\textnormal{tf}}(\sigma)
,

H_{\textnormal{ss}}(\sigma)
,
H_{\textnormal{tt}}(\sigma)
, and
H^{\textnormal{tt}}(\sigma)
to denote the
respective categories associated to any generic vector
\theta\in\sigma
.

Remark 4.5

(Zooming into the heart fan)
.

Given a parameter
\theta\in\Theta
, the correspondence

4.3
can be used to essentially ‘read off’ the heart
fan of
H_{\textnormal{ss}}(\theta)
from that of
H
. To see this, note that the
exact inclusion
H_{\textnormal{ss}}(\theta)\hookrightarrow H
induces a map of Grothendieck
groups which can be composed with the surjection onto
\mathfrak{h}
to
obtain a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\KK H_{\textnormal{ss}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\KK H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{h}_{\textnormal{ss}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{h},}

where
\mathfrak{h}_{\textnormal{ss}}
is again a free Abelian group of finite rank.
The heart fan of
H_{\textnormal{ss}}(\theta)
with respect to
\mathfrak{h}_{\textnormal{ss}}
thus
lives in the vector space

\Theta_{\textnormal{ss}}=\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathfrak{h}_{\textnormal{ss}},\mathbb{R})
which is
naturally a quotient of
\Theta
. It can be shown that the surjection

\Theta\twoheadrightarrow\Theta_{\textnormal{ss}}
is such that the image of (the
sub-fan generated by)

\{\sigma\in\HFan(H)\;|\;\theta\text{ lies in some face of }\sigma\}

is a sub-fan of
\HFan(H_{\textnormal{ss}}\theta)
. More precisely, given a heart
K\in[H_{\textnormal{tt}}\theta,H^{\textnormal{tt}}\theta]
and the corresponding category

K_{\textnormal{ss}}(\theta)\in\tilt(H_{\textnormal{ss}}\theta)
, it can be shown that the image of

\CC(K)
is always a face of
\CC(K_{\textnormal{ss}}\theta)
and is in fact equal to

\CC(K_{\textnormal{ss}}\theta)
if
K
is algebraic.

To see an example where the image of
\CC K
is a proper face of

\CC(K_{\textnormal{ss}})
, one should examine the category

H=\mathop{\text{}}{Rep}(\text{\small{2}}\rightrightarrows\text{\small{1}})

associated to the
2
-Kronecker quiver
[
BPPW24
, example
4.6]
, and the stability parameter

\theta=(-1,1)
given in the basis dual to vertex simples.

Fixing the algebraic heart
H=\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}\simeq\mathcal{f\kern-0.5ptl\kern-1.0ptm\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}\Lambda
in
\Dm X\simeq\Dfl\Lambda
, we will use the above results to compute the complete
subposet of numerical hearts in
\tilt(H)
. The
\mathfrak{J}
-cone arrangement, a
complete simplicial fan arising form the Dynkin data associated to
X
,
naturally acts as the heart fan in this setting.

Theorem 4.6
.

Upon identifying the Grothendieck group
\KK X
with the restricted
root lattice
\mathfrak{h}(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
in a way that each
simple root
\alpha_{i}
(
i\in\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J}
) corresponds to the
i
th simple of

H=\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
(indexed as in
3.1
), the heart fan of

H
is given by the
\mathfrak{J}
-cone arrangement
\Arr(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})
in

\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})\simeq\Hom(\KK X,\mathbb{R})
.

In order to prove
theorem

4.6
it will suffice to identify, for
each cone
\sigma
of the form
\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{\pm}
or
\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}
, a heart
K\in\tilt(H)
with heart cone
\sigma
. This is
accomplished in
propositions

4.8
and
4.11
.
We then examine the categories
H_{\textnormal{ss}}(\sigma)
for each (not necessarily
maximal) cone
\sigma
in the
\mathfrak{J}
-cone arrangement
(
4.4
) to obtain
a complete list of numerical intermediate hearts.

§ 4.2 Algebraic intermediate hearts

Each modifying module
\nu N\in\mathop{\textnormal{}}{MM}{}^{N}R
yields a tilting
\Lambda
-module
{}_{\nu}\Lambda
and a
tilting
{}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\nu}
-module
\Lambda_{\nu}
, defining the
functors
\Dfl{}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\nu}\rightrightarrows\Dfl\Lambda
given as in
(
11
). Applying the Brenner–Butler theorem
[
AHK
, chapter 4, remark
3.10]
to these equivalences
gives two torsion pairs

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline qn:mutatedtorsion}\mathcal{f\kern-0.5ptl\kern-1.0ptm\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}\Lambda=T_{\nu}\ast F_{\nu}=U_{\nu}\ast V_{\nu},

(17)

\displaystyle T_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\{x\in\mathcal{f\kern-0.5ptl\kern-1.0ptm\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}\Lambda\;|\;\Ext^{1}({}_{\nu}\Lambda,x)=0\}

\displaystyle\qquad U_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\{x\in\mathcal{f\kern-0.5ptl\kern-1.0ptm\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}\Lambda\;|\;x\otimes\Lambda_{\nu}=0\}

\displaystyle F_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\{x\in\mathcal{f\kern-0.5ptl\kern-1.0ptm\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}\Lambda\;|\;\Hom({}_{\nu}\Lambda,x)=0\}

\displaystyle\qquad V_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\{x\in\mathcal{f\kern-0.5ptl\kern-1.0ptm\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}\Lambda\;|\;\Tor_{1}(x,\Lambda_{\nu})=0\}

such that the squares below commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dfl{}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\nu}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dfl\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{f\kern-0.5ptl\kern-1.0ptm\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}{}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\nu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\nu}[1]\ast T_{\nu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{\nu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dfl{}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\nu}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dfl\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{f\kern-0.5ptl\kern-1.0ptm\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}{}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\nu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\nu}\ast U_{\nu}[-1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\nu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Abusing notation to write
H
for
\mathcal{f\kern-0.5ptl\kern-1.0ptm\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}\Lambda\subset\Dfl\Lambda
as well as

\mathcal{f\kern-0.5ptl\kern-1.0ptm\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}{}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\nu}\subset\Dfl{}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\nu}
, each
\mathfrak{J}
-path

\nu
thus gives us two hearts in
\Dfl\Lambda
that are intermediate with
respect to
\mathcal{f\kern-0.5ptl\kern-1.0ptm\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}\Lambda
, namely

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline qn:intermediatehearts}\Phi_{\nu}H[-1]=F_{\nu}\ast T_{\nu}[-1],\qquad\Psi_{\nu}H=V_{\nu}\ast U_{\nu}[-1].

(18)

To compute the heart cones of these, we now show that the

\KK
-theoretic maps induced by the mutation functors

\Phi_{\nu},\Psi_{\nu}
obey the same combinatorial rules which govern
mutations of root lattices. Note that for any
\mathfrak{J}
-path

\nu
,
theorem

3.5
indexes the summands
of
\nu N
(and hence the simple
{}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\nu}
-modules) over the
vertices of
\underline{\Delta}\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J}
. This identifies

\KK({}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\nu})
with the restricted root lattice

\mathfrak{h}(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J})
by mapping the classes of simples to
corresponding simple restricted roots. We then have the following.

Lemma 4.7
.

For
i\in\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J}
, the simple mutation functors

\Phi_{i},\Psi_{i}\colon\Dfl{}_{\nu_{i}}\Lambda_{\nu_{i}}\to\Dfl\Lambda
both
induce the same map on
\KK
-theory and this agrees with the map

\varphi_{i}\colon\mathfrak{h}(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\nu_{i}\mathfrak{J})\to\mathfrak{h}(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
given as in
(
3
).

Proof.

This is essentially
[
NW23
, remark 5.2]
, we
spell out the details for clarity. By

[
HW23
, lemma 3.7]
, both
\Phi_{i}

and
\Psi_{i}
induce the same map on
\KK
-theory which we
momentarily call
\psi_{i}\colon\mathfrak{h}(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\nu_{i}\mathfrak{J})\to\mathfrak{h}(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
.

As in the proof of

theorem

3.5
, the connection to mutation-combinatorics comes by
reducing to surfaces. Thus we consider a general elephant
\Spec\overline{R}\hookrightarrow\Spec R
and the reduction functor

\mathbb{F}(-)=(-)\otimes_{R}\overline{R}
. We have the identifications of
vector spaces

\KK_{\text{split}}(\mathcal{p\kern-0.5ptr\kern-0.5pto\kern-1.0ptj}\mathbb{F}\Lambda)\otimes\mathbb{R}\quad\simeq\quad\KK_{\text{split}}(\mathcal{p\kern-0.5ptr\kern-0.5pto\kern-1.0ptj}\Lambda)\otimes\mathbb{R}\quad\simeq\quad\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J}),

where the first is an identification of
g
-fans
[
Gar23
, proposition
B]
and the second arises from
the duality between simples and indecomposable projectives. Here
Iyama–Wemyss
[
IW
, theorem 5.2]
show that
the
g
-fan of the contracted preprojective algebra
\mathbb{F}\Lambda
,
and hence also that of the modification algebra
\Lambda
, coincides with the fan
\Arr^{+}(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})\cup\Arr^{-}(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})
.

Analogously, the
g
-fan of
{}_{\nu_{i}}\Lambda_{\nu_{i}}
sits in the

\nu_{i}\mathfrak{J}
-cone arrangement.

Being induced by a derived equivalence, the map

(\psi_{i}\check{}\,)^{-1}\colon\KK_{\text{split}}(\mathcal{p\kern-0.5ptr\kern-0.5pto\kern-1.0ptj}{}_{\nu_{i}}\Lambda_{\nu_{i}})\otimes\mathbb{R}\to\KK_{\text{split}}(\mathcal{p\kern-0.5ptr\kern-0.5pto\kern-1.0ptj}\Lambda)\otimes\mathbb{R},

respects silting theory and is a map of
g
-fans. In particular, the
image of
\mathrm{C}^{+}_{\nu_{i}\mathfrak{J}}
under
(\psi_{i}\check{}\,)^{-1}
is a cone
in the intersection arrangement of
\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
.
Reading off the matrix
(\psi_{i}\check{}\,)^{-1}
from
[
HW23
, lemma
5.2]
, we see that

(\psi_{i}\check{}\,)^{-1}\mathrm{C}_{\nu_{i}\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
intersects (and hence is equal to) the cone

\nu_{i}\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
, and the images of primitive integral generators
are primitive integral.

Evidently from
proposition

2.6
the
map
(\varphi_{i}\check{}\,){}^{-1}
admits an identical description, so that

\psi_{i}=\varphi_{i}
as required.
∎

Combined with
lemma

3.7
,
the above lemma shows that if
\nu=\nu_{i_{n}}\ldots\nu_{i_{1}}
is a

\mathfrak{J}
-path then the map on
\KK
-theory induced by
\Phi_{\nu}
and

\Psi_{\nu}
agrees with the composite
\varphi_{i_{n}}\circ\cdots\circ\varphi_{i_{1}}
. Computation of the heart cones
\CC(\Psi_{\nu}H)
and

\CC(\Phi_{\nu}H[-1])
is then immediate.

Proposition 4.8
.

For each
\mathfrak{J}
-path
\nu
, the heart
\Psi_{\nu}H
has heart cone

\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
while the heart
\Phi_{\nu}H[-1]
has heart cone

\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{-}
.

Since the complement of
\bigcup_{\nu}\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{\pm}
has positive codimension
in
\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
, we deduce from the above proposition and

theorem

4.2
that every algebraic heart in
\tilt(H)
must be of
the form
\Psi_{\nu}H
or
\Phi_{\nu}H[-1]
for some
\nu
.

Faces of algebraic heart cones

We show that non-maximal cones in

\Arr^{\pm}(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
cannot arise as heart cones. This can be
accomplished by observing that the corresponding subcategories of semistable
modules are module categories over
\tau
-tilting finite
algebras
[
Gar23
]
, we take a
more direct approach instead.

Theorem 4.9
.

A non-zero cone
\sigma\in\Arr^{\pm}(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})
is the heart cone of some

K\in\tilt(H)
if and only if
\sigma
is full-dimensional. In this
case,
(K,\sigma)
are of the form
(\Psi_{\nu}H,\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+})
or

(\Phi_{\nu}H[-1],\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{-})
for some positive
\mathfrak{J}
-path
\nu
.

Proof.

If
\sigma
is full dimensional it must be of the form

\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{\pm}
, so it is a heart cone of some
K\in\tilt(H)

determined uniquely by
proposition

4.8
.

To show the converse, suppose for the sake of contradiction that there
is a heart
K
in
\tilt(H)
whose heart cone
\sigma=\CC K
is non-zero but non-maximal in (without loss of generality)
\Arr^{+}(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})
. Consider the sets

\displaystyle\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline qn:Kcomparable}\{H^{\prime}\in\tilt(H)\;|\;H^{\prime}\text{ algebraic},\quad\sigma\subset\CC(H^{\prime}),\quad H^{\prime}\geq K\}

(19)

\displaystyle\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline qn:Kcomparable2}\{H^{\prime}\in\tilt(H)\;|\;H^{\prime}\text{ algebraic},\quad\sigma\subset\CC(H^{\prime}),\quad H^{\prime}\leq K\}

(20)

of algebraic hearts which are comparable with
K
and have
\sigma

in their heart cone. Since the
\mathfrak{J}
-cone arrangement is locally finite away from the
origin, these are finite.

Replacing
(K,\sigma)
by another pair
if necessary, we may assume one of the sets (
19
)
or (
20
), without loss of generality the former, is
non-empty. Indeed if not, then in particular the heart

K^{\prime}=H_{\textnormal{tt}}(\sigma)
is non-algebraic so that
\sigma^{\prime}=\CC(K^{\prime})
is a
non-maximal cone containing
\sigma
, and for each chamber

\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}=\CC(\Psi_{\nu}H)
surrounding
\sigma^{\prime}
we evidently
have
K^{\prime}=H_{\textnormal{tt}}(\sigma)\leq\Psi_{\nu}H
.

Thus there is a minimal algebraic heart
H^{\prime}
satisfying
H^{\prime}\geq K
and
\sigma\subset\CC(H^{\prime})
.
Examining the inequalities

H\;\geq\;H^{\prime}\;\geq\;K\;\geq\;H[-1]\;\geq\;H^{\prime}

shows that
K
is a tilt of
H^{\prime}
. Since
K\neq H^{\prime}
the
corresponding torsion class
K[1]\cap H^{\prime}
is non-zero and in
particular contains a simple object
s
of
H^{\prime}
. But then the simple
tilt

H^{\prime\prime}=\{h\in H^{\prime}\;|\;\Hom(s,h)=0\}\ast\langle s\rangle[-1]

satisfies
H^{\prime}>H^{\prime\prime}\geq K
(in particular
H^{\prime\prime}\in\tilt(H)
and

\sigma\subset\CC(H^{\prime\prime})
) and moreover is algebraic
[
HW23
, lemma
5.5]
—thus contradicting the
minimality of
H^{\prime}
.
∎

We remark that the key ingredient in the above proof, namely covering relations
arising from simple tilts, are addressed in greater detail in

5
below and in particular we identify explicitly all simple
tilts of
\Psi_{\nu}H
and
\Phi_{\nu}H[-1]
as arising from simple mutation.

§ 4.3 The subfan of movable cones

Setting aside momentarily the question
of whether the geometric and semi-geometric hearts of

3.3
and
3.4
are intermediate with
respect to
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
, we show that each non-zero cone in the finite
subfan
\Arr(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})\subset\Arr(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})
is the heart cone of some

\Psi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{\nu X}{Y}\right)$}}
arising from a sequence of flops

X\dashrightarrow\nu X
and crepant contraction
\nu X\to Y
.

We fix, as in
4.2
, the identification
\KK X\simeq\mathfrak{h}(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
given by writing

\alpha_{0}=\left[\omega_{\underline{C}}[1]\right]
and
\alpha_{i}=[{O}_{C_{i}}(-1)]

for
i\in\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J}
. For birational models
W=\nu X
, there is
the analogous identification
\KK W\simeq\mathfrak{h}(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J})
.

Divisors and root lattices

By
[
Van04
, lemma
3.4.3]
the inclusion of the
reduced exceptional fiber
C\hookrightarrow X
induces an isomorphism of Picard groups,
in particular
\Pic X
is a free Abelian group of rank

|\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J}|
that is naturally dual to the group
\textnormal{Z}_{1}\kern-1.0pt(X)
of
\pi
-relative
1
-cycles via the perfect pairing

\displaystyle\Pic X\;\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\;\textnormal{Z}_{1}\kern-1.0pt(X)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z},\qquad({L},C_{i})\mapsto\deg({L}|_{C_{i}}).

(21)

Since objects in
\coh X
have proper support of dimension
\leq 1
,
Snapper–Kleiman intersection theory
[
Kol96
, §VI]
extends the above map to an injection

\Pic X\hookrightarrow\Hom(\KK X,\mathbb{Z})
by associating a line bundle
{L}\in\Pic X

to the functional
({L}\cdot-)\colon\KK X\to\mathbb{Z}
given on (classes of)
coherent sheaves
{F}\in\coh(X)
as

[{F}]\longmapsto\chi({F})-\chi({L}\,\check{}\,\otimes{F}).

Note we have
({L}\,\cdot\,{O}_{C_{i}}(n))=\deg({L}|_{C_{i}})
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
i\in\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J}
, consequently
({L}\,\cdot\,{O}_{p})=0

for any closed point
p\in C
.

Lemma 4.10
.

For any closed point
p
in the exceptional fiber of
\pi\colon X\to Z
, the skyscraper sheaf
{O}_{p}
has
\KK
-theory class

\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}
. Consequently the assignment
{L}\mapsto({L}\,\cdot\,-)

extends to an isomorphism of
\RealPic(X)=\Pic X\,\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\,\mathbb{R}

with the hyperplane

\{\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}=0\}
in
\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
.

Proof.

By
[
Kar17
, theorem 5.2.4]
, the

\KK
-theory class of
{O}_{p}
is determined by the ranks of summands
of
{V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
as
[{O}_{p}]=\sum\text{rk}({N}_{i})\alpha_{i}
. By

[
IW
, proposition 9.4]
, this vector is
precisely the imaginary root
\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}
.

It follows that the injection
\RealPic(X)\to\Hom(\KK X,\mathbb{R})\simeq\Theta(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})
lands in the subspace

\{\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}=0\}
, and counting dimensions shows it is an isomorphism
onto this hyperplane.
∎

This explicit description of the functionals allows us to readily compute heart
cones of geometric and semi-geometric hearts.

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
\tau\colon X\to Y
be the crepant contraction of the collection of
exceptional curves
\{C_{i}\;|\;i\in I\}
defined by

I\subset\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J}
, and suppose
Y\not\simeq Z
(i.e. not all

\pi
-exceptional curves are contracted). Then the heart

\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}\subset\Dm X
of
\tau
-perverse sheaves has heart cone

\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}\cup I}^{0}\subset\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
.

Proof.

If
\theta
lies in the heart cone of
K=\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
, then
considering the sheaves
{O}_{C_{i}}(n)
(
n\in\mathbb{Z}
) on
uncontracted curves and the sheaves
{O}_{C_{i}}(-1)
on contracted
curves shows
\theta
lies in the chamber
\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}
. In
particular,
\theta
necessarily vanishes on the class
\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}

of skyscraper sheaves. But for a connected component
C_{J}\subset C_{I}

and a closed point
p\in C_{J}
, we see from

lemma

4.10
that
{O}_{p}
admits a filtration
by the simples of
\mathcal{p\kern-0.5pte\kern-0.5ptr\kern-1.0pt}_{J}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
in which every simple appears
at least once. Thus in
\KK
-theory we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline qn:ktheoryofpartialcontraction}[{O}_{p}]=\left[\omega_{\underline{C}_{J}}[1]\right]+\sum_{i\in J}m_{i}\,[{O}_{C_{i}}(-1)]\quad=\left[\omega_{\underline{C}_{J}}[1]\right]+\sum_{i\in J}m_{i}\,\alpha_{i}

(22)

for positive integers
m_{i}
. Since
\theta
is non-negative
on each class appearing in the above expression and in particular
vanishes on
[{O}_{p}]
, it must vanish on every class on the right-hand
side. Repeating the argument on all connected components of
C_{I}

shows
\theta(\alpha_{i})=0
whenever
i\in I
, and thus the heart
cone
\CC K
is contained in
\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}\cup I}^{0}
.

Conversely if
\theta
lies in
\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}\cup I}^{0}
, then in
particular
\theta
lies in the image of the nef cone in
\Pic X

under the map of
lemma

4.10
, and thus

\theta[x]\geq 0
whenever
x
is a coherent sheaf. On the other
hand if
x\in\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
is supported in
C_{I}
, then we may assume

x
is supported in some connected component
C_{J}\subset C_{I}
so
that
x
lies in
\mathcal{p\kern-0.5pte\kern-0.5ptr\kern-1.0pt}_{J}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
. But then

theorem

3.13
and the expression
(
22
) show that the
\KK
-theory
class
[x]
can be expressed as some integral linear combination

\displaystyle[x]=n_{\delta}\,[{O}_{p}]+\sum_{i\in J}n_{i}\,[{O}_{C_{i}}(-1)]\quad=n_{\delta}\,\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}+\sum_{i\in J}n_{i}\,\alpha_{i},

so that
\theta[x]=0
. Since an arbitrary complex
x\in\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}

is an extension of objects of the above forms
(
theorem

3.16
), it follows that
\theta[x]\geq 0

and thus
\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}\cup I}^{0}
is equal to the heart cone of

\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
.
∎

Likewise if
W=\nu X
is a birational model of
X
, we have an injection of

\RealPic(W)
into
\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J})
, and hence
into
\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
via
the wall-crossing isomorphisms in
proposition

2.6
. It follows that
for
I\subsetneq\Delta\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J}
and the crepant contraction

\tau\colon W\to Y
of the curves
\{C_{i}\;|\;i\in I\}
in
W
, the heart

\Psi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}\subset\Dm X
has heart cone

(\varphi_{\nu}\kern-1.5pt\check{}\;)^{-1}\mathrm{C}_{\nu\mathfrak{J}\cup I}^{0}\quad=\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}\;\cap\;\bigcap_{i\in I}\{\varphi_{\nu}\alpha_{i}=0\}.

Intermediacy of (semi-)geometric hearts

It is evident from the description (
7
) of
H=\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}

that we have
\coh X\subset H[-1,0]
, and from
theorem

3.16

we deduce that the same can be said of all the hearts
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
arising from
crepant contractions
X\to Y
.

We will now show that for any birational model
W=\nu X
and any crepant
contraction
W\to Y
, the heart
\Psi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}
is intermediate with
respect to
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
. This will in particular show that every non-zero
cone in
\Arr(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})
is of the form
\CC K
for some
K\in\tilt(H)
, thus (with
proposition

4.8
) completing the proof of

theorem

4.6
.

Proposition 4.12
.

For any sequence of flops
\nu
from
X
and any crepant contraction

W\to Y
involving the birational model
W=\nu X
, the heart
\Psi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}\subset\Dm X
is intermediate with respect to
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
.

Proof.

Again,
theorem

3.16
allows us to reduce to the case
of
\Psi_{\nu}\coh(W)
. Since
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}\geq\Psi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Z}\right)$}}

(see
4.2
) and
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Z}\right)$}}\geq\coh(W)
,
we have
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}\geq\Psi_{\nu}\coh(W)
and it thus suffices to exhibit
the lower bound

\Psi_{\nu}\coh(W)\geq\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}[-1].

Applying the equivalence
{(\Psi_{\nu})}^{-1}
and swapping
X
and
W

for simplicity, we equivalently show

\coh(X)\geq\Phi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{\nu X}{Z}\right)$}}[-1]

for any spherical and (without loss of generality) atomic
\mathfrak{J}
-path

\nu
.

Noting there are only finitely many atomic spherical
\mathfrak{J}
-paths
(since the set
\Chambers(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})
is finite), we can
further reduce to the case when
\nu
a maximal spherical atomic

\mathfrak{J}
-path. Indeed if
\nu
and
\nu_{i}\,\nu
are both atomic
spherical
\mathfrak{J}
-paths, then we have

\Phi_{i}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{\nu_{i}W}{Z}\right)$}}\geq\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Z}\right)$}}
,
and hence (by
lemma

3.6
)

\Phi_{\nu_{i}\,\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{\nu_{i}\nu X}{Z}\right)$}}=\Phi_{\nu}\Phi_{i}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{\nu_{i}W}{Z}\right)$}}\geq\Phi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Z}\right)$}}.

Noting
\Chambers(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})
is the face poset of a finite
hyperplane arrangement, any longest spherical
\mathfrak{J}
-path
\nu

defines the chamber
\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}=-\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}
that is furthest
from the principal chamber
\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}
. Likewise
\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{-}
contains the ray

\mathrm{C}_{\Delta}^{-}
and is the furthest
such chamber from
\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{-}
.

It follows that a generic vector

\theta\in\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{-}
satisfies
\theta(\alpha_{i})>0
for all

i\in\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J}
, and thus (up to rescaling) can be written as

\theta=\theta_{0}-\alpha_{0}^{\ast}
for some
\theta_{0}\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}
and
\alpha_{0}
defined as

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline qn:deltastar}\alpha_{0}^{\ast}(\alpha_{i})=\begin{cases}1,&i=0,\\
0,&i\in\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J}\end{cases}.

(23)

Since
\theta
lies in the heart cone of
\Phi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Z}\right)$}}[-1]

(
proposition

4.8
), by

theorem

4.3
we see that the corresponding torsion-free
class satisfies

\displaystyle\Phi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Z}\right)$}}[-1]\cap H\quad

\displaystyle=\quad H_{\textnormal{tf}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\quad\left\{h\in H\;\middle|\;\theta[s]>0\text{ for all non-zero sub-objects }s\hookrightarrow h\right\}

\displaystyle=\quad\left\{h\in H\;\middle|\;\theta_{0}[s]>\alpha_{0}^{\ast}[s]\text{ for all non-zero sub-objects }s\hookrightarrow h\right\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\quad\left\{h\in H\;\middle|\;\theta_{0}[s]>0\text{ for all non-zero sub-objects }s\hookrightarrow h\right\}

\displaystyle=\quad H_{\textnormal{tf}}(\theta_{0}),

but
H_{\textnormal{tf}}(\theta_{0})
is evidently contained in the torsion-free class

\coh(X)\cap H
since
\theta_{0}
lies in
\CC(\coh X)
. The
desired inequality of t-structures can then be read off from the above
containment of torsion-free classes.
∎

§ 4.4 Semistable categories and numerical
hearts

The sub-poset of
numerical tilts of
H
is by
theorem

4.3
the union

\bigcup_{\sigma}\left[H_{\textnormal{tt}}\sigma,H^{\textnormal{tt}}\sigma\right]
of intervals defined by
non-zero heart cones
\sigma
in the heart fan of
H
. Following

theorems

4.9
and
4.11
we thus see
that all numerical tilts of
H=\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
can be enumerated as

\{\Psi_{\nu}H\;|\;\nu\text{ a }\mathfrak{J}\text{-path}\}\;\cup\;\{\Phi_{\nu}H[-1]\;|\;\nu\text{ a }\mathfrak{J}\text{-path}\}\;\cup\bigcup_{\sigma\in\Arr(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})\smallsetminus 0}\;[H_{\textnormal{tt}}\sigma,H^{\textnormal{tt}}\sigma]

and it remains to address the question of when (semi-)geometric hearts are
maximal occupants of their heart cones.

Classic, mixed, and reversed geometric hearts

Considering the heart
cones
\sigma=\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}
associated to geometric hearts
\Psi_{\nu}\coh(\nu X)
, we show that the interval
[H_{\textnormal{tt}}\sigma,H^{\textnormal{tt}}\sigma]
is a boolean
lattice in which the geometric heart is maximal. For this a computation of all

\sigma
-semistable objects in
H
is necessary, this has been accomplished
by Karmazyn
[
Kar17
, §5.2]
and Wemyss
[
Wem18
, §5]
via geometric invariant theory.

Lemma 4.13
.

For a chamber
\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}\in\Chambers(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})
and a generic
vector
\theta\in\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}
, an object of
H=\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
is

\theta
-stable if and only if it is equal to a perverse point sheaf

\Psi_{\nu}{O}_{p}
for some closed point
p
in
\nu X
. In particular,
subcategory of semistable objects associated to
\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}
is given
by the extension closure of such objects, i.e.

H_{\textnormal{ss}}\left(\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}\right)=\left\langle\Psi_{\nu}{O}_{p}\;|\;p\in\nu X(\mathbb{C})\right\rangle.

Proof.

By
[
Wem18
, theorem 2.15]
the category

\zeroPer\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}\cong\mathcal{m\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}\Lambda
can be identified with a subcategory of
representations of a quiver with vertices
\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J}
. Viewing
elements of
\mathfrak{h}(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
as dimension
vectors for this quiver, the identification is such that the dimension
vector of the representation corresponding to
x\in\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
is
precisely its
\KK
-theory class.

Under the above identifications, elements of the dual vector space

\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
naturally give stability parameters (in
the sense of
[
Kin94
]
) on
\zeroPer\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}

and by
[
Wem18
, theorem 5.12]
, the flop
functor
\Psi_{\nu}:\Db(\nu X)\to\Db X
restricts to an equivalence of the
associated semistable categories

\left\{x\in\zeroPer\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{\nu X}{Z}\right)$}}\;|\;x\text{ is }\varphi_{\nu}\kern-1.5pt\check{}\;\theta\text{-semistable }\right\}\longrightarrow\left\{x\in\zeroPer\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}\;|\;x\text{ is }\theta\text{-semistable }.\right\}

It suffices to show that for a generic

\theta\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}
, the
\theta
-stable objects in
\zeroPer\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}

are precisely all the skyscraper sheaves at closed points—in our complete
local setting, such points automatically lie in the
\pi
-exceptional
fiber and thus the skyscrapers are contained in
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
. Given this,
the description of
H_{\textnormal{ss}}\left(\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}\right)
follows by
recalling that any semistable object admits a finite filtration by stable
ones.

Now if an object is
\theta
-semistable, it remains so upon
small perturbations of
\theta
within the hyperplane

\{\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}=0\}
and thus it must have dimension vector

n\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}
for some integer
n>0
. The case
n=1
is addressed by
constructing the coarse moduli space
\mathcal{M}(\theta,\delta_{\mathfrak{J}})
,
which Karmazyn shows is isomorphic to the variety
\nu X
in a way that
each closed point

p\in\nu X
corresponds to the ‘perverse point sheaf’
\Psi_{\nu}{O}_{p}\in\zeroPer\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
. Thus the collection of
\theta
-stable objects in

\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
that have
\KK
-theory class
\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}
is precisely

\left\{{O}_{p}\;|\;p\subset X(\mathbb{C})\right\}
.

We show the case
n\geq 2
does not occur by replicating the argument
of
[
Gar22
, lemma 4.12]
.
Indeed suppose for the sake of contradiction that
x\in\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
is

\theta
-stable and
[x]=n\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}
for
n\geq 2
. Since any
skyscraper sheaf
{O}_{p}\in\zeroPer\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
is
\theta
-stable and
necessarily distinct from
x
, we have
\Hom(x,{O}_{p})=0
for all
p\in X
. Combining this with the fact that
x
is a two-term complex of
coherent sheaves, we see that the sheaf
\textnormal{H}^{0}_{\coh X}(x)
has empty
support and hence
x[-1]
is a sheaf on
X
. Choosing a sufficiently
ample bundle
{L}
on
X
then gives us

\chi({L}\otimes x[-1])\geq 0
, but this is absurd since

x
is numerically equivalent to some skyscraper sheaf
{O}_{p}^{\oplus n}
.
∎

The following is then immediate from
corollary

4.4
and

theorem

4.3
.

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
\nu
be a spherical
\mathfrak{J}
-path, defining a birational model
W=\nu X
and a cone
\sigma=\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}
. An intermediate heart
K\in\tilt(\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}})
has heart cone
\sigma
for some spherical

\mathfrak{J}
-path
\nu
if and only if
K
is a tilt of
\Psi_{\nu}\coh(W)
in a torsion class

\langle\Psi_{\nu}{O}_{p}\;|\;p\in U\rangle

associated to some subset of closed points
U\subset W(\mathbb{C})
. The
correspondence

\left\{K\in\tilt(\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}})\;|\;\CC K=\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}\right\}\longrightarrow\{U\subset\nu X(\mathbb{C})\}

thus obtained is an anti-isomorphism of posets.

In particular
\coh(X)
, corresponding to
U=\varnothing
, is maximal with
heart cone
\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}
, while the minimal such heart
\anticoh(X)
,
corresponding to
U=X(\mathbb{C})
, is the category of two-term complexes

\{x\in\coh X\;|\;x\text{ pure of dimension
}1\}\;\ast\;\{y[-1]\in\coh X[-1]\;|\;\dim\Supp(y)=0\}.

Analogous categories considered in
[
BPPW24
, example
2.6]
were dubbed
reversed geometric hearts
.

Remark 4.15
.

It is a classical fact that
\coh X
is a Noetherian category. On the
other hand, any heart
K\in[\anticoh X,\coh X)
is necessarily
non-Noetherian, since
K
then contains some
{O}_{p}[-1]

(p\in C_{i}\subset X)
and hence the sequence of morphisms

{O}_{C_{i}}\to{O}_{C_{i}}(1)\to{O}_{C_{i}}(2)\to\cdots,

each of which has cone
{O}_{p}\in K[1]
, gives an infinite chain of proper surjections in
K
.

Dually, the heart
\anticoh X
is the only Artinian heart in the interval

[\anticoh X,\coh X]
. Indeed if a morphism
x\to y
is injective in

\anticoh X
with non-zero cokernel
y/x
, then considering cohomologies
with respect to
\coh X
produces a long exact sequence

0\to\underbrace{\textnormal{H}^{0}(x)\to\textnormal{H}^{0}(y)\to\textnormal{H}^{0}(y/x)}_{\text{pure
sheaves with one-dimensional support}}\!\!\longrightarrow\underbrace{\textnormal{H}^{1}(x)\to\textnormal{H}^{1}(y)\to\textnormal{H}^{1}(y/x)}_{\text{sheaves with zero-dimensional support}}\to 0

in
\coh X
. In particular, either
\textnormal{H}^{0}x
has lower rank than

\textnormal{H}^{0}y
on some exceptional curve
C_{i}\subset X
, or
y/x

has zero-dimensional support (i.e.
\textnormal{H}^{0}(y/x)=0
) and the length
of
\textnormal{H}^{1}x
is lower than that of
\textnormal{H}^{1}y
. Thus
\anticoh X
has no infinite descending chains of inclusions
\cdots\hookrightarrow x_{2}\hookrightarrow x_{1}\hookrightarrow x
.

§ 4.5 Many flavours of semi-geometric hearts

We compute the semistable
categories associated to non-maximal cones
\sigma
in the hyperplane

\{\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}=0\}
, which will subsequently furnish a description of the
interval
[H_{\textnormal{tt}}\sigma,H^{\textnormal{tt}}\sigma]
.

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
W=\nu X
be a birational model of
X
, and
\tau\colon W\to Y
a
crepant contraction of some (but not all) exceptional curves in
W
.
Writing
K=\Psi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}
for the corresponding heart in
\Dm X

and picking a generic vector
\theta\in\CC K
, an object
k\in K

satisfies
\theta[k]=0
if and only if it is filtered by the simple
objects of
K
.

Proof.

It suffices to address the case
W=X
since the rest are analogous.
Thus supposing
\tau
contracts the locus
C_{I}=\bigcup_{i\in I}C_{i}

defined by
I\subset\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J}
, we must show

K=\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
satisfies

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline qn:partialperversesemisimples}K_{\textnormal{ss}}(\theta)=\mathcal{p\kern-0.5pte\kern-0.5ptr\kern-1.0pt}_{I}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}\;\;\oplus\bigoplus_{p\in C\setminus C_{I}}\big\langle{O}_{p}\big\rangle

(24)

for
\theta
generic in
\CC K=\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}\cup I}^{0}
. It is
evident that
\theta
vanishes on every simple of
K
, so we address
the converse containment.

Thus suppose
k\in K
satisfies
\theta[k]=0
. By

theorem

3.16
,
k\in\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
is an extension of
the simples of
\mathcal{p\kern-0.5pte\kern-0.5ptr\kern-1.0pt}_{I}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
by some coherent sheaf supported
on the
\tau
-uncontracted curves, so we can reduce to the case where

k
lies in
\coh(X)
and is supported within the closure of

C\setminus C_{I}
.

We show such
k
continues to satisfy
\theta[k]=0
if
\theta

perturbed from the wall
\mathrm{C}^{0}_{\mathfrak{J}\cup I}
into the interior of

\mathrm{C}^{0}_{\mathfrak{J}}
, from whence the result follows from the observation

\left\{x\in\coh(X)\;\middle|\;\dim\Supp(x)=0\right\}\;\overset{(\ref{prop:semistablesonacone})}{=}\;H_{\textnormal{ss}}(\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0})\;\overset{(\ref{thm:allheartsonacone})}{=}\;\left\{x\in H^{\textnormal{tt}}(\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0})\;\middle|\;\theta^{\prime}[x]=0\text{for generic }\theta^{\prime}\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}\right\}.

Thus consider the vector
\theta^{\prime}=\theta+\sum_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}^{\ast}
, where the vectors
(\alpha_{i}^{\ast})_{i\in I}
are
defined as

\alpha_{i}^{\ast}(\delta_{\mathfrak{J}})=0,\qquad\alpha_{i}^{\ast}(\alpha_{j})=\begin{cases}1,&j=i\\
0,&j\in\Delta\setminus(\mathfrak{J}\cup\{i\})\end{cases}.

Evidently
\theta^{\prime}
lies generically in
\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}
. Writing

I^{c}=\Delta\setminus(\mathfrak{J}\cup I)
so that
k
is supported on

C_{I^{c}}=\bigcup_{i\in I^{c}}C_{i}
, note that
k
lies in the thick
subcategory generated by
\mathcal{p\kern-0.5pte\kern-0.5ptr\kern-1.0pt}_{J}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
. It follows that the class
[k]\in\KK X
can
be written as a linear combination of the classes
\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}
and

\alpha_{i}
(
i\in I^{c}
), and consequently
\theta^{\prime}[k]=0
as required.
∎

Consider the crepant contraction
\tau\colon X\to Y
of exceptional curves

C_{I}=\bigcup_{i\in I}C_{i}
, defining the intermediate heart
K=\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}

and the category
K_{\textnormal{ss}}(\theta)
as in (
24
)
for generic
\theta\in\CC K
. The category
K_{\textnormal{ss}}(\theta)
entirely lies
within
H=\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
, and thus
lemma

4.13
shows
K
is
the maximal tilt of
H
with heart cone
\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}\cup I}^{0}
. The minimal
such heart, which we call the
reversed semi-geometric heart

\antizeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
, is the Artinian heart obtained by tilting
K
in the
torsion class
K_{\textnormal{ss}}(\theta)
. To spell this out, writing

I^{c}=\Delta\setminus(\mathfrak{J}\cup I)
and
C_{J}=\bigcup_{i\in I^{c}}C_{i}
we have

\displaystyle\antizeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}

\displaystyle=\left\{x\in\coh X\;\middle|\;\begin{array}[]{l}\Supp(x)\subset C_{I^{c}}\\
x\text{ pure of dim. 1}\end{array}\right\}\;\ast\;\left(\bigoplus_{p\in C\setminus C_{I}}\;\left\langle{O}_{p}[-1]\right\rangle\right)\;\ast\;\mathcal{p\kern-0.5pte\kern-0.5ptr\kern-1.0pt}_{I}\kern-1.0pt{\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}}[-1]

\displaystyle=\big\langle\left\{x\in\anticoh(X)\;|\;\Supp(x)\subset C_{I^{c}}\right\}\;\cup\;\left\{x\in\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}[-1]\;|\;\Supp(x)\subset C_{I}\right\}\big\rangle.

The poset
\{K\in\tilt(H)\;|\;\CC K\supset\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}\cup I}^{0}\}
is
thus equal to the interval
[\antizeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}},\antizeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}]
. The structure
can be described explicitly—decomposing
I=I_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup I_{k}
such
that each
C_{J}=\bigcup_{i\in J}C_{i}
(
J=I_{1},\ldots,I_{k}
) is a connected
component of
C_{I}
, we have the following.

Lemma 4.17
.

Let
\tau\colon X\to Y
be the crepant contraction of
C_{I}=C_{I_{1}}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup C_{I_{k}}
as above. Given
K\in\left[\,\antizeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}},\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}\right]
, each restricted heart

K\cap\Dm X_{J}=\{k\in K\;|\;\Supp(k)\subset C_{J}\}\qquad(J=I_{1},\ldots,I_{k})

is a tilt of
\mathcal{p\kern-0.5pte\kern-0.5ptr\kern-1.0pt}_{J}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
. Further, these
restrictions and the set
\left\{p\in C\setminus C_{I}\;|\;{O}_{p}[-1]\in K\right\}
uniquely determine
K
, and this determines a poset
isomorphism

\left[\,\antizeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}},\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}\,\right]\quad\simeq\quad\prod_{J=I_{1},...,I_{k}}\tilt\left(\mathcal{p\kern-0.5pte\kern-0.5ptr\kern-1.0pt}_{J}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}\right)\;\times\;\textnormal{Bool}\left(C\setminus C_{I}\right).

Here
\textnormal{Bool}(-)
denotes the boolean lattice on closed points, and
the poset on the right hand side is given the product order i.e.
(a_{i})\leq(b_{i})
if and only if
a_{i}\leq b_{i}
for every
i
.

Proof.

By
theorem

4.3
and the discussion following it, hearts

K\in\left[\antizeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}},\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}\right]
are in
bijection with torsion classes
T\subset\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
that satisfy

T\subseteq K_{\textnormal{ss}}\left(\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}\cup I}^{0}\right)
. For such a
T
,

lemma

4.16
induces the decomposition

T=\bigoplus_{J=I_{1},...,I_{k}}\;T\cap\mathcal{p\kern-0.5pte\kern-0.5ptr\kern-1.0pt}_{J}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}\;\;\oplus\;\bigoplus_{p\in C\setminus C_{I}}T\cap\big\langle{O}_{p}\big\rangle,

and each summand in the above decomposition is closed under extensions
and factors (in
K_{\textnormal{ss}}\left(\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}\cup I}^{0}\right)
) since
T

is so. It follows that each
T_{J}=T\,\cap\,\mathcal{p\kern-0.5pte\kern-0.5ptr\kern-1.0pt}_{J}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
is a
torsion class in
\mathcal{p\kern-0.5pte\kern-0.5ptr\kern-1.0pt}_{J}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
, and
K\,\cap\,\Dm X_{J}
is the
tilt of
\mathcal{p\kern-0.5pte\kern-0.5ptr\kern-1.0pt}_{J}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}=\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}\,\cap\,\Dm X_{J}
in

T_{J}=T\,\cap\,\Dm X_{J}
. Further
T
is clearly determined the
summands, i.e. the desired bijection follows.
∎

Note when
C
has two or more irreducible components, each non-zero
non-maximal cone
\sigma\in\Arr(\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J})
is the heart-cone of
multiple hearts of the form
\Psi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{\nu X}{Y}\right)$}}
—to be precise,
one for each birational model
\nu X
such that
\sigma\subset\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}
. Indeed each chamber
\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}
containing
\sigma
uniquely determines a subset
I\subset\Delta\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J}
such that

\varphi_{\nu}\kern-1.5pt\check{}\;\sigma=\mathrm{C}_{\nu\mathfrak{J}\cup I}^{0}
, and the data
(\nu,I)
together determine a partial contraction
\nu X\to Y
such
that
\CC\left(\Psi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{\nu X}{Y}\right)$}}\right)=\sigma
.

Definition 4.18
.

Given a non-zero cone
\sigma\in\Arr(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})
, we say a birational
model
\nu X\in\mathop{\textnormal{}}{Bir}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
is
\sigma
-positive
if
\sigma
is a
face of
\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}=\CC(\Psi_{\nu}\coh(\nu X))
.

Fixing the reference heart
H=\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
, and in particular the model
X
,
endows the set of birational models
\mathop{\textnormal{}}{Bir}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
with a partial order where

W\geq W^{\prime}
if and only if there is a minimal sequence of simple flops from

X
to
W^{\prime}
passing through
W
. This order, which under the natural
bijections
\mathop{\textnormal{}}{Bir}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}\simeq\Chambers(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})\simeq\mathop{\textnormal{}}{MMG}{}^{N}R
corresponds
to the weak order on chambers or the mutation-order on modifying modules, helps
discern when a semi-geometric heart
\Psi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{\nu X}{W}\right)$}}
is the
maximal occupant of its heart cone.

Theorem 4.19
.

Consider a birational model
W=\nu X
of
X
and let
\tau:W\to Y
be
the crepant contraction of a collection of exceptional curves
C_{I}\subset W
. Writing
\sigma\subset\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
for the heart
cone of
\Psi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}
, the following statements are equivalent.

(1)

The birational model
W
is maximal among all
\sigma
-positive
elements of
\mathop{\textnormal{}}{Bir}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
.

(2a)

The heart
\Psi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}
is the supremum (in
\tilt(H)
) of
