\left\{\Psi_{\upsilon}\coh(\upsilon X)\;|\;\sigma\subset\upsilon\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}\right\}
.

(2b)

The heart
\Psi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}
is equal to the numerically
defined tilt
H^{\textnormal{tt}}(\sigma)
.

(3)

The reversed semi-geometric heart
\Psi_{\nu}\antizeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}
is
intermediate with respect to
H
.

(3a)

The heart
\Psi_{\nu}\antizeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}
is the infimum of

\left\{\Psi_{\upsilon}\anticoh(\upsilon X)\;|\;\sigma\subset\upsilon\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}\right\}
.

(3b)

The heart
\Psi_{\nu}\antizeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}
is equal to the numerically
defined tilt
H_{\textnormal{tt}}(\sigma)
.

(4)

For generic
\theta\in\sigma
, an object of
H
is

\theta
-stable if and only if it is a simple of

\Psi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}
.

Further for any non-zero cone
\sigma\in\Arr(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})
, there is a
unique birational model
W=\nu X
and a unique partial contraction

W\to Y
for which
\sigma=\CC(\Psi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}})
and
the above holds.

Before proving the various equivalences we sketch a proof of the final claim
i.e. we show that for any
\sigma\in\Arr(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})
there is a unique
birational model and partial contraction satisfying the said conditions. By the
preceding discussion, each
\sigma
-positive birational
W=\nu X
admits
precisely one crepant contraction
W\to Y
such that

\CC(\Psi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}})=\sigma
and hence it suffices to show that exactly
one
\sigma
-positive
W\in\mathop{\textnormal{}}{Bir}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
can be maximal. But the corresponding
statement is evident for the weak order of cones—perturbing a generic vector
in
\sigma
‘in the direction of’
\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}
picks out the maximal
chamber surrounding
\sigma
, see for example
[
BLSWZ99
, lemma
4.2.12]
.

Returning to the setup of
theorem

4.19
, we now establish the
equivalence of statements

(1)
–
(4)
. The following
equivalences arise from the interplay between numerical tilts and semistable
categories.

Proof of

(2b)
\iff
(4)
\iff
(3b)
.

Considering the heart
K=\Psi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}
and a generic parameter

\theta\in\sigma
, note that the statement
(4)
is
equivalent to
H_{\textnormal{ss}}\theta=K_{\textnormal{ss}}\theta
by

lemma

4.16
. But we know
K
is intermediate
with respect to
H
and has heart cone
\sigma
, so

corollary

4.4
shows that we have

H_{\textnormal{ss}}\theta=K_{\textnormal{ss}}\theta
if and only if
K
is equal to

H^{\textnormal{tt}}\theta
, thus showing

(2b)
\iff
(4)
.a

If
(2b)
(and hence also
(4)
)
is true, then it is immediate that
(3b)
holds, since

\Psi_{\nu}\antizeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}
(i.e. the tilt of
K
in
K_{\textnormal{ss}}\theta
)
must coincide with
H_{\textnormal{tt}}\theta
(i.e. the tilt of
H^{\textnormal{tt}}\theta
in

H_{\textnormal{ss}}\theta
). Conversely suppose

\Psi_{\nu}\antizeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}=H^{\textnormal{tt}}\theta
. Since
K
lies in

[H_{\textnormal{tt}}\theta,H^{\textnormal{tt}}\theta]
, we see that
K\cap H_{\textnormal{tt}}\theta[1]
is a
torsion-free class in
H_{\textnormal{ss}}\theta
and in particular is contained in

H
. But we have

K\cap H_{\textnormal{tt}}\theta[1]=\Psi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}\cap\Psi_{\nu}\antizeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}[1])=K_{\textnormal{ss}}\theta,

so in particular

K_{\textnormal{ss}}\theta\subset H
and hence
corollary

4.4
shows
that both
(2b)
and
(4)
hold.
∎

Likewise, the equivalences

(2a)
\iff
(2b)
and

(3a)
\iff
(3b)
only rely on the
geometry of heart cones. We show how to prove the former, the latter being
similar.

Proof of
(2a)
\iff
(2b)
.

It suffices to show that
H^{\textnormal{tt}}(\sigma)
is the supremum of the given
collection of geometric hearts. If
\sigma
is a face of

\upsilon\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}=\CC(\Psi_{\upsilon}\coh(\upsilon X))
, then
we clearly have
\Psi_{\upsilon}\coh(\upsilon X)\leq H^{\textnormal{tt}}(\sigma)
.

Conversely, choose generic vectors
\theta_{\upsilon}\in\upsilon\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}
for each
\upsilon\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}
containing

\sigma
as a face. Since the fan
\Arr(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})
is induced
from a simplicial hyperplane arrangement, there is a tuple of positive
reals
(\lambda_{\upsilon})
such that the weighted average

\theta=\sum\lambda_{\upsilon}\theta_{\upsilon}
sits
generically in the face
\sigma
. By the construction of numerical
torsion theories, we see that if
h
lies in each torsion class

H_{\text{tr}}(\theta_{\upsilon})=\Psi_{\upsilon}\coh(\upsilon X)[1]\cap H
,
then we also have
h\in H_{\text{tr}}(\theta)=H^{\textnormal{tt}}(\sigma)[1]\cap H
.
It follows that we have
H^{\textnormal{tt}}(\sigma)\leq\mathop{\textnormal{}}{sup}\left\{\Psi_{\upsilon}\coh(\upsilon X)\;|\;\sigma\subset\upsilon\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}\right\}
, and hence equality holds.
∎

Note that the
implication
(3b)
\Rightarrow
(3)

is immediate from definitions, so the following establishes that the
statements
(2a)
–
(4)
are all
equivalent.

Proof of

(3)
\Rightarrow
(4)
.

Suppose we
have
\Psi_{\nu}\antizeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}\subset H[-1,0]
. Since

K=\Psi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}
also lies in
H[-1,0]
, we see
that for generic
\theta\in\sigma
the category

K_{\textnormal{ss}}(\theta)=\Psi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}\cap\Psi_{\nu}\antizeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}[1]

lies in

H
, and hence by
corollary

4.4
and

corollary

3.17
we conclude that every object in

H_{\textnormal{ss}}(\theta)
is filtered by the simples of
K
i.e.
(4)
holds.
∎

It remains to show these are equivalent to
(1)
, which
asserts that the shortest
\mathfrak{J}
-path
\mu
satisfying
\sigma\subset\mu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}
gives a sequence of flops from
X
to
W
(i.e.
W=\mu X
). Unsurprisingly, we leverage our control over compositions of atomic
mutations.

Proof of
(1)
\Rightarrow
(2a)
.

First consider the case when the shortest such path
\mu
is given by the
empty word
\emptyset
, i.e.
W=X
and
\sigma=\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}\cup I}^{0}

is a face of
\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}
. Then for
K=\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
and

\theta\in\sigma
generic, the category
K_{\textnormal{ss}}(\theta)
(as computed in

lemma

4.16
) clearly lies in
H
and thus

corollary

4.4
shows we have
K=H^{\textnormal{tt}}(\theta)
, i.e. statement
(2b)
(and hence also

(2a)
) holds.

Now suppose
W,Y,\sigma
are as in the general situation and
\mu
is
the shortest spherical
\mathfrak{J}
-path satisfying
\sigma\subset\mu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}
, i.e.
\varphi_{\nu}\kern-1.5pt\check{}\;\sigma=\mathrm{C}_{\mu\mathfrak{J}\cup I}
for some
I\subset\mu\mathfrak{J}
. If
(1)
holds, then
we have
W=\mu X
and thus
W\to Y
is the crepant contraction of

C_{I}\subset W
. The reasoning above then shows

\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}=\mathop{\textnormal{}}{sup}\;\left\{\Psi_{\upsilon}\coh(\upsilon W)\;|\;\mathrm{C}_{\mu\mathfrak{J}\cup I}^{0}\subset\upsilon\mathrm{C}_{\mu\mathfrak{J}}^{0}\right\},

and it suffices to restrict the above expression to spherical

\mu\mathfrak{J}
-path
\upsilon
that are atomic. But if
\upsilon
is
atomic then so is
\upsilon\mu
, since every hyperplane crossed by

\upsilon
must contain
\sigma
while the choice of
\mu
ensures
that no hyperplane crossed by it contains
\sigma
. Thus have

\displaystyle\Psi_{\mu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\textnormal{}}{sup}\;\left\{\Psi_{\mu}\circ\Psi_{\upsilon}\coh(\upsilon W)\;|\;\mathrm{C}_{\mu\mathfrak{J}\cup I}^{0}\subset\upsilon\mathrm{C}_{\mu\mathfrak{J}}^{0}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\textnormal{}}{sup}\;\left\{\Psi_{\upsilon\mu}\coh(\upsilon\mu X)\;|\;\sigma\subset\upsilon\mu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}\right\}

To see this is equivalent to the required statement

(2b)
, observe that for ny spherical
\mu\mathfrak{J}
-path

\upsilon
we have
\mathrm{C}^{0}_{\mu\mathfrak{J}\cup I}\subset\upsilon\mathrm{C}_{\mu\mathfrak{J}}^{0}
if and only if
\sigma\subset\upsilon\mu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}
, and every maximal chamber containing
\sigma

as a face can be realised in this way since

\varphi_{\mu}\kern-1.5pt\check{}\;\colon\Arr(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})\to\Arr(\underline{\Delta},\mu\mathfrak{J})
is
an isomorphism of fans.
∎

Proof of

(4)
\Rightarrow
(1)
.

Let
W^{\prime}=\mu X
be the maximal
\sigma
-positive birational model,
admitting the partial contraction
W^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}
such that

\CC\left(\Psi_{\mu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W^{\prime}}{Y^{\prime}}\right)$}}\right)=\sigma
. The statement

(1)
(and hence
(4)
) holds for
this partial contraction by construction, so we see that an object of
H

is
\theta
-stable if and only if it is a simple object of

\Psi_{\mu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W^{\prime}}{Y^{\prime}}\right)$}}
.

Choosing an atomic sequence of flops
\upsilon
from
W^{\prime}
to
W
, we
note that the path
\upsilon
from
\mu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}
to

\upsilon\mu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}=\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}
only crosses hyperplanes
containing
\sigma
while the path
\mu
by definition never passes
through a chamber containing
\sigma
. Thus the composite path

\upsilon\mu
is also atomic and
lemma

3.7
allows us to
write
\Psi_{\nu}=\Psi_{\mu}\circ\Psi_{\upsilon}
.

Suppose
(4)
also holds for
W\to Y
, i.e. an object of

H
is
\theta
-stable if and only if it is a simple of

\Psi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}
. Thus we have the equality of sets

\left\{\Psi_{\mu}^{-1}h\;|\;h\in H\text{ $\theta$-stable}\right\}=\left\{s\;|\;s\in\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W^{\prime}}{Y^{\prime}}\right)$}}\text{ simple}\right\}=\left\{\Psi_{\upsilon}t\;|\;t\in\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}\text{ simple}\right\}.

We need to show
W=W^{\prime}
, or equivalently that

\upsilon
is the empty path. If not, it has length
\geq 1
so can be
written as
\upsilon=\nu_{i}\cdot\upsilon^{\prime}
for some
\mu\mathfrak{J}
-path

\upsilon^{\prime}
and
i\in\Delta\setminus\upsilon^{\prime}\mu\mathfrak{J}
. In other words,
the final curve flopped by
\upsilon
is
C_{i}\subset\upsilon^{\prime}W^{\prime}
, which
has proper transform
C_{\iota(i)}\subset W
.

Further the wall
\upsilon^{\prime}\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}\cap\nu_{i}\upsilon^{\prime}\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}

contains the cone
\sigma
, so we must have
\iota(i)\in I
. Thus the
curve
C_{\iota(i)}\subset W
is contracted by
\tau:W\to Y
.

In particular the object
t={O}_{C_{\iota(i)}}(-1)
is simple in

\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}
, and tracking it across the flop functors using

[
Wem18
, lemma 4.15]
we see that

\displaystyle\Psi_{\upsilon}(t)\;=\;\Psi_{\upsilon^{\prime}}\circ\Psi_{i}({O}_{C_{\iota(i)}}(-1))\;=\;\Psi_{\upsilon^{\prime}}\left({O}_{C_{i}}(-1)\right)[-1]\;\in\;\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W^{\prime}}{Z}\right)$}}[-2,-1].

But all simples of
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W^{\prime}}{Y^{\prime}}\right)$}}
must lie in

\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W^{\prime}}{Z}\right)$}}
, this furnishes the desired contradiction.
∎

This concludes our analysis of the non-maximal heart cones in

\HFan(\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}})
, and thus of all numerical tilts of
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
. In
the next section we examine local and global properties of the partial order to
conclude that all tilts of
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
are numerical.

§ 5 Covering and limiting relations

With the goal of showing that every heart in
\tilt(\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}})
is detected
by
\KK
-theory—thus proving
Theorem

C

1
—we now examine
covering relations in the poset of t-structures and the dynamics of
\Pic(\nu X)
-actions.

A happy consequence of the study of covering relations is the classification of
bricks in
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
. To explain this, as well as to point out the peculiar
behaviours that (non-)widely generated torsion classes in
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}

exhibit (see discussion following
remark

5.5
), we first recall standard
facts in the lattice theory of torsion classes in an algebraic category.

§ 5.1 The lattice theory of torsion classes

Let
{T}
be a triangulated category with a
t-structure whose heart
H\subset{T}
is algebraic, i.e. is an Artinian
and Noetherian Abelian category with finitely many simple objects. We have the
poset
\tilt(H)
of t-structures in
{T}
intermediate with respect
to
H
, the poset
\tors(H)
of torsion classes in
H
, and the poset

\torf(H)
of torsion-free classes in
H
—these are related by the
order-preserving (or order-reversing) bijections (
15
).

The hypotheses on
H
guarantee that each poset is a
complete
lattice
, i.e. admits arbitrary infima (meets, or greatest lower bounds) and suprema
(joins, or least upper bounds). Indeed a subcategory of an Artinian heart is a
torsion-free class if and only if it is closed under taking extensions and
sub-objects, and thus
\torf(H)
has infima since an arbitrary intersection of
torsion-free classes remains a torsion-free class. The existence of suprema is
dual.

Brick labelling

Recall that we say
a

covers

b

(written
a\gtrdot b
) in a partially ordered set if
a>b
and there is no
element
c
satisfying
a>c>b
. The
Hasse quiver
of a poset has
vertices given by the elements, and an arrow
a\to b
for each covering
relation
a\gtrdot b
. The join and meet operations on lattices of torsion
classes are semidistributive so their Hasse quivers are naturally labelled by
certain indecomposable objects
[
BCZ19
]
, we
now describe the construction.

Definition 5.1
.

An object
b\in H
is a
brick
if all non-zero endomorphisms of

b
are invertible. A
semibrick

S\subset H
is a set of bricks that are
pairwise orthogonal, i.e. each
b\in S
is a brick, and whenever

b_{1},b_{2}\in S
are non-isomorphic bricks, we have
\Hom(b_{1},b_{2})=0
.

By Schur’s lemma, every simple object in
H
is a brick and the collection of all
simples forms a semibrick. It is not hard to see that if
s\in H
is simple, then
T=\langle s\rangle
is a minimal non-zero torsion
class in
H
(i.e. the relation
0\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\subset$\cr\hbox{$\cdot\mkern 5.0mu$}\cr}}}T
is covering in
\tors(H)
),
and any torsion-free class covering
0
must be of this form. Likewise every
maximal proper torsion class (i.e. a torsion class
U
such that
the relation
U\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\subset$\cr\hbox{$\cdot\mkern 5.0mu$}\cr}}}H
is covering) is precisely of the form

{}^{\perp}s
for some simple object
s\in H
. Intermediate hearts arising from
such torsion theories are called
simple tilts
, and all covering relations
arise in this way.

Theorem 5.2

(
[
DIRRT23
, theorems 3.3–3.4]
)
.

If
K^{\prime}\lessdot K
is a covering relation in
\tilt(H)
, then
(K\cap H)\setminus K^{\prime}
contains a unique brick
b
which we call the

brick-label
of the covering relation. This brick is a simple object
of
K
, and the corresponding simple tilt in the torsion class
\langle b\rangle\in\tors(K)
is
K^{\prime}
.

More generally if
K^{\prime}<K
are intermediate t-structures with respect to

H
then the set of bricks in
(K\cap H)\setminus K^{\prime}
is non-empty and
equals the set of brick-labels of covering relations in the interval

[K^{\prime},K]\subset\tilt(H)
.

Thus each arrow in the Hasse quiver of
\tilt(H)
is labelled by a unique
brick in
H
, and each brick in
H
arises as the brick-label of at least
one such arrow.

The posets
\tors(H)
and
\torf(H)
inherit this labelling. In particular
if the covering relation
T^{\prime}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\supset$\cr\hbox{$\cdot\mkern 5.0mu$}\cr}}}T
in
\tors(H)
has brick-label

b
, then
b
is the unique brick in
T^{\prime}\setminus T
and we have
T^{\prime}\cap T{}^{\perp}=\langle b\rangle
,
T=({}^{\perp}b)\,\ast\,T^{\prime}
, and
T^{\prime}=T\ast\langle b\rangle
. The analogous statement holds for covering relations of
torsion-free classes.

Widely generated torsion theories

We review how the lattice of torsion classes can be employed to
study the set
\sbrick(H)
of semibricks in
H
. Note that if
K\subset H[-1,0]
is an intermediate heart, then every simple object of
K
lies in a
single cohomological degree with respect to
H
. Thus writing
\simp(K)
for
the set of simple objects of
K
, we see that
\simp(K)[1]\cap H
is a
semibrick in
H
, and this semibrick is contained in the torsion class
associated to
K
. This defines a map
\simp(-)[1]\cap H:\tilt(H)\to\sbrick(H)
, equivalently a map
\tors(H)\to\sbrick(H)
which maps any
torsion class to a semibrick inside it.

On the other hand each
S\in\sbrick(H)
defines a torsion-free class
F=S{}^{\perp}
, with corresponding torsion class
T={}^{\perp}(S{}^{\perp})
characterised by
the property of being minimal among all torsion classes containing
S
. We say
the torsion class
T
in this case is
generated by
S
. This defines
a map
\sbrick(H)\to\tors(H)
, equivalently a map
\sbrick(H)\to\tilt(H)

which we show is a section of
\simp(-)[1]\cap H
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Suppose
H=T\ast F
is a torsion pair such that the torsion class
T
is
generated by a semibrick
S\subseteq H
. Writing
K=F\ast T[-1]
for the
corresponding tilt, then an object
k\in K\cap H[-1]
is simple in
K
if
and only if it lies in
S[-1]
.

Proof.

Given
k\in S[-1]
, suppose there is an injection

k^{\prime}\hookrightarrow k
in
K
, with
k^{\prime}\neq 0
. Since
T[-1]\subset K
is a
torsion-free class,
k^{\prime}
also lies in
T[-1]
. Thus the
object
k^{\prime}[1]\in T
is filtered by objects in
S
and their factors
in
H

[
MS17
, lemma 3.1]
. In particular, there is some

s\in S
with a non-zero morphism
s\to k^{\prime}[1]
.

By injectivity of
k^{\prime}\hookrightarrow k
, the composite map
s[-1]\to k^{\prime}\to k

is non-zero. Since
S
is a semibrick, it follows that
s[-1]\cong k

and this is a splitting of the injection
k^{\prime}\hookrightarrow k
. But
k
is
indecomposable (since
k[1]
is a brick), so the map
k^{\prime}\to k
is an
isomorphism. Thus
k
has no non-trivial sub-objects in
K
, i.e.
k
is simple as required.

Conversely suppose
k\in K\cap H[-1]
is simple in
K
. It follows
that
k[1]
is the quotient (in
H
) of some
s\in S
. In other
words, there is an
h\in H
and an exact triangle
h\to s\to k[1]\to h[1]
. Claim
h=0
, so that
k[1]=s
lies in
S
as
required.

To prove the claim, first note that the triangle
s[-1]\to k\to h\to s

shows that
h
cannot be a non-zero object in
K
. Thus if
h
is
non-zero, then considering the torsion part of
h
shows that there is
a non-zero composite map
s^{\prime}\to h\to s
for some
s^{\prime}\in S
. But

S
is a semibrick, so a similar argument as above shows that the map

h\to s
is an isomorphism and
k=0
, which is a contradiction.
∎

It follows that assigning a semibrick
S\subseteq H
to the the minimal
torsion class it generates gives an injective map
\sbrick(H)\to\tors(H)
.
The following proposition provides lattice-theoretic and homological
characterisations of the image of this map, and provides alternative ways to
read off a semibrick
S
from the torsion class it generates.

Proposition 5.4
.

Given a torsion pair
H=T\ast F
with corresponding tilt
K=F\ast T[-1]
, the following are equivalent.

1.

The torsion class
T
is generated by a semibrick
S\subseteq H
.

2.

The interval
[0,T]\subset\tors(H)
is
coatomic
, i.e. for every
U\in[0,T)
there is a
U^{\prime}\in[0,T)
with

U\subseteq U^{\prime}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\subset$\cr\hbox{$\cdot\mkern 5.0mu$}\cr}}}T
.

3.

In
K
, every non-zero object has a simple sub-object.

If the above statements hold, then the semibrick
S
is uniquely determined as

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=\{b\in H\;|\;b\text{ is the brick-label of a relation
$U\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\subset$\cr\hbox{$\cdot\mkern 5.0mu$}\cr}}}T$ in }\tors(H)\}

\displaystyle=\{b\in H\;|\;b[-1]\text{ is a simple object of }K\}.

We have seen (
lemma

5.3
) that if
1
holds,
then the semibrick
S
which generates
T
is unique and is given by

\simp(K)[1]\cap H
. The
equivalence
1
\iff
2
is the content of
[
AP22
, theorem 7.2]
, which also shows that in this case

T
is generated by the set of brick labels of relations
U\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\subset$\cr\hbox{$\cdot\mkern 5.0mu$}\cr}}}T
.
Since this set is a semibrick (see theorem 4.2
ibid.
), it must coincide
with
S
.

It thus remains to show the equivalence
1
\iff
3
, which we now do.

Proof of

proposition

5.4

1
\Rightarrow
3
.

Suppose
T
is generated by a semibrick
S
, and
k\in K
is a
non-zero object with no simple sub-object. In particular any sub-object

k^{\prime}\hookrightarrow k
shares this property (i.e.
k^{\prime}
has no simple sub-object),
and there is at least one such proper non-zero sub-object
k^{\prime}
.

Now no object of
S\subset\simp(K)
can map to
k
, so
k
lies
in
T[-1]{}^{\perp}
. Thus neither
k
nor
k^{\prime}
lie in
T[-1]
,
so passing to sub-objects if necessary, we may in fact assume
k^{\prime}
and

k
lie in
F
. But
F\subset K
is a torsion class, so the
cokernel
k/k^{\prime}
of this morphism also lies in
F
. It follows that the
map
k^{\prime}\to k
is also a proper injection in
H
.

But repeating the argument produces a chain of proper injections

\cdots\hookrightarrow k^{\prime\prime}\hookrightarrow k^{\prime}\hookrightarrow k
in
H
, which is a contradiction since

H
is Artinian. Thus every non-zero
k\in K
necessarily has a
simple sub-object.
∎

Proof of

proposition

5.4

3
\Rightarrow
1
.

Suppose
3
holds. We show that any torsion-free
class that is larger than
F
must contain some
element of the semibrick
S=\simp(K)[1]\cap H
. It follows that
T

is the minimal torsion class containing
S
, i.e.
T
is generated
by
S
as required.

Thus consider any torsion pair
H=T\ast F
such that
F\subsetneq F^{\prime}
. Thus
T\cap F^{\prime}
is a non-zero torsion-free class in the Abelian
category
K[1]=F[1]\ast(T\cap T^{\prime})\ast(T\cap F^{\prime})
, in particular

T\cap F^{\prime}
is closed under taking sub-objects in this category. But by
hypothesis on
K
(equivalently
K[1]
), any non-zero object in
T\cap F^{\prime}
has a simple sub-object which therefore also lies in
T\cap F^{\prime}
.
Thus
F^{\prime}
has non-trivial intersection with the set
\simp(K[1])\cap H=S
, as required.
∎

The obvious dual statements to

lemmas

5.3
and
5.4
hold. In particular if

H=T\ast F
is a torsion pair, then
F
is generated by a semibrick
S
if
and only if every non-zero object in the tilt
K=F\ast T[-1]
has a simple
factor, and in this case the semibrick generating
F
is determined as

S=\simp(K)\cap H
.

Remark 5.5
.

If
W\subseteq H
is a
wide
subcategory (i.e.
W
is closed
under extensions, kernels, and cokernels, and is therefore Abelian),
then the set of simples of
W
is evidently a semibrick of
H
.
Ringel
[
Rin76
, §1.2]
shows that
every wide subcategory of
H
is in fact the extension-closure of its
simples, and conversely the extension closure of any semibrick is a wide
subcategory. Thus torsion(-free) classes in
H
that are generated by a
semibrick are called
widely generated

[
Asa20
,
AP22
,
BCZ19
,
MS17
]
.

By
proposition

5.4
and its dual, any torsion
(torsion-free) class in
H
associated to an Artinian (resp. Noetherian) tilted heart is widely generated. On the other hand a torsion pair

H=T\ast F
is such that both
T
and
F
are widely generated then it is
straightforward to show that the tilt
K=F\ast T[-1]
is Artinian if and only if it is
Noetherian. Thus a torsion pair
H=T\ast F
can, in theory, exhibit seven
behaviours with regards to whether or not
T
and
F
are widely generated,
and whether
K=F\ast T[-1]
is Artinian or Noetherian or neither.

All seven behaviours can be observed in the setting of a flopping contraction

\pi\colon X\to Z
and its standard algebraic heart
H=\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
. Indeed
by
remark

4.15
we have the following.

Now consider a heart
K\in[\anticoh X,\coh X]
associated to a set of
closed points
U\subseteq X(\mathbb{C})
as in

proposition

4.14
. All simples of
K
arise as skyscrapers at
closed points, hence the simple sub-objects of any
k\in K
are given as

\{{O}_{p}[-1]\;|\;p\in\Supp(k)\cap U\}
while its simple quotients are

\{{O}_{p}\;|\;p\in\Supp(k)\setminus U\}
. Thus if we additionally assume that
the
\pi
-exceptional fiber has
n\geq 3
integral components

C_{1},C_{2},\ldots,C_{n}
(indexed such that
C_{1}\cap C_{n}=\emptyset
), then
choosing closed points
p_{i}\in C_{i}
(
i=1,\ldots,n
) allows the construction
of hearts in
H[-1,0]
which exhibit the remaining four behaviours.
