§ 5.2 Simple tilts of length hearts

Following
[
HW18
, §5]
, we will show that
simple tilts of algebraic hearts in
\tilt(\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}})
arise precisely from
simple mutation functors.

Recall that the algebraic tilts of
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
are
all given by
\Psi_{\nu}H
or
\Phi_{\nu}H[-1]
, where we write

H=\mathcal{f\kern-0.5ptl\kern-1.0ptm\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}({}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\nu})
for the standard heart in the category

\Dfl({}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\nu})
associated to a modifying
R
-module
M=\nu N
.
Each indecomposable summand
M_{i}\subset M
(
i\in\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathbb{J}(M)
) then gives an indecomposable projective

(\End_{R}M)
-module
P_{i}=\Hom_{R}(M,M_{i})
, and hence a dual simple module

S_{i}\in H
.

We show that the intermediate hearts
\Phi_{i}H[-1]
and

\Psi_{i}H
are precisely the simple tilts of
H
in
S_{i}
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
M=\nu N
and
H=\mathcal{f\kern-0.5ptl\kern-1.0ptm\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}({}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\nu})
be as above. For any

i\in\underline{\Delta}\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J}
, we have
\langle S_{i}\rangle=\Psi_{i}H[1]\cap H=\Phi_{i}H[-1]\cap H
. In other words,
\Psi_{i}H
is the tilt of

H
in the torsion class
\langle S_{i}\rangle
, while
\Phi_{i}H[-1]
is
the tilt of
H
in the torsion-free class
\langle S_{i}\rangle
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that
\Phi_{i}H[-1]\cap H=\langle S_{i}\rangle
; the
analogous statement for
\Psi_{i}
follows by applying the functor

\Phi_{i}^{-1}=\Psi_{\iota(i)}
(
lemma

3.6
) and
noting that
\Psi_{\iota(i)}S_{i}=S_{\iota(i)}[-1]

[
HW18
, lemma 5.3]
. For ease of notation we state the proof for
M=N
.

Write
H=T_{i}\ast F_{i}
for the torsion pair defined by
\Phi_{i}H\in H[-1,0]
, noting
the torsion and torsion-free classes are defined in terms of Hom- or
Ext-orthogonality with respect to the tilting module

{}_{\nu_{i}}\Lambda
as in (
17
).

Now
{}_{\nu_{i}}\Lambda
is a mutation of
\Lambda=\bigoplus_{i\in\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J}}P_{j}
at an indecomposable summand, so there is an
exact sequence

0\to\Lambda\to P^{\prime}\to{}_{\nu_{i}}\Lambda\to 0

with
P^{\prime}\in\mathop{\text{}}{add}\{P_{j}\;|\;j\neq i\}
. Since

\Hom(P_{j},S_{i})=0
for
j\neq i
, the exact sequence gives us

\Hom({}_{\nu_{i}}\Lambda,S_{i})=0
showing
S_{i}\in T_{i}
. On the other hand if
x\in{}^{\perp}\{S_{i}\}
then there is a
projective cover
P\to x\to 0
with
P\in\mathop{\text{}}{add}\{P_{j}\;|\;j\neq i\}
, so noting that

{}_{\nu_{i}}\Lambda
has projective dimension
\leq 1
we get an
exact sequence
\Ext^{1}({}_{\nu_{i}}\Lambda,P)\to\Ext^{1}({}_{\nu_{i}}\Lambda,x)\to 0
. But
P\in\mathop{\text{}}{add}({}_{\nu_{i}}\Lambda)
and
{}_{\nu_{i}}\Lambda
has
no self-extensions, so in fact all the terms in the
sequence must vanish and we have
x\in T_{i}
. Thus we have shown

{}^{\perp}\{S_{i}\}\subseteq T_{i}
and
\langle S_{i}\rangle\subseteq F_{i}
,
and it is clear that equality must hold.
∎

The following is then immediate from
theorem

5.2
and

lemma

3.6
.

Proposition 5.7
.

If
\nu
is a
\mathfrak{J}
-path and
i\in\underline{\Delta}\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J}
is such
that
\nu N>\nu_{i}\nu N
, then the poset
\tilt(\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}})
has
relations

\Psi_{\nu}H>\Psi_{\nu_{i}\nu}H,\qquad\Phi_{\nu_{i}\nu}H[-1]>\Phi_{\nu}H[-1].

These are covering and have brick labels
\Phi_{\nu}S_{i}
and
\Psi_{\nu}S_{i}
respectively.
Further, every covering relation in
\tilt(\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}})
which involves an algebraic
heart is of the above form.

It follows that the sub-posets of
\tilt(H)
given by

\displaystyle\tilt^{+}(H)

\displaystyle=\{\Psi_{\nu}H\phantom{[-1]}\;|\;\nu\text{ a positive $\mathfrak{J}$-path}\},

\displaystyle\tilt^{-}(H)

\displaystyle=\{\Phi_{\nu}H[-1]\;|\;\nu\text{ a positive $\mathfrak{J}$-path}\}

are respectively isomorphic and anti-isomorphic to the poset of chambers

\Chambers(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})
, and inherit its properties outlined in

lemma

2.8
. Further, it is evident that any interval in

\tilt(H)
whose end points lie in
\tilt^{+}(H)
(
\tilt^{-}(H)
) must entirely lie in

\tilt^{+}(H)
(resp.
\tilt^{-}(H)
)—that is to say if
K
is a heart
satisfying
\Psi_{\nu}H\geq K\geq\Psi_{\nu^{\prime}}H
for some
\mathfrak{J}
-paths

\nu,\nu^{\prime}
, then there is a
\mathfrak{J}
-path
\nu^{\prime\prime}
such that

K=\Psi_{\nu^{\prime\prime}}H
.

§ 5.3 Dynamics of the nef monoid

The Hasse quiver of algebraic hearts, in principle, should say
all one wants to hear about the poset
\tilt(H)
. Lattice theoretic analyses
of affine weak orders however remain elusive in practice. Fortunately, our
understanding of the global structure of
\tilt(H)
is greatly aided by the
presence of the natural actions of various Picard groups—these play a role
identical to that of coweight lattices in the study of Bruhat orders.

To spell this out, note that tensoring by any line bundle
{L}\in\Pic X

naturally gives an autoequivalence of
\Dm X
. We focus on the induced action on the poset
of t-structures, where a line bundle
{L}\in\Pic X
thus twists a heart
K\subset\Dm X
to

{L}\otimes K=\left\{{L}\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}_{X}k\;|\;k\in K\right\}.

In what follows we study the orbit of
H=\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
in relation to the poset

\tilt(H)
, setting up notation and stating key results. Proofs will
follow (and rely on) a discussion of how these actions are observed on

\KK
-theory.

Certain twists of
H
can be described explicitly—namely, if we write

{L}_{i}=\det({N}_{i})
(
i\in\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J}
) for elements of the
standard basis of
\Pic X
with indexing as in

lemma

3.2
, then we have the following.

Proposition 5.8
.

For any
i\in\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J}
and
n>0
, the following statements hold.

1.

The heart
({L}_{i}\check{}\,)^{\otimes n}\otimes H
is intermediate
with respect to
H
, and is the
tilt of
H
in the smallest torsion class containing

{O}_{C_{i}}(-n-1)[1]
.

2.

The heart
{L}_{i}^{\otimes n}\otimes H[-1]
is intermediate with
respect to
H
, and is the tilt of
H
in the smallest
torsion-free class containing
{O}_{C_{i}}(n-2)
.

If we denote by
\tau\colon X\to X_{i}
the crepant partial contraction of

\bigcup_{j\in\Delta\setminus\{i\}}C_{j}
(i.e. of all exceptional curves

except

C_{i}
), then it is clear from the above description that we have

\begin{array}[]{rclclclcl}H&>&{L}_{i}\check{}\,\otimes H&>&({L}_{i}\check{}\,)^{\otimes 2}\otimes H&>&\cdots&>&\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X\phantom{{}_{i}}}{X_{i}}\right)$}},\\
H[-1]&<&{L}_{i}\otimes H[-1]&<&{L}_{i}^{\otimes 2}\otimes H[-1]&<&\cdots&<&\antizeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X\phantom{{}_{i}}}{X_{i}}\right)$}}.\end{array}

Proposition 5.9
.

For any
i\in\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J}
, the torsion class
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X\phantom{{}_{i}}}{X_{i}}\right)$}}[1]\cap H\in\tors(H)
is generated by
\{{O}_{C_{i}}(n)[1]\;|\;n\leq-2\}
while the torsion-free class
\antizeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X\phantom{{}_{i}}}{X_{i}}\right)$}}\cap H\in\torf(H)
is generated by
\{{O}_{C_{i}}(n)\;|\;n\geq-1\}
. In other
words, in the poset
\tilt(H)
we have

\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X\phantom{{}_{i}}}{X_{i}}\right)$}}=\mathop{\textnormal{}}{inf}\;\left\{({L}_{i}\check{}\,)^{\otimes n}\otimes H\;|\;n\geq 0\right\},\qquad\antizeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X\phantom{{}_{i}}}{X_{i}}\right)$}}=\mathop{\textnormal{}}{sup}\;\left\{{L}_{i}^{\otimes n}\otimes H[-1]\;|\;n\geq 0\right\}.

In fact we realise many more geometric and semi-geometric hearts as limits of
twists by line bundles on birational models. To explain this, note that if

W=\nu X
is a birational model then the natural action of
\Pic W
on

\Dm W
can be translated across the flop functor
\Psi_{\nu}
to an action on

\Dm X
. For brevity we continue to denote this action

\Pic W\circlearrowright\Dm X
by
\;\otimes\;
, i.e. for
{L}\in\Pic(W)
and
h\in\Dm X
we write

{L}\otimes h=\Psi_{\nu}\left({L}\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}_{W}\Psi_{\nu}^{-1}h\right).

Now
\Pic W
sits as the lattice of integral points in the vector space

\RealPic(W)
, which we identify (as in
4.3
) with the
hyperplane
\{\theta\in\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})\;|\;\theta(\delta_{\mathfrak{J}})=0\}
. Cones in this hyperplane cut submonoids of

\Pic W
, in particular we can consider the various monoid actions
(\Pic W\cap\sigma)\circlearrowright\Dm X
determined by the choice of a birational model

W\in\mathop{\textnormal{}}{Bir}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
and a cone
\sigma\in\Arr(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})
.

The birational model
W
is
\sigma
-positive (
definition

4.18
)
precisely when
\Pic W\cap\sigma
lies in the nef cone.
For such
\sigma
and
W
we can consider the
(\Pic W\cap\sigma)
-orbit of
the reference heart
H=\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Y}\right)$}}
. The following result shows that the orbit
is independent of the choice of
\sigma
-positive birational model, lies in

\tilt(H)
, and limits to the maximal (semi-)geometric heart determined by

\sigma
.

Theorem 5.10
.

Given a sequence of flops
\nu
from
X
with corresponding
birational model
W=\nu X
, the following statements hold in
\Dm X
.

1.

For any
{L}\in\Pic W
, we have
H\geq{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H
in the poset of t-structures on
\Dm X
if and only if

{L}
is nef, if and only if
{L}\otimes H[-1]\geq H[-1]
. When
these conditions hold, we have

H\;\geq\;{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H\;>\;\Psi_{\nu}\coh(W)\;>\;\Psi_{\nu}\anticoh W\;>\;{L}\otimes H[-1]\;\geq\;H[-1].

2.

Let
\sigma\in\Arr(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})
be a a cone such that
W
is

\sigma
-positive. Then

\displaystyle H^{\textnormal{tt}}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\textnormal{}}{inf}\,\left\{{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H\phantom{[-1]}\;|\;{L}\in\Pic W\cap\sigma\right\},

\displaystyle H_{\textnormal{tt}}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\textnormal{}}{sup}\,\left\{{L}\otimes H[-1]\;|\;{L}\in\Pic W\cap\sigma\right\}.

3.

Suppose
W^{\prime}=\nu^{\prime}X
is another birational model, and the line
bundles
{L}\in\Pic W
and
{L}^{\prime}\in\Pic W^{\prime}
coincide as
elements of
\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
.
If both
{L}
and
{L}^{\prime}
are nef, then

{L}^{\prime}\,\check{}\,\otimes H={L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H
and
{L}^{\prime}\otimes H[-1]={L}\otimes H[-1]
.

4.

Let
K\in\tilt(H)
be of the form
{L}^{\prime}\,\check{}\,\otimes H
or

{L}^{\prime}\otimes H[-1]
for some line bundle
{L}^{\prime}
on some
birational model
W^{\prime}
. If we have an inequality
K\leq\coh(W)

or
K\geq\anticoh(W)
, then there is an
{L}\in\Pic W
such
that
K={L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H
or
K={L}\otimes H[-1]
.

Remark 5.11
.

For birational models
W
and
W^{\prime}
of
X
, the line bundles
{L}\in\Pic W
and

{L}^{\prime}\in\Pic W^{\prime}
coincide as elements of

\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
if and only if they are proper transforms
of each other across the birational map
W\dashrightarrow W^{\prime}
. The
decomposition of
\{\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}=0\}\subset\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})

into chambers
\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}
and their faces then coincides with the
decomposition of
\RealPic(X)
into nef cones of birational models, the
so-called
decomposition of the movable cone
that recovers the
Kawamata–Kollár–Mori–Reid decomposition

[
Kaw88
,
Kol89
,
Mor82
,
Rei
]
when
X
is
a minimal model (i.e.
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial).

Given any non-zero cone

\sigma\in\Arr(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})
,
theorem

4.19
gives a
birational model
W=\nu X
and a partial contraction
W\to Y
such that

H^{\textnormal{tt}}(\sigma)=\Psi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}
. Then the monoid
\Pic W\cap\sigma

can be naturally identified with the nef cone of
Y
, and the limit of the
twists
{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H
ranging over
{L}\in\Pic W\cap\sigma
is
precisely the heart
\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Y}\right)$}}
which is geometric on the curves ‘detected
by’
\Pic W\cap\sigma
. In particular the limit of all twists

\{{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H\;|\;{L}\in\Pic W\text{ nef}\}
is the geometric
heart
\Psi_{\nu}\coh W
, a ‘fixed point’ of the action of
\Pic W
.

Global order on Picard orbits

Suppose
W=\nu X
is a birational model of
X
with
exceptional curves
C_{i}

(i\in\Delta\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J})
. The
intersection pairing with these
1
-cycles gives an isomorphism

\deg\colon\Pic W\to\mathbb{Z}^{(\Delta\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J})}
.

We endow
\mathbb{Z}^{(\Delta\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J})}
with
the product order (i.e. for tuples
(a_{i}),(b_{i})\in\mathbb{Z}^{(\Delta\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J})}
we have
(a_{i})\leq(b_{i})
if and only if

a_{i}\leq b_{i}
for each
i\in\Delta\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J}
) and write

\underline{0},\underline{1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{(\Delta\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J})}
for the tuples whose
entries are all
0
or all
1
respectively. Thus for example a line bundle

{L}\in\Pic W
is nef if and only if
\underline{0}\leq\deg({L})
, and two line
bundles
{L}_{1},{L}_{2}\in\Pic W
satisfy
\deg({L}_{1})\leq\deg({L}_{2})
if and
only if
{L}_{2}\otimes{L}_{1}\,\check{}\,
is nef.

The partial order on various
\Pic W
-twists and shifts of
H=\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
can then be described as
follows.

Corollary 5.12
.

Given line bundles
{L}_{1},{L}_{2}
on some birational model
W\in\mathop{\textnormal{}}{Bir}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
and
integers
n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have
{L}_{1}\otimes H[n_{1}]\geq{L}_{2}\otimes H[n_{2}]
in the poset of t-structures on
\Dm X
if and only if either

n_{1}>n_{2}
, or
n_{1}=n_{2}
and
\deg({L}_{1})\geq\deg({L}_{2})
. Thus we have an isomorphism of posets

\{{L}\otimes H[n]\;|\;{L}\in\Pic W,\;n\in\mathbb{Z}\}\quad\simeq\quad\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}^{(\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J})}

where
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}^{(\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J})}
is ordered
lexicographically on its two factors.

Proof.

If
n_{1}=n_{2}
, then it is clear that
{L}_{1}\otimes H[n_{1}]\geq{L}_{2}\otimes H[n_{1}]
if and only if
H\geq{L}_{1}\check{}\,\otimes{L}_{2}\otimes H
, which
by
theorem

5.10

1
occurs if and
only if
\deg({L}_{1})\geq\deg({L}_{2})
.

Thus to conclude it suffices to show
{L}_{1}\otimes H>{L}_{2}\otimes H[-1]
for all
{L}_{1},{L}_{2}\in\Pic W
. Now we may write
{L}_{1}={L}_{-}\check{}\,\otimes{L}_{+}
where the line bundles
{L}_{+},{L}_{-}\in\Pic W
are nef.
Theorem

5.10

1

shows there are inequalities
{L}_{+}\otimes H[-1]\geq H[-1]
and

{L}_{-}\check{}\,\otimes H>\Psi_{\nu}\coh W
. Applying the functor

{L}_{-}\check{}\,\otimes(-)[1]
to the first inequality thus yields

{L}_{1}\otimes H>\Psi_{\nu}\coh W
, and a similar argument gives

\Psi_{\nu}\anticoh W>{L}_{2}\otimes H[-1]
.
∎

Restricting to the interval of intermediate hearts
\left[H[-1],H\right]
, we
see that

\left\{K\in\tilt(H)\;\middle|\;\begin{array}[]{l}K={L}\otimes H\text{
or }{L}\otimes H[-1]\text{ for}\\
\text{some }W\in\mathop{\textnormal{}}{Bir}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}},\;{L}\in\Pic W\end{array}\right\}

decomposes into a union of sub-posets isomorphic to
(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{|\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J}|}\sqcup(\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0})^{|\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J}|}

indexed over
\mathop{\textnormal{}}{Bir}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
. This highly regular sub-poset of
\tilt(H)
provides
anchors to track the remaining intermediate algebraic hearts.

Theorem 5.13
.

For any algebraic heart
K\in\tilt(H)
, there is a birational model
W
of
X
and line bundles
{L}_{1},{L}_{2}\in\Pic W
with degrees

\underline{0}\;\leq\;\deg({L}_{1})\;<\;\deg({L}_{2})\;\leq\;\deg({L}_{1})+\underline{1}

such that one of the inequalities below holds:

{L}_{1}\check{}\,\otimes H\;\geq\;K\;>\;{L}_{2}\check{}\,\otimes H,\quad\text{or}\quad{L}_{1}\otimes H[-1]\;\leq\;K\;<\;{L}_{2}\otimes H[-1].

In order to prove

propositions

5.8
,
5.9
,
5.10
and
5.13
,
we now examine the actions of Picard groups on t-structures, heart cones, and
modifying modules. This builds upon the work of Hirano–Wemyss
[
HW23
, §7]
on the subject.

Actions on the heart fan

The action of
\Pic X
on the
Grothendieck group
\KK X
is straightforward to analyse after choosing
an appropriate basis—choosing the classes
\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}=[{O}_{p}]
and
\alpha_{i}=[{O}_{C_{i}}(-1)]
(
i\in\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J}
), the action
\Pic X\circlearrowright\KK X
is given by

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline qn:picactiononktheory}{L}_{i}\otimes\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}=\delta_{\mathfrak{J}},\qquad{L}_{i}\otimes\alpha_{j}=\begin{cases}\alpha_{j}+\delta_{\mathfrak{J}},&j=i\\
\alpha_{j},&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

(25)

This clearly preserves the root system, i.e. the transposed action on

\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
preserves the intersection
arrangement
\Arr(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})
and in particular takes chambers to
chambers. This can be visualised by noting that the functionals

\{\alpha_{i}\;|\;i\in\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{I}\}
give coordinates on the
level set
\{\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}=1\}
and the action of
\Pic X
to the level set
restricts to translations along the lattice of integral points.

If
W=\nu X
is a different birational model, then flop functor
\Psi_{\nu}:\Dm W\to\Dm X
gives an isomorphism of
\KK
-theory
\KK W\to\KK X
which
maps
\delta_{\nu\mathfrak{J}}\mapsto\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}
. Writing
\beta_{i}=\left[\Psi_{\nu}{O}_{C_{i}}(-1)\right]
(
i\in\Delta\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J}
) for the classes of
the simples of
\Psi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Z}\right)$}}
, we see that
\{\delta_{\mathfrak{J}},\beta_{i}\;|\;i\in\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{I}\}
is a basis for
\KK X
and the action
\Pic W\circlearrowright\KK X
in this basis is given by a formula analogous to
(
25
). In particular the intersection arrangement is
again preserved, and there is an induced action on the set of chambers.

For a bounded heart
K\subset\Dm X
and
{L}\in\Pic W
, the chamber

{L}\cdot\CC K
is the heart cone of
{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes K
. The following
preliminary lemma shows that this action plays well with the partial order on
chambers.

Lemma 5.14
.

Given a birational model
W=\nu X
and nef line bundles
{L}_{1},{L}_{2}\in\Pic W
, we have
{L}_{1}\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}\geq{L}_{2}\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
in the weak
order if and only if
\deg({L}_{1})\leq\deg({L}_{2})
.

Proof.

If
{L}\in\Pic W
is nef and
i\in\Delta\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J}
, then the hyperplane

\{n\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}-\beta_{i}=0\}
separates
\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
and

{L}\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
if and only if
0<n\leq\deg({L}|_{C_{i}})
. It
follows that if
{L}_{1},{L}_{2}
are nef such that
{L}_{1}\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}\geq{L}_{2}\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
, then we necessarily have
\deg({L}_{1})\leq\deg({L}_{2})
.

Conversely consider any hyperplane

\{\alpha=0\}
defined by a restricted root
\alpha
. Since
\alpha
is simply
the projection of a root in an affine Dynkin root system, we may write

\alpha=n\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}+\sum_{i\in\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J}}{a_{i}}\beta_{i},

for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and some tuple of positive integers
\underline{a}=(a_{i})
.
Now for generic
\theta\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
normalised so that

\theta(\delta_{\mathfrak{J}})=1
, and for any
{L}\in\Pic W
, we have

({L}\,\theta)(\alpha)=\theta(\alpha)+\langle\underline{a},\deg{L}\rangle

where
\langle-,-\rangle
denotes the standard (Euclidean) inner
product between two tuples in
\mathbb{Z}^{(\Delta\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J})}
.
In particular if
{L}
is nef then
\langle\underline{a},\deg{L}\rangle\geq 0
, so the hyperplane
\{\alpha=0\}
separates
\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
and

{L}\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
if and only if

\langle\underline{a},\deg{L}\rangle\geq\quad-\theta(\alpha)\quad\geq\quad 0.

It follows that if
{L}_{1},{L}_{2}\in\Pic W
are such that

\deg({L}_{2})\geq\deg({L}_{1})\geq\underline{0}
then any hyperplane
separating
\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
from
{L}_{1}\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
also separates
it from
{L}_{2}\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
as required.
∎

Actions on modifying modules

Given a birational model
\pi\colon W\to Z
and a line bundle
{L}\in\Pic W
, the pushforward
\pi_{\ast}{L}
gives
a reflexive
R
-module and this determines an injective homomorphism
\Pic(W)\to\mathop{\text{}}{Cl}(R)
into the class group of

R
. It is clear that if
W^{\prime}
is another birational model, then the inclusions of

\Pic W
and
\Pic W^{\prime}
into
\mathop{\text{}}{Cl}(R)
are compatible with the
isomorphism
\Pic W\cong\Pic W^{\prime}
induced by taking proper transforms,
and we write
\cl(R)\subseteq\mathop{\text{}}{Cl}(R)
for the common image of all
such inclusions. We remark that the equality
\cl(R)=\text{Cl}(R)
holds if
and only if
X
(and hence every
W\in\mathop{\textnormal{}}{Bir}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
) is a minimal model of

Z
, this follows from
[
IW
, proposition 9.1]
and
relies on the assumption that the singularity of
Z
is isolated.

Iyama and Wemyss
[
IW
, §9.4]
extensively study the
action of
\cl(R)
on the set
\mathop{\textnormal{}}{MM}{}^{N}R
, given by

L\cdot M=(L^{\ast}\otimes_{R}M)^{\ast\ast}

for
L\in\cl(R)
,
M\in\mathop{\textnormal{}}{MM}{}^{N}R
, and

(-)^{\ast}=\Hom(-,R)
. In particular they show that the action is compatible
with that on the intersection arrangement under the natural bijection

\CC\colon\mathop{\textnormal{}}{MM}{}^{N}R\to\Chambers(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})
of

theorem

3.5
, we translate the result into a form suitable for our
purposes (see also
[
HW23
, lemma 7.2]
).

Proposition 5.15
.

If
\pi:W\to Z
is a birational model of
X
, then for every
{L}\in\Pic W
and
M\in\mathop{\textnormal{}}{MM}{}^{N}R
we have
{L}\cdot\CC(M)=\CC(\pi_{\ast}{L}\cdot M)
.

Proof.

It suffices to consider
W=X
, and to prove the statement for the line
bundles
{L}_{i}=(\det{N}_{i})\,\check{}\,

(
i\in\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J}
) which generate

\Pic X
. Further, we may assume the basic modifying module
N
is

maximal modifying
, i.e.

\mathop{\text{}}{add}\left(N\right)=\left\{M\in\mathcal{m\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}R\text{
reflexive}\;|\;\Ext^{1}\left(N,M\right)=\Ext^{1}\left(M,N\right)=0\right\}.

Indeed if not, then note that by
[
IW
, theorem
9.1(1)]
and
[
IW14
, corollary
4.18]
there is a modifying
module
N^{c}
such that
N\oplus N^{c}
is maximal modifying. This
choice can clearly be made such that
N\oplus N^{c}
is basic, and
further
[
IW
, theorem 9.5]
shows that this

N\oplus N^{c}
has at most
|\underline{\Delta}|
indecomposable summands so
we can index the summands of
N^{c}
as
N^{c}=\oplus_{i\in\mathfrak{I}}N_{i}
for
some
\mathfrak{I}\subset\mathfrak{J}
. Then we can work with the pair
(N\oplus N^{c},\mathfrak{J}\setminus\mathfrak{I})
instead of
(N,\mathfrak{J})
in what follows, using

[
IW
, theorem 8.15]
to translate the results
back to
N
.

Consider as in the proof of
theorem

3.5
a general elephant
\Spec\overline{R}\hookrightarrow\Spec R
and the
corresponding reduction functor
\mathbb{F}(-)=(-)\otimes_{R}\overline{R}
. Writing

\mathcal{m\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd\kern-0.5pti\kern-0.8ptf}(R)
for the set of modifying
R
-modules,
Iyama–Wemyss
[
IW
, definition 9.7]
define
a map
\text{ind}\colon\mathcal{m\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd\kern-0.5pti\kern-0.8ptf}(R)\to\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})

as the composite

\mathcal{m\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd\kern-0.5pti\kern-0.8ptf}(R)\xrightarrow{\;[\Hom_{R}(N,-)]\;}\KK_{\text{split}}(\mathcal{p\kern-0.5ptr\kern-0.5pto\kern-1.0ptj}\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\hskip 7.97224pt\mathbb{F}(-)\hskip 7.97224pt}\KK_{\text{split}}(\mathcal{p\kern-0.5ptr\kern-0.5pto\kern-1.0ptj}\mathbb{F}\Lambda)\longrightarrow\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})

(26)

where the final map sends class of the indecomposable projective

\mathbb{F}\Lambda_{i}=\mathbb{F}\Hom_{R}(N,N_{i})
to the vector
\theta_{i}\in\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
dual to
\alpha_{i}\in\mathfrak{h}(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
. By construction, for a maximal
modifying module
M
the cone
\CC(M)
is generated by the vectors

\text{ind}(M_{i})
associated to indecomposable summands
M_{i}\subset M
. Now
[
IW
, proposition 9.10 (1) and theorem 9.23
(2)]
show that for any
M\in\mathcal{m\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd\kern-0.5pti\kern-0.8ptf}(R)

we have

\displaystyle\mathop{\text{}}{ind}(\pi_{\ast}{L}_{i}\cdot M)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\text{}}{ind}(\det N_{i}\otimes_{R}M)^{\ast\ast}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\text{}}{ind}M+\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}(\mathop{\text{}}{ind}{M})\cdot(\mathop{\text{}}{ind}(\det N_{i})-\mathop{\text{}}{ind}R)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\text{}}{ind}M+\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}(\mathop{\text{}}{ind}{M})\cdot(\mathop{\text{}}{ind}N_{i}-\mathop{\text{}}{rk}N_{i}\cdot\mathop{\text{}}{ind}N_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathop{\text{}}{ind}M\circ({L}_{i}\otimes-)

where the penultimate equality uses the identity

\mathop{\text{}}{ind}N_{i}-\mathop{\text{}}{ind}(\det N_{i})=(\mathop{\text{}}{rk}N_{i}-1)\cdot\mathop{\text{}}{ind}R
which can be deduced,
for example, from
[
IW
, corollary 9.22]
.
Thus

\mathop{\text{}}{ind}(\pi_{\ast}{L}_{i}\cdot M)={L}_{i}\cdot\mathop{\text{}}{ind}M

for every
M\in\mathcal{m\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd\kern-0.5pti\kern-0.8ptf}(R)
, and the result follows.
∎

In what follows we suppress the map
\pi_{\ast}
from notation,
directly considering the action
\Pic W\circlearrowright\mathop{\textnormal{}}{MM}{}^{N}R
. Given

M\in\mathop{\textnormal{}}{MM}{}^{N}R
and
{L}\in\Pic W
, we write
{L}\cdot(\End_{R}M)=\End_{R}({L}M)
, in particular
{L}\Lambda
is the endomorphism algebra of
{L}N
.
Note there is natural isomorphism of algebras
\varepsilon\colon{L}\cdot\End_{R}M\to\End_{R}M
and thus an equivalence

\varepsilon\colon\Dfl\left({L}\,\End_{R}M\right)\to\Dfl\left(\End_{R}M\right)

which in particular identifies the standard hearts, i.e.
\varepsilon(H)=H
.

Actions on t-structures

Given
{L}\in\Pic W
, we examine
the intermediacy of
{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H
(with respect to
H
) by
comparing it with the tautologically intermediate heart associated with

{L}N\in\mathop{\textnormal{}}{MM}{}^{N}R
, i.e. the image of the Brenner–Butler map

\mathcal{f\kern-0.5ptl\kern-1.0ptm\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}({L}\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\RHom(\Hom_{R}({L}\cdot N,N),-)\hskip 7.97224pt}\Dfl\Lambda.

Choosing an atomic sequence of mutations
\lambda
from
N
to
{L}\cdot N
, the above map is simply
\Psi_{\lambda}
and the resulting intermediate
heart is
\Psi_{\lambda}H
. Evidently this heart agrees with

{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H
in
\KK
-theory, and we now show that they are in fact
equal when
{L}
is nef.

Proposition 5.16
.

Let
{L}\in\Pic W
be a nef line bundle and
\lambda
an atomic path
from
N
to
{L}\,N
. Then the following diagram commutes on objects.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dm X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dm X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dfl\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dfl\;{L}\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dfl\Lambda}
VdB
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes(-)}
VdB
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\lambda}}

(27)

Proof.

First consider the case when
{L}
is a nef bundle on

X
, i.e. when the sequence of flops
\nu
is trivial. We induct on
\deg{L}
, noting that the statement trivially holds for

{L}={O}_{X}
.

So suppose the diagram (
27
)
commutes for a nef
{L}\in\Pic X
, and consider the bundle
{L}_{i}\otimes{L}
for some
i\in\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J}
. By
lemmas

5.14
and
2.8
, we can choose positive paths
\lambda
,
\lambda_{i}
whose composite

\lambda\lambda_{i}
remains atomic such that

{L}_{i}\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}=\lambda_{i}\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+},\qquad({L}_{i}\otimes{L})\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}=\lambda\lambda_{i}\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}.

Further since
\lambda
is a reduced path from
{L}_{i}\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
to
({L}_{i}\otimes{L})\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
, translating
it along
{L}_{i}\,\check{}\,
gives a reduced (hence atomic) path

\lambda^{\prime}
such that

{L}\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}=\lambda^{\prime}\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}.

By
proposition

5.15
, we have
{L}_{i}N=\lambda N
,
({L}_{i}\otimes{L})N=\lambda\lambda_{i}N
, and
{L}N=\lambda^{\prime}N
, and each path appearing in these expressions is atomic.
Then the corresponding diagram (
27
) for

{L}_{i}\otimes{L}
is precisely the outer circuit of the diagram below,
and it suffices to prove that each component circuit (i)–(v) commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dm X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dm X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dm X}
(i)
(ii)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dfl\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dfl\;{L}\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dfl\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dfl\,{L}_{i}\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dfl\Lambda}
(iii)
(iv)

(v)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dfl\,({L}_{i}\otimes{L})\Lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L}_{i}\check{}\,\otimes(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\lambda^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\lambda_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\lambda\lambda_{i}}}

The pentagon (i) commutes by induction hypothesis, and (ii)
is precisely the diagram shown to commute in

[
HW23
, theorem 7.4]
. The triangle (iii)
commutes since there is a natural isomorphism of functors

(\pi_{\ast}{L}_{i}\,\check{}\,\otimes_{R}-)^{\ast}{}^{\ast}\circ(\pi_{\ast}{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes_{R}-)^{\ast}{}^{\ast}\quad\cong\quad(\pi_{\ast}({L}_{i}\otimes{L})\,\check{}\,\otimes_{R}-)^{\ast}{}^{\ast},

so that the isomorphism of algebras

\Lambda\xrightarrow{\;\varepsilon\;}({L}_{i}\otimes{L})\Lambda
is the
composite
\Lambda\xrightarrow{\;\varepsilon\;}{L}\,\Lambda\xrightarrow{\;\varepsilon\;}({L}_{i}\otimes{L})\Lambda
. The
commutativity of (iv) is the content of
[
HW23
, lemma
7.3]
, while the triangle (v)
commutes by
lemma

3.7
since
\lambda\lambda_{i}
is an
atomic path. Thus the whole diagram commutes as required.

Now suppose we are in the general case, i.e.
{L}
is a nef
bundle on the flop
W=\nu X
and
\lambda
is an atomic path from

N
to
{L}\,N
. Write
M=\nu N
for the modifying
R
-module
generator associated to
W
, and choose an atomic path
\lambda^{\prime}

from
M
to
{L}\,M
. Further we may assume the sequence of flops

\nu
is atomic, so that translating
\nu
by
{L}
as above gives
an atomic path
\nu^{\prime}
from
{L}\,N
to
{L}\,M
.

We can thus construct a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dm X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dm W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dm W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dm X}
(i)
(ii)
(iii)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dfl\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dfl{}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\nu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dfl\;{L}\,{}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\nu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dfl{}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\nu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dfl\Lambda}
(iv)
(v)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dfl\;{L}\,\Lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{flop}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\nu}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\lambda^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\nu^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\nu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\lambda}}

where the squares (i) and (iii) commute by
theorem

3.11
, while the
circuit (ii) commutes from the above discussion since
{L}
is a
nef bundle on
W
(the trivial flop of
W
). The commutativity
of (iv) is again the content of
[
HW23
, lemma
7.3]
. To show (v) commutes we use

lemma

3.7
, observing that the composite paths

\lambda^{\prime}\nu
and
\nu^{\prime}\lambda
are both atomic—indeed any
hyperplane crossed by the atomic path
\nu
must pass through the
cones

\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}\cap\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}\quad\supseteq\quad\bigcap_{i\in\Delta\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J}}\;\{\beta_{i}=0\}

and hence is of the form
\left\{\sum_{i\in\Delta\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J}}b_{i}\beta_{i}=0\right\}
for some tuple of non-negative integers
b_{i}
.
But then any generic vector
\theta\in\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
and its
translate
{L}\,\theta\in\lambda^{\prime}\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
both evidently lie on
the same side of such a hyperplane, which thereby cannot be crossed by
the atomic path
\lambda^{\prime}
. Similar reasoning shows the hyperplanes
crossed by
\lambda
and
\nu^{\prime}
are distinct.

Thus the whole diagram commutes, and
reading the outer circuit yields the required diagram
(
27
).
∎

Intermediacy of twists

We now prove parts

1
,
3
,

2
, and
4
of

theorem

5.10
in that order, noting that each successive proof
relies on the previous ones.

Proof of
theorem

5.10

1
.

If
{L}\in\Pic W
is nef, then
proposition

5.16
shows that the heart

{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H
coincides with
\Psi_{\lambda}H
for some atomic
path
\lambda
. In particular, it is
intermediate with respect to
H
.

On the other hand given any
{L}\in\Pic W
such that
H\geq{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H
, we may express
{L}
as a
difference of nef bundles
{L}={L}_{-}\check{}\,\otimes{L}_{+}
to obtain the
inequality
{L}_{-}\check{}\,\otimes H\geq{L}_{+}\check{}\,\otimes H
in
\tilt(H)
.
Noting
{L}_{-}\check{}\,\otimes H=\Psi_{\nu}H
and
{L}_{+}\check{}\,\otimes H=\Psi_{\nu^{\prime}}H
for some paths
\nu,\nu^{\prime}
, by

proposition

5.7
we thus have an inequality of chambers

{L}_{-}\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}\geq{L}_{+}\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
in the weak order. Then

lemma

5.14
shows
\deg({L}_{-})\leq\deg({L}_{+})
, i.e.
{L}
is necessarily nef.

One can likewise show that the nef-ness of
{L}

is also equivalent to the inequality of hearts
{L}\otimes H[-1]\geq H[-1]
, by first
inverting the diagram (
27
) to show
{L}\otimes H[-1]=\Phi_{\lambda}H[-1]
for some
\mathfrak{J}
-path
\lambda
and
then proceeding as above.

Lastly we show
{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H>\Psi_{\nu}\coh(W)
when

{L}\in\Pic W
is nef. Consider the
\KK
-theory classes

\beta_{i}=[\Psi_{\nu}{O}_{C_{i}}(-1)]
for
i\in\Delta\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J}
, and the vector

\beta_{0}^{\ast}\in\Theta(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
determined as

\beta_{0}^{\ast}(\beta_{i})=\begin{cases}1,&i=0\\
0,&i\in\Delta\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J}.\end{cases}

One checks that
\beta_{0}^{\ast}(\delta_{\mathfrak{J}})=1
and

\beta_{0}^{\ast}(\alpha_{i})=0
for all
i\in\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J}
, so we have

\beta_{0}^{\ast}\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
and
thus the vector
{L}\cdot\beta_{0}^{\ast}
lies in
{L}\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}=\CC({L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H)
. Further since
{L}
is nef, we
see that
{L}\cdot\beta_{0}^{\ast}=\beta_{0}^{\ast}+\theta^{0}
for some

\theta^{0}\in\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}
. It follows that

\displaystyle{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H[1]\cap H\;

\displaystyle\subseteq\;H^{\textnormal{tr}}({L}\cdot\beta_{0}^{\ast})

\displaystyle=\{h\in H\;|\;{L}\cdot\beta_{0}^{\ast}[f]\leq 0\text{ for all
factors }h\twoheadrightarrow f\}

\displaystyle=\{h\in H\;|\;\theta^{0}[f]\leq-\beta_{0}^{\ast}[f]\text{ for all
factors }h\twoheadrightarrow f\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\{h\in H\;|\;\theta^{0}[f]<0\text{ for all
non-zero factors }h\twoheadrightarrow f\neq 0\}

\displaystyle=H_{\textnormal{tr}}(\theta_{0})

\displaystyle\subseteq\;\Psi_{\nu}\coh W[1]\cap H

and thus
{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H\geq\Psi_{\nu}\coh W
, with the inequality
being necessarily strict since
\Psi_{\nu}\coh W
is not algebraic.
The proof of the corresponding statement for
{L}\otimes H[-1]
is
analogous.
∎

Proof of
theorem

5.10

3
.

Given birational models
W=\nu X
and
W^{\prime}\in\nu^{\prime}X
, we have seen that
the action of any
{L}\in\Pic W
on
\KK X
coincides with that of its
proper transform
{L}^{\prime}\in\Pic W^{\prime}
. Thus in particular
{L}\,\check{}\,\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}={L}^{\prime}\,\check{}\,\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
, i.e.
\CC({L}\otimes H)=\CC({L}^{\prime}\otimes H)
.

Now if
{L}
and
{L}^{\prime}
are both nef, then
{L}\otimes H
and

{L}^{\prime}\otimes H
are both intermediate with respect to
H
by
theorem

5.10

1
. But
the assignment
\CC\colon\tilt(H)\to\HFan(H)
restricts
to a bijection between algebraic intermediate hearts and full dimensional
heart cones, so
{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H={L}^{\prime}\,\check{}\,\otimes H
. Likewise,
the hearts
{L}\otimes H[-1]
and
{L}^{\prime}\otimes H[-1]
are equal.
∎

Proof of
theorem

5.10

2
.

Suppose
\sigma
and
W
are as given, and consider a line bundle

{L}\in\Pic W\cap\sigma
. If
\upsilon X
is another

\sigma
-positive birational model, then the proper transform of
{L}

is nef on
\upsilon X
and thus
theorem

5.10

1
and
3
give us

{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H>\Psi_{\upsilon}\coh(\upsilon X)
. Considering all such
inequalities together, we obtain

\mathop{\textnormal{}}{inf}\,\left\{{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H\;|\;{L}\in\Pic W\cap\sigma\right\}\;\geq\;\mathop{\textnormal{}}{sup}\,\left\{\Psi_{\upsilon}\coh(\upsilon X)\;|\;\sigma\subset\upsilon\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}\right\}\;\overset{(\ref{thm:nonmaximalheartcones})}{=}H^{\textnormal{tt}}(\sigma).

Conversely suppose
h\in H
lies in
{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H
for every

{L}\in\Pic W\cap\sigma
, i.e.
H
lies in the torsion-free class
associated to the infimum on the left-hand side above.
Pick a line bundle
{L}_{0}
that lies
generically
in
\Pic(W)\cap\sigma
, i.e.
{L}_{0}
does not lie in
\Pic W\cap\sigma^{\prime}
for
any proper face
\sigma^{\prime}\subset\sigma
. Thus
{L}_{0}
determines a generic
vector
\theta_{0}\in\sigma
. Considering the vector
\theta={L}_{0}^{\otimes n}\cdot\alpha_{0}^{\ast}
where
n>0
is some integer and
\alpha_{0}^{\ast}
is the vector given by
(
23
), one calculates

\theta=\alpha_{0}^{\ast}+n\cdot\theta_{0}.

Moreover
\theta
clearly lies in
{L}_{0}^{\otimes n}\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}=\CC(({L}_{0}\check{}\,)^{\otimes n}\otimes H)
, and thus we have

h\in H_{\textnormal{tf}}(\theta)
. In other words,
\alpha_{0}^{\ast}[s]+n\cdot\theta_{0}[s]>0

for every non-zero sub-object
s\hookrightarrow h
. But
n>0
was arbitrary, so we
must in fact have
\theta_{0}[s]\geq 0
for every sub-object
s\hookrightarrow h
–
this is precisely the condition
h\in H^{\textnormal{tf}}(\theta_{0})
, and
thus giving the opposite inequality

\mathop{\textnormal{}}{inf}\,\left\{{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H\;|\;{L}\in\Pic W\cap\sigma\right\}\;\leq\;H^{\textnormal{tt}}(\theta_{0})\;=\;H^{\textnormal{tt}}(\sigma).

The statement for
H_{\textnormal{tt}}(\sigma)
can be proved analogously.
∎

Proof of
theorem

5.10

4
.

Suppose
K
is as given, in particular the heart cone
\CC K
is of the
form
{L}\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
or
{L}\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{-}
for a unique
{L}\in\Pic W
—without loss of generality the former. If this
{L}
were nef,
then by
theorem

5.10

1
we would have
that
K
and
{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H
are elements of
\tilt(H)
with
identical full-dimensional heart cones, whence they must be equal as
required.

Noting
\CC K
lies in
\{\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}\geq 0\}
, we see that the inequality
K\leq\anticoh(W)
cannot occur. Therefore we must show
that
{L}
is necessarily nef if
K\geq\coh(W)
.
By
theorem

5.10

2
, this inequality
can be rewritten as the containment of torsion classes

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline qn:kinsup}K[1]\cap H\;\subseteq\;\mathop{\textnormal{}}{sup}\,\{{L}_{0}\check{}\,\otimes H[1]\cap H\;|\;{{L}_{0}\in\Pic W\text{ nef}}\}.

(28)

Now for any two nef bundles
{L}_{1},{L}_{2}\in\Pic W
, from

theorem

5.10

1
we see that both

{L}_{1}\,\check{}\,\otimes H
and
{L}_{2}\,\check{}\,\otimes H
are greater than

({L}_{1}\otimes{L}_{2})\,\check{}\,\otimes H
in the poset of t-structures. In
other words, we have the containmen of torsion classes

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline qn:lbtorsionclassdomination}\left({L}_{1}\,\check{}\,\otimes H[1]\cap H\right)\;\cup\;\left({L}_{2}\,\check{}\,\otimes H[1]\cap H\right)\quad\subseteq\quad({L}_{1}\otimes{L}_{2})\,\check{}\,\otimes H[1]\cap H.

(29)

It follows that the supremum in (
28
) is in fact a union. The
torsion class
K[1]\cap H
is finitely generated (for example by the
finite set of brick labels in the interval
[K,H]\subset\tilt(H)
), so a
sufficiently ample
{L}_{0}\in\Pic W
determines a torsion class

{L}_{0}\otimes H[1]\cap H
in this union that contains all the generators
of
K[1]\cap H
, and hence contains
K[1]\cap H
.

Thus we have
H\geq K\geq{L}_{0}\check{}\,\otimes H
, and consequently (by
proposition

5.7
) the
inequality of heart cones

\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}\geq{L}\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}\geq{L}_{0}\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}

for some for some nef
{L}_{0}\in\Pic W
. The nef-ness of
{L}
is then evident, e.g. by considering
the hyperplanes
\left\{[{\Psi_{\nu}{O}_{C_{i}}(n)}]=0\right\}
defined by curves
C_{i}\subset W
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

The proofs of

propositions

5.8
,
5.9
and
5.13

now follow.

Proof of
proposition

5.8
.

Given the line bundle
{L}={L}_{i}^{\otimes n}
(
i\in\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J}
,
n>0
),
theorem

5.10

1

shows that
{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H
is intermediate with respect to
H
. Further since it
is an Artinian tilt,
proposition

5.4
shows that the
corresponding torsion class
{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H[1]\cap H
is generated by the
semibrick

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=\{b\in H\;|\;b[-1]\text{ is a simple object of }{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H\}

\displaystyle=\{b\in H\;|\;b\text{ is the brick label of a covering relation
}K\gtrdot{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H\text{ in }\tilt(H)\}.

In particular, the size of
S
is equal to the number of hearts in

\tilt(H)
covered by
{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H
. Equivalently
(
proposition

5.7
), this is the number of covering
relations of the form
\sigma\lessdot{L}\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
in

\Chambers(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})
.

Such covering relations in the weak order correspond to hyperplanes that
support (i.e. contain a codimension one face of)
{L}\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
and
separate it from
\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
. But the hyperplanes supporting

{L}\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
are defined by the roots

{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}-n\delta_{\mathfrak{J}},\quad{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes\alpha_{j}=\alpha_{j}\quad(j\in\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J},\;j\neq i),\quad\text{and}\quad{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{0}+n\delta_{i}\cdot\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}.

Of these only
\alpha_{i}-n\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}
separates
\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
from

{L}\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
. Thus the semibrick
S
has precisely one element,
and examining the simples of
{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H
shows that this element
must be

{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes{O}_{C_{i}}(-1)[1]={O}_{C_{i}}(-n-1)[1].

The statement for
{L}\otimes H[-1]
can be proved analogously.
∎

Proof of
proposition

5.9
.

Immediate from
proposition

5.8
and

theorem

5.10

2
.
∎

Proof of
theorem

5.13
.

We prove the statement for
K=\Psi_{\nu}H
for some positive
\mathfrak{J}
-path

\nu
, the case for
\Phi_{\nu}H[-1]
being analogous. Now given a
positive
\mathfrak{J}
-path
\nu
, there is a unique spherical
\mathfrak{J}
-path

\mu
such that

\displaystyle\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}

\displaystyle\;\subseteq\;\mu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0}\;+\;\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\cdot\alpha_{0}^{\ast}

\displaystyle\;=\;\left\{\theta_{0}+t\alpha_{0}^{\ast}\;|\;\theta_{0}\in\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0},\;t\geq 0\right\},

where
\alpha_{0}^{\ast}
is as in (
23
). Indeed, the half
space
\{\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}\geq 0\}
(which contains
\Arr^{+}(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})
)
can be written as the union of all such regions and they intersect pairwise in
subsets of positive codimension. In particular for each class

\beta_{i}=[\Psi_{\nu}{O}_{C_{i}}(-1)]

(i\in\Delta\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J})
,
we have

\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}\subseteq\{\theta\;|\;\theta(\beta_{i})\geq 0\}.

A similar argument involving a decomposition of

\{\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}\geq 0\}
shows that for each
i\in\Delta\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J}

there is a unique integer
d_{i}
such that

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline qn:boundsonCK}\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}\;\subseteq\;\left\{\theta\;|\;(d_{i}+1)\cdot\theta(\delta_{\mathfrak{J}})\geq\theta(\beta_{i})\geq d_{i}\cdot\theta(\delta_{\mathfrak{J}})\right\},

(30)

and clearly we must have
d_{i}\geq 0
in this case. Then the vector

(d_{i})\in\mathbb{Z}^{|\Delta\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J}|}
determines line bundles

{L}_{1},{L}_{2}\in\Pic W
with degrees
(d_{i})
,
(d_{i}+1)
respectively
—evidently we have

\underline{0}\;\leq\;\deg({L}_{1})\;<\;\deg({L}_{2})\;=\;\deg({L}_{1})+\underline{1}.

Moreover, by construction we have the inequalities of chambers

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline qn:conebounds}{L}_{1}\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}\;\geq\;\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}\;\geq\;{L}_{2}\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}.

(31)

To see why, suppose the hyperplane
\{\alpha=0\}
separates

\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
and
{L}_{1}\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
, where (up to replacing

\alpha
by its negative) we may write

\alpha=n\delta_{\mathfrak{J}}+\sum_{i\in\Delta\setminus\mu\mathfrak{J}}a_{i}\beta_{i}

for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and a tuple of non-negative integers
(a_{i})
. For
generic
\theta\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
, the only way in which
\theta(\alpha)
and

({L}_{1}\theta)(\alpha)=\theta(\alpha)+\sum_{i}a_{i}d_{i}\,\theta(\delta_{\mathfrak{J}})
can have opposite signs
is if
\theta(\alpha)\leq 0
and
({L}_{1}\theta)(\alpha)\geq 0
. In
particular choosing the vector
\theta=\alpha_{0}^{\ast}
as in (
23
)
which vanishes on all
\beta_{i}
(
i\in\Delta\setminus\mu\mathfrak{J}
), we obtain the inequality

n+\sum_{i\in\Delta\setminus\mu\mathfrak{J}}a_{i}d_{i}\geq 0.

It follows that for generic
\theta\in\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
normalised so that

\theta(\delta_{\mathfrak{J}})=1
, we have

\theta(\alpha)\;=\;n+\sum_{i\in\Delta\setminus\mu\mathfrak{J}}a_{i}\theta(\beta_{i})\;\geq\;n+\sum_{i\in\Delta\setminus\mu\mathfrak{J}}a_{i}d_{i}\;\geq\;0.

In other words, the hyperplane cut by
\alpha
must also separate

\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
and
\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
, showing
{L}_{1}\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}\geq\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
as required. The inequality

\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}>{L}_{2}\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}
is analogous.

The desired inequality on
K
then follows by translating the inequality
of heart cones (
31
) into inequalities of intermediate
algebraic hearts via
proposition

5.7
.
∎

§ 5.4 Every torsion theory on the perverse heart is numerical

The above analysis of
\tilt(H)
paves the way for this result, showing that
the heart fan of
H
detects
every
intermediate t-structure and thus an
arbitrary heart in
\tilt(H)
must be one of the algebraic, geometric, or
semi-geometric t-structures described in
4
.

Theorem 5.17
.

Let
K
be an arbitrary tilt of
H
. Then the heart cone

\CC K
is non-zero.

We prove
theorem

5.17
over the course of this subsection.
Thus, fix
K\in\tilt(H)
as above. If
K
is algebraic, then
\CC K

is full-dimensional (in particular, non-zero) by

theorem

4.2
. So we may restrict to the case when
K
is
non-algebraic. By
theorem

4.9
,
\CC K
is thus a
(possibly zero) cone in
\Arr(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})
. The following lemma is then
the key tool we exploit.

Lemma 5.18
.

Given
K\in\tilt(H)
, suppose there is a non-zero cone

\sigma\in\Arr(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})
and a
\sigma
-positive birational model

W\in\mathop{\textnormal{}}{Bir}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
such that
{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H\;\geq\;K\;\geq\;{L}\otimes H[-1]
for
every
{L}\in\Pic W\cap\sigma
. Then the heart cone
\CC K
contains

\sigma
, and is in particular non-zero.

Proof.

The conditions, combined with
theorem

5.10

2
show
H^{\textnormal{tt}}(\sigma)\geq K\geq H_{\textnormal{tt}}(\sigma)
so the result follows
from
theorem

4.3
.
∎

It is relatively straightforward to obtain the bound on one side for

lemma

5.18
.

Lemma 5.19
.

If the heart
K\in\tilt(H)
is not algebraic, then there is a non-zero cone

\sigma\in\Arr(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})
and a
\sigma
-positive birational model

W\in\mathop{\textnormal{}}{Bir}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
such that
{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H\geq K
for every
{L}\in\Pic W\cap\sigma
.

Likewise, there is a (possibly different) non-zero cone
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Arr(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})
and a
\sigma^{\prime}
-positive birational model

W^{\prime}\in\mathop{\textnormal{}}{Bir}\kern-1.0pt\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
such that
K\geq{L}^{\prime}\otimes H[-1]
for every
{L}^{\prime}\in\Pic W^{\prime}\cap\sigma^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We prove the first statement, the second being analogous. Since

K
is non-algebraic,
proposition

5.7
shows that

K
is not covered by an algebraic heart. In particular if

K_{n}\in\tilt(H)
is algebraic and satisfies
K_{n}>K
then there is an algebraic
heart
K_{n+1}
such that
K_{n}\gtrdot K_{n+1}>K
. Starting with the tautological relation

H=K_{0}>K
, this produces an infinite chain
K_{0}>K_{1}>K_{2}>\cdots>K
approaching
K
using algebraic tilts of

H
, each of the form
\Psi_{\nu}H
for some
\mathfrak{J}
-path
\nu
.

By
theorem

5.13
, for each
K_{n}
there is a birational
model
W_{n}
and line bundles
{L}_{n},{L}_{n}^{\prime}
in
\Pic W_{n}
such that

{L}_{n}\check{}\,\otimes H\;\geq\;K_{n}\;\geq\;{L}^{\prime}_{n}\check{}\,\otimes H,\quad\text{and}\quad\underline{0}\;\leq\;\deg({L}_{n})<\deg({L}_{n}^{\prime})\leq\deg({L}_{n})+\underline{1}.

But there are only finitely many birational models of
X
, so we can
pass to a subsequence if necessary to assume all birational
models
W_{n}
are equal. Thus there is a
W=\nu X
and a sequence of
nef line bundles
{L}_{1},{L}_{2},\ldots\in\Pic W
such that

{L}_{n}\check{}\,\otimes H>K
for each
n
.

Claim there is some integral exceptional curve
C_{i}\subset W
such that

\deg({L}_{n}|_{C_{i}})
attains arbitrarily large magnitude. If not, then
the tuples
\deg({L}_{n})
are bounded and we can
choose a line bundle
{L}_{\infty}\in\Pic W
such that

\deg{L}_{\infty}\geq\deg{L}_{n}+\underline{1}
for each
n
.
Thus
\deg{L}_{n}^{\prime}\leq\deg{L}_{\infty}
for each
n
, which shows

{L}_{n}^{\prime}\check{}\,\otimes H\geq{L}_{\infty}\check{}\,\otimes H
and hence
K_{n}\in[{L}_{\infty}\check{}\,\otimes H,H]
for each
n
.

Noting

{L}_{\infty}\check{}\,\otimes H=\Psi_{\nu}H
for some
\mathfrak{J}
-path
\nu
,
we see that the interval
[\Psi_{\nu}H,H]\subset\tilt(H)
contains
infinitely many elements. But we have seen
(
proposition

5.7
) that there is a poset
isomorphism

\tilt(H)\supset[\Psi_{\nu}H,H]\quad\simeq\quad[\nu\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+},\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{+}]\subset\Chambers(\underline{\Delta},\mathfrak{J})

and the latter interval is finite by
lemma

2.8
—a
contradiction.

Therefore writing
{L}\in\Pic W
for the line bundle which has degree
1
on
C_{i}
and is trivial elsewhere, we see that for every

N>0
there is an
n
such that
({L}\,\check{}\,)^{\otimes N}\otimes H>{L}_{n}\check{}\,\otimes H
and thus

({L}\,\check{}\,)^{\otimes N}\otimes H>K
for all
N
.

Choosing
\sigma\in\Arr(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})
to be the ray generated by (the
vector corresponding to)
{L}
then yields the result.
∎

As an immediate consequence, we see that any heart bounded by a geometric
one is numerical.

Lemma 5.20
.

If
K\in\tilt(H)
is such that
K\geq\Psi_{\nu}\coh W
or
\Psi_{\nu}\anticoh W\geq K
for some birational model
W=\nu X
, then
\CC K

is non-zero.

Proof.

Again we may assume
K
is non-algebraic and lies in
[\Psi_{\nu}\coh W,H]
, so that
lemma

5.19
gives a non-zero cone

\sigma\in\Arr(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})
and a
\sigma
-positive birational
model
W^{\prime}
such that
{L}^{\prime}\,\check{}\,\otimes H\geq K\geq\Psi_{\nu}\coh W
for every
{L}^{\prime}\in\Pic W^{\prime}\cap\sigma
.

But then
theorem

5.10

4
shows that for
each
{L}^{\prime}\in\Pic W^{\prime}\cap\sigma
, we may choose a nef bundle
{L}\in\Pic(W)
(given by the proper transform of
{L}^{\prime}
) such that
{L}^{\prime}\,\check{}\,\otimes H={L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H
. In particular,
W
is
\sigma
-positive and
we have
{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H\geq K
for every
{L}\in\Pic W\cap\sigma
.

On the other hand we also have the inequality
K\geq\Psi_{\nu}\coh W\geq{L}\otimes H[-1]
for every
{L}\in\Pic W\cap\sigma
, so
lemma

5.18
yields the conclusion.
∎

We deduce analogous bounds on hearts
K\in\tilt(H)
which contain skyscraper
sheaves
{O}_{p}
(for closed points
p\in X
) or shifts thereof, noting that a priori each

{O}_{p}
is only a two-term complex in
K[0,1]
.

Lemma 5.21
.

Suppose
C_{i}\subset X
is an integral exceptional curve with a closed
point
p\in C_{i}
, and
{L}_{i}\in\Pic X
is the line bundle which has
degree
1
on
C_{i}
and is trivial elsewhere. Writing
\sigma\in\Arr(\Delta,\mathfrak{J})
for the cone generated by
{L}_{i}
, the following
statements hold for any
K\in\tilt(H)
.

1.

If
{O}_{p}\in K
, then
K\geq{L}\otimes H[-1]
for
every
{L}\in\Pic X\cap\sigma
.

2.

If
{O}_{p}\in K[1]
, then
{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H\geq K
for
every
{L}\in\Pic X\cap\sigma
.

In particular if there are closed points
p,q\in C_{i}
such that
{O}_{p}\in K
and
{O}_{q}\in K[1]
, then
\CC K
is non-zero.

Proof.

If
p\in C_{i}
is a closed point
such that
{O}_{p}\in K
, then
{O}_{p}
lies in the torsion-free class

F=K\cap H
. The exact triangle

{O}_{C_{i}}(-1)\to{O}_{p}\to{O}_{C_{i}}(-2)[1]\to{O}_{C_{i}}(-1)[1]

shows
{O}_{C_{i}}(-1)

is a sub-object (in
H
) of
{O}_{p}
, thus we also have

{O}_{C_{i}}(-1)\in F
. Further, inductively considering extensions

{O}_{C_{i}}(n)\to{O}_{C_{i}}(n+1)\to{O}_{p}\to{O}_{C_{i}}(n)[1]

shows

{O}_{C_{i}}(n)\in F
for every
n\geq-1
, and thus
F
contains
each torsion class
{L}_{i}^{\otimes n}\otimes H[-1]\cap H
by

proposition

5.8
. Statement
1

follows, and the proof of
2
is analogous.
∎

Thus given an arbitrary non-algebraic t-structure
K
, we show
\CC K
is
non-zero by comparing
K
against some appropriately chosen geometry. The
following lemma informs this choice.

Lemma 5.22
.

Given
K\in\tilt(H)
, there is a sequence of flops
\nu
from
X

with corresponding birational model
W=\nu X
such that
K
lies in

\Psi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Z}\right)$}}[-1,0]
and
contains the objects
\Psi_{\nu}{O}_{C_{i}}(-1)
for each exceptional curve

C_{i}\subset W
.

Proof.

Consider the sub-poset of
\tilt(H)
given by

[K,H]\;\cap\;\{\Psi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{\nu X}{Z}\right)$}}\;|\;\nu\text{ a spherical $\mathfrak{J}$-path}\}.

This poset is non-empty (it contains
H
) and finite, so has a minimal
element determined by some spherical
\mathfrak{J}
-path
\nu
. This
determines our sequence of flops.

Continuing to write
\Psi_{\nu}H
for
the heart
\Psi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{\nu X}{Z}\right)$}}=\Psi_{\nu}\mathcal{f\kern-0.5ptl\kern-1.0ptm\kern-0.5pto\kern-0.5ptd}({}_{\nu}\Lambda_{\nu})
, we evidently have the chain of inequalities

H\;\geq\;\Psi_{\nu}H\;\geq\;K\;\geq\;H[-1]\;\geq\;\Psi_{\nu}H[-1]
,
so that
K
lies in
\Psi_{\nu}H[-1,0]
.

Suppose
i\in\Delta\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J}
is such that
\Psi_{\nu}S_{i}=\Psi_{\nu}{O}_{C_{i}}(-1)
lies outside
K
, and hence outside the
torsion-free class
K\cap\Psi_{\nu}H
in
\Psi_{\nu}H
. Being a simple of

\Psi_{\nu}H
, this object must then lie in the torsion class

K[1]\cap\Psi_{\nu}H
. Noting
\langle\Psi_{\nu}S_{i}\rangle
is
precisely the torsion class
\Psi_{\nu}\Psi_{i}H[1]\cap\Psi_{\nu}H

(
lemma

5.6
), we thus have

\Psi_{\nu}H\;\geq\;\Psi_{\nu}\Psi_{i}H\;\geq\;K
.

It follows that
\Psi_{\nu}\Psi_{i}H
lies in the interval

[K,H]
, and in particular is an algebraic heart in
\tilt(H)
. Since its heart cone
coincides with that of
\Psi_{\nu_{i}\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{\nu_{i}\nu X}{Z}\right)$}}

we conclude that the two hearts must be equal (
theorem

4.2
).
But this contradicts the minimality of
\Psi_{\nu}H
, so we conclude
that
K
must contain every simple
\Psi_{\nu}S_{i}
for
i\in\Delta\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J}
.
∎

Definition 5.23
.

Given a birational model
W=\nu X
, we say a heart
K\subset\Dm X

lives on
W
if it is intermediate with respect to
\Psi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Z}\right)$}}
and contains the object
\Psi_{\nu}{O}_{C_{i}}(-1)
for every
integral exceptional curve
C_{i}\subset W
, i.e. if there are inclusions

\langle\Psi_{\nu}{O}_{C_{i}}(-1)\;|\;i\in\Delta\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J}\rangle\;\subseteq\;K\;\subseteq\;\Psi_{\nu}\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{W}{Z}\right)$}}[-1,0].

Then
lemma

5.22

explains that every non-algebraic heart in
\tilt(H)
lives on
some

birational model, so up to the application of a flop functor we may assume
K

lives on
X
. This additional hypothesis gives us tremendous control on the torsion pair
associated to
K
, in particular we show that every skyscraper on
X
is
either torsion or torsion-free.

Lemma 5.24
.

If
K\in\tilt(H)
lives on
X
and
p\in X(\mathbb{C})
is a closed point in the
exceptional locus, then the sheaf
{O}_{p}
lies in a single
cohomological degree with respect to
K
(i.e. we have
{O}_{p}\in K
or

{O}_{p}\in K[1]
.)

Proof.

The intermediate heart
K
induces the torsion pair
H=(K[1]\cap H)\ast(K\cap H)
, and thus an exact triangle
t\to{O}_{p}\to f\to t[1]
with
t\in K[1]\cap H
,
f\in K\cap H
. In particular,
considering
\KK
-theory classes

[t]=\sum_{i\in\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J}}t_{i}\alpha_{i},\qquad[{O}_{p}]=\sum_{i\in\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J}}\delta_{i}\alpha_{i},\qquad[f]=\sum_{i\in\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J}}f_{i}\alpha_{i},

we see that for
each
i
the
t_{i},\delta_{i},f_{i}
are non-negative integers satisfying

t_{i}+f_{i}=\delta_{i}
, and the coefficients
\delta_{i}
correspond to
ranks of the summands of
{V\kern-1.0pt}\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
as in

lemma

4.10
. In particular we have
\delta_{0}=\text{rk}({O}_{X})=1
, so we must have
(t_{0},f_{0})=(0,1)
or

(t_{0},f_{0})=(1,0)
.

If
f_{0}=0
, then
f
is filtered by the simples

S_{i}={O}_{C_{i}}(-1)
for
i\in\Delta\setminus\mathfrak{J}
, and is in
particular a pure sheaf. But this implies
\Hom({O}_{p},f)=0
, thus the map
t\to{O}_{p}
is an isomorphism and
{O}_{p}
lies in
K[1]
.

On the other hand if
t_{0}=0
, then
t
lies in the extension-closure
of the sheaves
{O}_{C_{i}}(-1)
, and hence in
K
(since
K
lives
on
X
). It follows that
t\in K[1]\cap K
must be the zero object,
so that
{O}_{p}=f\in K
.
∎

We are equipped to show that an arbitrary t-structure in
\tilt(H)
is
numerical.

Proof of
theorem

5.17
.

We may assume
K
is non-algebraic and, applying a flop functor if
necessary, lives on
X
. Thus by
lemma

5.24
, for
every closed point
p
in the exceptional locus of
X
, either
{O}_{p}
or

{O}_{p}[-1]
lies in
K
.

If there is some curve
C_{i}
with two points
p,q\in C_{i}
such that

{O}_{p}\in K
and
{O}_{q}[-1]\in K
, then we obtain the conclusion using

lemma

5.21
.

Otherwise, since the exceptional locus of
X
is connected, either
K

or
K[1]
contains the subcategory
\{{O}_{p}\;|\;p\in X(\mathbb{C})\}
.
If
{O}_{p}\in K
for every closed point
p
, then (by

lemma

5.21
) we have
K\geq{L}\otimes H[-1]

for every nef bundle
{L}\in\Pic X
and thus
K\geq\anticoh X
. Further, the
torsion-free part
K\cap H
contains

H_{\textnormal{ss}}(\mathrm{C}_{\mathfrak{J}}^{0})=\langle{O}_{p}\;|\;p\in C\rangle
so in fact
K\geq\coh X
. By
lemma

5.20
, we conclude
\CC K\neq 0
.

The case when
{O}_{p}[-1]\in K
for every
p
is handled likewise.
∎

§ 5.5 Classification of bricks

We show
that every brick in
H=\zeroper\text{\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\tfrac{X}{Z}\right)$}}
must arise from an algebraic or geometric
simple.

Theorem 5.25
.

Let
b\in H
be a brick. Then either

(1)

there is a spherical
\mathfrak{J}
-path
\nu
and a closed point

p\in\nu X(\mathbb{C})
such that
b=\Psi_{\nu}{O}_{p}
, or

(2)

there is a
\mathfrak{J}
-path
\nu
and an index

i\in\underline{\Delta}\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J}
such that
b\in\left\{\Psi_{\nu}S_{i}[1],\Phi_{\nu}S_{i}[-1]\right\}
.

In particular, the
\KK
-theory class
[b]\in\mathfrak{h}(\underline{\Delta}\setminus\mathfrak{J})
is a primitive restricted root.

We prove the theorem over the course of this subsection. Thus fix a
brick
b\in H
, and consider the torsion pair
H=T\ast F
where
T
is the minimal torsion class containing
b
and accordingly

F=\{h\in H\;|\;\Hom(b,h)=0\}
.

By
lemma

5.22
, the corresponding
tilted heart
K=F\ast T[-1]
lives on some birational model
W=\upsilon X
.
We show that
b
then lies in a single cohomological degree with respect to

\Psi_{\upsilon}\coh W
, i.e.
\Psi_{\upsilon}^{-1}b
or

\Psi_{\upsilon}^{-1}b[-1]
is a sheaf on
W
. To this end, we establish the
following preliminary lemma.

Lemma 5.26
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a coherent sheaf on a variety, with

\dim\Supp(\mathcal{F})\leq 1
. If
p
is a closed point such that

\Hom(\mathcal{F},{O}_{p})=0
or

\Hom({O}_{p},\mathcal{F})=\Ext^{1}({O}_{p},\mathcal{F})=0
, then
p
lies
outside
\Supp(\mathcal{F})
.

Proof.

The conclusion is immediate from the first hypothesis. On the other hand
if
\Hom({O}_{p},\mathcal{F})=\Ext^{1}({O}_{p},\mathcal{F})=0
, then in
particular we have
\mathcal{H\kern-2.0pto\kern-1.0ptm}({O}_{p},\mathcal{F})=0
where we note that
the homomorphism sheaf has zero dimensional support and is therefore
determined by its module of global sections. Likewise we can deduce
(e.g. from the Grothendieck spectral sequence associated to

\Gamma\circ\mathbf{R}\mathcal{H\kern-2.0pto\kern-1.0ptm}({O}_{p},-)
) that

\Gamma(\mathcal{E\kern-0.5ptx\kern-1.0ptt}^{1}({O}_{p},\mathcal{F}))=\Ext^{1}({O}_{p},\mathcal{F})=0

and hence
\mathcal{E\kern-0.5ptx\kern-1.0ptt}^{1}({O}_{p},\mathcal{F})=0
.

Taking stalks at
p
, we thus have

\Hom({O}_{p},\mathcal{F}_{p})=\Ext^{1}({O}_{p},\mathcal{F}_{p})=0
where

\mathcal{F}_{p}
is a finitely generated module over the local ring at

p
, and
{O}_{p}
is the unique simple module. It follows that

\mathcal{F}_{p}
is either zero (i.e.
p\notin\Supp(\mathcal{F})
)
or
\text{depth}(\mathcal{F}_{p})\geq 2
—but the latter situation is
absurd since the depth of a module is bounded above by the dimension of
its support.
∎

We also require the following lemma, which follows from a more general
observation due to Sota Asai regarding torsion classes generated by bricks.

Lemma 5.27
.

Let
b,T
be as above, and suppose
b^{\prime}
is another brick that lies in
T
. Then
either
b=b^{\prime}
, or
\Hom(b^{\prime},b)=0
.

Proof.

By
[
Asa20
, lemma 1.7]
, if
\Hom(b^{\prime},b)=0
then there
is a surjection
b^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow b
in
H
, and in particular,
b
lies in
the minimal torsion class
T^{\prime}
containing
b^{\prime}
. Thus we must have

T=T^{\prime}
. But the set of torsion classes in
H
generated by bricks (i.e.
completely join irreducible
torsion classes) are naturally in
bijection with the set of bricks in
H

[
BCZ19
, theorem
1.5]
so in fact
b=b^{\prime}
as
required.
∎

By
lemma

5.22
,
K
lives on some birational
model
W=\upsilon X
and in particular, for every closed point
p\in W(\mathbb{C})
we either have
\Psi_{\upsilon}{O}_{p}\in T
or
\Psi_{\upsilon}{O}_{p}\in F
(
lemma

5.24
). By the above lemma, for each

p\in W(\mathbb{C})
we thus have

\Hom(\Psi_{\upsilon}{O}_{p},b)=0\qquad\text{or}\qquad b=\Psi_{\upsilon}{O}_{p}\qquad\text{or}\qquad\Hom(b,\Psi_{\upsilon}{O}_{p})=0

where the first two cases occur when
\Psi_{\upsilon}{O}_{p}\in T
, while the
last is equivalent to
\Psi_{\upsilon}{O}_{p}\in F
.

Proposition 5.28
.

Let
b\in H
be a brick,
H=T\ast F
the torsion pair whose torsion
class
T
is generated by
b
, and
W=\upsilon X
a birational model
such that
K=F\ast T[-1]
lives on
W
. Then one of the
following (mutually exclusive) possibilities is true.

1.

Either
b=\Psi_{\upsilon}{O}_{q}
for some closed point
q\in W(\mathbb{C})
,

2.

or
b=\Psi_{\upsilon}\mathcal{F}[1]
for a pure sheaf

\mathcal{F}\in\coh(W)
with one-dimensional support,

3.

or
b=\Psi_{\upsilon}\mathcal{G}
for a pure sheaf

\mathcal{G}\in\coh(W)
with one-dimensional support.

Proof.

If
b=\Psi_{\upsilon}{O}_{p}
for some
p
then there is nothing to prove,
so we assume that doesn’t happen. Thus from the above discussion, for every
p
we have

\Hom(\Psi_{\upsilon}{O}_{p},b)=0
or
\Hom(b,\Psi_{\upsilon}{O}_{p})=0
.

Considering the torsion pair
H=(\Psi_{\upsilon}\coh W[1]\cap H)\ast(\coh W\cap H)
shows
b
lies in an exact triangle

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline qn:bsheaves}\Psi_{\upsilon}\mathcal{F}[1]\to b\to\Psi_{\upsilon}\mathcal{G}\to\Psi_{\upsilon}\mathcal{F}[2]

(32)

where
\mathcal{F}\in\coh(W)\cap\Psi_{\upsilon}^{-1}H[-1]
and

\mathcal{G}\in\coh(W)\cap\Psi_{\upsilon}^{-1}H
are the cohomology
objects of
\Psi_{\upsilon}^{-1}b
seen as a complex of sheaves on

W
.

For any
p\in W(\mathbb{C})
, note that
{O}_{p}
lies in the heart

\Psi_{\upsilon}^{-1}H
and thus
\Hom({O}_{p},\mathcal{F})=0
.
On the other hand, considering long exact sequences shows that

\Hom(b,\Psi_{\upsilon}{O}_{p})=\Hom(\mathcal{G},{O}_{p})
and

\Ext^{1}({O}_{p},\mathcal{F})\subset\Hom(\Psi_{\upsilon}{O}_{p},b)
.

It
follows that if
\Hom(b,\Psi_{\upsilon}{O}_{p})=0
then
p\notin\Supp(\mathcal{G})
,
while if
\Hom(\Psi_{\upsilon}{O}_{p},b)=0
then
p\notin\Supp(\mathcal{F})
. Thus

\Supp(\mathcal{F})\cap\Supp(\mathcal{G})=\varnothing
, so that the
sequence (
32
) splits. But
b
is a brick (in
particular indecomposable), so one of
\mathcal{F}
or
\mathcal{G}

must vanish.

In the case
b=\Psi_{\upsilon}\mathcal{F}[1]
, the fact that

\mathcal{F}
is pure of one-dimensional support follows from the
observation that
\Hom({O}_{p},\mathcal{F})=0
for all
p
.

On the other hand if
b=\Psi_{\upsilon}\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}
has zero-dimensional support, then the condition
\End(\mathcal{G})=\mathbb{C}
ensures

\mathcal{G}={O}_{q}
for some
q\in W(\mathbb{C})
and we are reduced
to the first case. If
\mathcal{G}
has one-dimensional support and
p
is a point in

\Supp(\mathcal{G})
, then we must have
\Hom({O}_{p},\mathcal{G})=0

so that
\mathcal{G}
is pure.
∎

The proof of
theorem

5.25
is then reduced to an analysis of
each of the cases arising in the proposition above. If
b=\Psi_{\upsilon}{O}_{q}

for a closed point then there is nothing to prove. The other two cases are
addressed below.

Lemma 5.29
.

If
b=\Psi_{\upsilon}\mathcal{F}[1]
for some sheaf

\mathcal{F}\in\coh(W)\cap\Psi_{\upsilon}^{-1}H[-1]
, then the heart
K

is algebraic of the form
\Psi_{\nu}H
for some
\mathfrak{J}
-path
\nu
.
Consequently,
b=\Psi_{\nu}S_{i}[1]
for some
i\in\underline{\Delta}\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J}
.

Proof.

By (the dual statement to)
proposition

5.4
the
torsion-free class
\Psi_{\upsilon}\coh(W)\cap H
is generated by the
semibrick
\{\Psi_{\upsilon}{O}_{p}\;|\;p\in W(\mathbb{C})\}
. The
constraints on
b
guarantee that every
\Psi_{\upsilon}{O}_{p}
lies in

F
, and thus
\Psi_{\upsilon}\coh(W)\cap H\subset F
. Equivalently,
we have the containment of torsion classes

T\;\subseteq\;\Psi_{\upsilon}\coh(W)[1]\cap H\;=\;\mathop{\textnormal{}}{sup}\left\{{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H[1]\cap H\;\middle|\;{{L}\in\Pic W\text{ nef}}\right\}.

The supremum on the right hand side is an infinite union (since
any two elements in the set are dominated by a third as
in (
29
)). It follows that
b
, and
hence also
T
, is contained in the torsion class
{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H[1]\cap H
for some
{L}\in\Pic W
nef. In other words,
K
is
contained in the interval
[{L}\,\check{}\,\otimes H,H]\subset\tilt^{+}(H)
. The result follows.
∎

Lemma 5.30
.

If
b=\Psi_{\upsilon}\mathcal{G}
for some purely one-dimensional sheaf

\mathcal{G}\in\coh(W)\cap\Psi_{\upsilon}^{-1}H
, then
b
is a
simple object in an algebraic heart
\Phi_{\nu}H[-1]
determined by some

\mathfrak{J}
-path
\nu
. Consequently,
b=\Phi_{\nu}S_{i}[-1]
for some
i\in\underline{\Delta}\setminus\nu\mathfrak{J}
.

Proof.

Consider the torsion pair
H=T^{\prime}\ast F^{\prime}
where
F^{\prime}
is the smallest
torsion-free class in
H
that contains
b
. By (the dual statement
to)
proposition

5.4
,
b
is a simple in the tilted
heart
K^{\prime}=F^{\prime}\ast T^{\prime}[-1]
.

Since
\mathcal{G}
is pure of one
dimensional support, we have

\Hom(\Psi_{\nu}{O}_{p},b)=\Hom({O}_{p},\mathcal{G})=0
for all
p\in W(\mathbb{C})
, and hence each
\Psi_{\nu}{O}_{p}
lies in
T^{\prime}
. It
follows that so does the torsion class
\Psi_{\nu}\anticoh(W)[1]\cap H
,
which is generated by the semibrick
\{\Psi_{\upsilon}{O}_{p}\;|\;p\in W(\mathbb{C})\}
. We can then conclude in a similar fashion as the
proof of
lemma

5.29
.
∎
